The benchmarking package should observe and evaluate the system, not silently change production behavior. Policy updates, capability promotion, and baseline promotion remain controlled by the existing governance layer. 
*voice TTS.txt

54.2 — Define the quality dimensions
Create dimension.py:

from enum import Enum


class VoiceQualityDimension(str, Enum):
    NATURALNESS = "naturalness"
    CONVERSATIONALITY = "conversationality"
    EXPRESSIVENESS = "expressiveness"
    EMOTIONAL_APPROPRIATENESS = "emotional_appropriateness"
    IDENTITY_CONSISTENCY = "identity_consistency"
    RESPONSIVENESS = "responsiveness"
    TURN_TAKING = "turn_taking"
    INTERRUPTION_HANDLING = "interruption_handling"
    SOCIAL_APPROPRIATENESS = "social_appropriateness"
    USER_ALIGNMENT = "user_alignment"
    LATENCY = "latency"
    RELIABILITY = "reliability"
    SYNCHRONIZATION = "synchronization"

Don't compress these into a universal "Aariya quality score." A system can sound more natural while becoming less consistent in its voice identity, or respond faster while handling interruptions less effectively.

Keep a separate historical series for each dimension.

54.3 — Define benchmark suites
Create benchmark.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass

from .dimension import VoiceQualityDimension


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class VoiceBenchmark:
    name: str
    dimensions: tuple[VoiceQualityDimension, ...]
    version: str
    description: str

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if not self.name.strip():
            raise ValueError("Benchmark name cannot be empty")

        if not self.version.strip():
            raise ValueError("Benchmark version cannot be empty")

        if not self.dimensions:
            raise ValueError(
                "A benchmark must define at least one dimension"
            )

        if len(set(self.dimensions)) != len(self.dimensions):
            raise ValueError(
                "Benchmark dimensions must be unique"
            )

Define different benchmark suites rather than one oversized test:

CORE_VOICE_BENCHMARK = VoiceBenchmark(
    name="Core Voice",
    version="1",
    description="Core quality and identity evaluation",
    dimensions=(
        VoiceQualityDimension.NATURALNESS,
        VoiceQualityDimension.CONVERSATIONALITY,
        VoiceQualityDimension.IDENTITY_CONSISTENCY,
        VoiceQualityDimension.RESPONSIVENESS,
    ),
)

IDENTITY_BENCHMARK = VoiceBenchmark(
    name="Identity",
    version="1",
    description="Long-term voice identity stability",
    dimensions=(
        VoiceQualityDimension.IDENTITY_CONSISTENCY,
        VoiceQualityDimension.EXPRESSIVENESS,
        VoiceQualityDimension.EMOTIONAL_APPROPRIATENESS,
    ),
)

RELIABILITY_BENCHMARK = VoiceBenchmark(
    name="Reliability",
    version="1",
    description="Operational reliability and recovery quality",
    dimensions=(
        VoiceQualityDimension.RELIABILITY,
        VoiceQualityDimension.INTERRUPTION_HANDLING,
        VoiceQualityDimension.SYNCHRONIZATION,
        VoiceQualityDimension.LATENCY,
    ),
)

These are benchmark definitions, not populated evaluation results. Your actual results must come from the measurement and evaluation components connected to Aariya.

54.4 — Create reproducible evaluation cohorts
Create cohort.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum
from typing import Mapping


class EvaluationSource(str, Enum):
    REAL_SESSIONS = "real_sessions"
    REAL_INCIDENTS = "real_incidents"
    VALIDATION_SCENARIOS = "validation_scenarios"
    HUMAN_REVIEWED = "human_reviewed"
    CONTROLLED_TESTS = "controlled_tests"


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class EvaluationCohort:
    name: str
    source_type: EvaluationSource
    selection_definition: Mapping[str, str]
    definition_version: str

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if not self.name.strip():
            raise ValueError("Cohort name cannot be empty")

        if not self.definition_version.strip():
            raise ValueError("Cohort definition version is required")

        if not self.selection_definition:
            raise ValueError(
                "Cohort selection must be explicit and reproducible"
            )

A cohort is a defined group of evaluation samples. For instance, you could separately examine technical conversations, casual interactions, interruption scenarios, or interactions involving a specific provider configuration.

Cohort definitions matter because changes in the user population or interaction context can make quality appear to improve or decline when the underlying system has not changed. Your roadmap explicitly requires this kind of cohort-drift awareness. 
*voice TTS.txt

54.5 — Capture versioned evaluation samples
Create sample.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime
from typing import Mapping

from .cohort import EvaluationSource


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class EvaluationSample:
    sample_id: str
    source_type: EvaluationSource
    recorded_at: datetime

    policy_version: str
    architecture_version: str
    provider_configuration: str
    calibration_version: str

    capability_versions: Mapping[str, str]
    measurements: Mapping[str, float]

    # References to protected evidence, not raw secrets or private audio.
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...] = ()

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if not self.sample_id.strip():
            raise ValueError("Sample ID cannot be empty")

        if self.recorded_at.tzinfo is None:
            raise ValueError(
                "recorded_at must be timezone-aware"
            )

        required_versions = {

The sample records which system configuration produced the measurements. The provenance lets you compare policy versions, provider changes, calibration changes, and capability upgrades over time.

In production, use the approved privacy controls for linking sessions to evaluation samples. Don't place private conversation content, access tokens, or raw audio inside general-purpose benchmark logs.

54.6 — Establish validated historical baselines
Create baseline.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Mapping


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class BenchmarkBaseline:
    baseline_id: str
    benchmark_name: str
    benchmark_version: str
    cohort_name: str

    measurements: Mapping[str, float]
    environment: Mapping[str, str]
    provenance: Mapping[str, str]

    validated: bool
    validation_run_id: str

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if not self.baseline_id.strip():
            raise ValueError("Baseline ID cannot be empty")

        if not self.validation_run_id.strip():
            raise ValueError(
                "Baseline requires a validation-run reference"
            )

        if not self.validated:
            raise ValueError(
                "Unvalidated results cannot become a baseline"
            )

        if not self.measurements:
            raise ValueError(

Only promote a baseline after its evaluation and governance requirements have passed. Otherwise, a gradually degraded version could become the new reference and make the degradation look normal.

54.7 — Build longitudinal trend analysis
Create trend.py.

This implementation is intentionally descriptive. It estimates the direction of a time series; statistical significance and improvement validation remain separate responsibilities.

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime
from enum import Enum
from math import sqrt
from typing import Sequence


class TrendState(str, Enum):
    IMPROVING = "improving"
    STABLE = "stable"
    DEGRADING = "degrading"
    VOLATILE = "volatile"
    INSUFFICIENT_EVIDENCE = "insufficient_evidence"


class TrendGoal(str, Enum):
    HIGHER_IS_BETTER = "higher_is_better"
    LOWER_IS_BETTER = "lower_is_better"


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class TrendPoint:
    observed_at: datetime
    value: float


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class TrendPolicy:
    minimum_points: int
    minimum_duration_days: float

    # Set from approved metric-specific evaluation policy.
    minimum_effect_per_day: float
    reference_scale: float

How to use this analyzer
For a metric where higher values indicate better performance, configure TrendGoal.HIGHER_IS_BETTER. For latency, configure TrendGoal.LOWER_IS_BETTER.

The reference_scale, minimum effect, observation window, and volatility limit must be appropriate to the metric and validated evaluation policy. The analyzer must not select arbitrary thresholds and then claim Aariya has improved.

This trend output does not establish causation or statistical significance. Those remain separate parts of Phase 54, as specified in your roadmap. 
*voice TTS.txt

54.8 — Add the rest of the evaluation components
Implement these modules against your existing quality, evaluation, telemetry, and governance interfaces.

Module

Required responsibility

measurement.py

Collect measurements from the actual system and established evaluation components.

normalization.py

Convert units using explicit, validated reference scales.

stability.py

Determine whether improvement is consistent or highly variable.

regression.py

Detect degradation over longer periods, not just after one change.

improvement.py

Assess whether improvement is meaningful, reproducible, stable, and within identity constraints.

human_evaluation.py

Support blind comparison of historical and current versions.

aggregation.py

Aggregate results without hiding important cohort differences.

significance.py

Select a suitable analysis for the metric, sample size, distribution, and study design.

uncertainty.py

Record estimates and uncertainty ranges where supported.

report.py

Produce a multidimensional longitudinal report.

dashboard.py

Display trends without changing production automatically.

repository.py

Persist samples, measurements, baselines, results, and provenance.

For human evaluation, compare the historical validated version with the current version in comparable contexts without telling evaluators which one is newer. Ask which version better supports the intended conversational goal, rather than relying exclusively on a generic rating out of ten. 
*voice TTS.txt

54.9 — Build the benchmark controller
Create controller.py. This controller coordinates the components; it is a scaffold until you connect each dependency to a real implementation.

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Protocol, Sequence

from .baseline import BenchmarkBaseline
from .benchmark import VoiceBenchmark
from .cohort import EvaluationCohort
from .sample import EvaluationSample
from .trend import TrendResult


class MeasurementEngine(Protocol):
    async def measure(
        self,
        sample: EvaluationSample,
        dimensions: Sequence,
    ) -> dict:
        ...


class TrendEngine(Protocol):
    async def analyze(self, history) -> dict[str, TrendResult]:
        ...


class StabilityEngine(Protocol):
    async def analyze(self, history) -> dict:
        ...


class RegressionEngine(Protocol):
    async def analyze(self, baseline, history) -> tuple:
        ...



The important integration boundaries are MeasurementEngine, TrendEngine, StabilityEngine, RegressionEngine, ImprovementEngine, SignificanceEngine, and BenchmarkRepository. They must be connected to actual data sources and implemented evaluation logic. The controller must not fabricate a score simply because one of those components is unavailable.

The source roadmap's controller follows this same overall pattern: measure samples, analyze trends and stability, detect regressions and improvements, evaluate significance, and persist the results. 
*voice TTS.txt

54.11 — Connect Phase 54 to the rest of Aariya
The longitudinal evaluation system should share evidence with the existing subsystems:

Phase 29 — Diagnosis: Investigate sustained naturalness, reliability, or identity decline.

Phase 31 — Knowledge: Save validated long-term findings instead of treating every experiment as a success.

Phase 32 — Causal reasoning: Investigate whether a provider, policy, or architecture change contributed to an observed decline.

Phase 33 — Experiments: Prioritize experiments that could resolve an important, uncertain trade-off.

Phase 45 — Identity preservation: Detect identity drift across many releases.

Phase 46 — Capability evolution: Flag capabilities that appear beneficial initially but become unstable over time.

Phase 47 — Architecture optimization: Evaluate architectural decisions under sustained workloads.

Phase 48 — Reliability: Detect gradual deterioration in failure recovery.

Phase 49 — Security and privacy: Track individual security and privacy requirements without reducing them to one arbitrary score.

Phase 50 — Governance: Control whether longitudinal evidence is sufficient to promote a high-impact change.

Phases 51–53: Supply telemetry, controlled scenarios, and regression evidence.

These integrations should preserve evidence provenance and distinguish actual user-interaction data from controlled scenario results. 
*voice TTS.txt

54.13 — Run evaluation at several timescales
Schedule

Purpose

Real time

Operational telemetry and immediate anomaly detection.

Per release

Regression and benchmark evaluation.

Periodic

Longitudinal quality, stability, and trend analysis.

Major change

More extensive human evaluation and review of trade-offs.

Not every evaluation needs to be equally expensive. However, the system must retain enough context to compare genuinely comparable versions and cohorts.

Before declaring improvement, ask whether the change is reproducible, stable, meaningful, within identity constraints, worth its cost, and supported across relevant cohorts. 
*voice TTS.txt

54.14 — Phase 54 acceptance criteria
Phase 54 is ready to advance when you have verified the following using your actual Aariya system:


Every benchmark is versioned and defines explicit quality dimensions.

Evaluation cohorts can be reproduced, with real and controlled evidence distinguishable.

Samples retain the policy, provider, calibration, capability, and architecture provenance needed for comparisons.

Baselines can only be established from validated historical results.

Trend analysis can return improving, stable, degrading, volatile, or insufficient evidence.

Statistical significance and uncertainty are evaluated independently from simple trend direction.

Blind human evaluations can compare historical and current versions.

The dashboard exposes multidimensional trade-offs rather than hiding them in a single score.

Reports and evidence are persisted successfully.

Governance controls baseline promotion and all production changes.
The completion criterion is sustained, attributable evidence of quality evolution—not a high score generated by an unvalidated model.



Phase 55 — Autonomous Voice Experience Modeling
Phase 54 measures whether Aariya's voice quality improves over time. Phase 55 models what it is actually like to interact with Aariya across complete conversations.

The system moves beyond acoustic quality metrics and examines whether responses are understandable, whether timing disrupts conversations, whether users must repeat themselves, and whether Aariya recovers effectively from misunderstandings. It connects voice delivery to observable interaction outcomes without inventing user satisfaction or assuming that longer conversations are necessarily better. *voice TTS.txt

55.3 — Create a coherent interaction snapshot
Create interaction.py:
from __future__ import annotationsfrom dataclasses import dataclassfrom typing import Mappingfrom .event import ExperienceEvent@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class InteractionSnapshot:    session_id: str    turn_id: str    context: Mapping[str, str | None]    events: tuple[ExperienceEvent, ...]    # Measurements from existing runtime/telemetry systems.    voice_metrics: Mapping[str, float | str | None]    # Explicit or system-observed outcomes.    outcome_metrics: Mapping[str, str | float | None]    def __post_init__(self) -> None:        if not self.session_id.strip():            raise ValueError("session_id cannot be empty")        if not self.turn_id.strip():            raise ValueError("turn_id cannot be empty")        for event in self.events:            if event.session_id != self.session_id:                raise ValueError(                    "Interaction contains an event from another session"                )



This snapshot gives the experience system one consistent view of a turn. It combines actual events, voice metrics, context, and outcomes without making the experience module responsible for generating those measurements.
55.4 — Measure interaction effort
Create effort.py:
from __future__ import annotationsfrom dataclasses import dataclassfrom .interaction import InteractionSnapshot@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class InteractionEffort:    repetitions: int    rephrasing: int    corrections: int    clarification_requests: intclass InteractionEffortAnalyzer:    async def analyze(        self,        interaction: InteractionSnapshot,    ) -> InteractionEffort:        counts = self._count_events(interaction)        return InteractionEffort(            repetitions=counts.get("user_repeated_request", 0),            rephrasing=counts.get("user_rephrased_request", 0),            corrections=counts.get("user_corrected_aariya", 0),            clarification_requests=counts.get(                "clarification_requested", 0            ),        )    @staticmethod    def _count_events(        interaction: InteractionSnapshot,    ) -> dict[str, int]:



These counts are observations of interaction effort, not proof that Aariya caused every repetition or clarification. A user may repeat something for reasons unrelated to Aariya's response. Later analysis must investigate possible causes.
55.5 — Evaluate conversational fluency and responsiveness
Create fluency.py:
from dataclasses import dataclassfrom .interaction import InteractionSnapshot@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class ConversationFluency:    aariya_interruptions: int    user_interruptions: int    completed_recoveries: intclass ConversationFluencyAnalyzer:    async def analyze(        self,        interaction: InteractionSnapshot,    ) -> ConversationFluency:        counts: dict[str, int] = {}        for event in interaction.events:            counts[event.event_type] = (                counts.get(event.event_type, 0) + 1            )        return ConversationFluency(            aariya_interruptions=counts.get(                "aariya_interrupted_user", 0            ),            user_interruptions=counts.get(                "user_interrupted", 0            ),            completed_recoveries=counts.get(                "recovery_completed", 0            ),        )



Create responsiveness.py:
from dataclasses import dataclassfrom .interaction import InteractionSnapshot@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class ResponsivenessEvidence:    time_to_first_audio_ms: float | None    turn_completion_latency_ms: float | Noneclass ResponsivenessAnalyzer:    async def analyze(        self,        interaction: InteractionSnapshot,    ) -> ResponsivenessEvidence:        return ResponsivenessEvidence(            time_to_first_audio_ms=self._number_or_none(                interaction.voice_metrics.get(                    "time_to_first_audio_ms"                )            ),            turn_completion_latency_ms=self._number_or_none(                interaction.voice_metrics.get(                    "turn_completion_latency_ms"                )            ),        )    @staticmethod    def _number_or_none(value: object) -> float | None:        if isinstance(value, bool):            return None        if isinstance(value, (float, int)):



The responsiveness analyzer reads latency from the telemetry system instead of fabricating an acceptable response time. Whether a measured latency harmed an interaction is a separate question, requiring evidence such as repeated interruptions, task outcomes, or user feedback.
55.6 — Detect misunderstandings and successful recovery
Create understanding.py:
from dataclasses import dataclassfrom .interaction import InteractionSnapshot@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class UnderstandingEvidence:    user_corrections: int    clarification_requests: int    resolved_clarifications: intclass UnderstandingAnalyzer:    async def analyze(        self,        interaction: InteractionSnapshot,    ) -> UnderstandingEvidence:        counts: dict[str, int] = {}        for event in interaction.events:            counts[event.event_type] = (                counts.get(event.event_type, 0) + 1            )        return UnderstandingEvidence(            user_corrections=counts.get(                "user_corrected_aariya", 0            ),            clarification_requests=counts.get(                "clarification_requested", 0            ),            resolved_clarifications=counts.get(                "clarification_resolved", 0            ),        )



Create recovery.py:
from __future__ import annotationsfrom dataclasses import dataclassfrom .interaction import InteractionSnapshot@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class RecoveryObservation:    failure_event_id: str    recovery_event_id: str | None    recovered: bool    turns_to_recovery: int | Noneclass RecoveryAnalyzer:    async def analyze(        self,        interaction: InteractionSnapshot,    ) -> tuple[RecoveryObservation, ...]:        events = sorted(            interaction.events,            key=lambda event: event.occurred_at,        )        failures = {            "user_corrected_aariya",            "clarification_requested",            "response_rejected",        }        recovery_events = {            "clarification_resolved",            "recovery_completed",        }



The event relationship resolves_event_id must be emitted or linked by your real interaction/recovery system. Without that link, the analyzer must not assume that a later successful turn resolved an earlier problem. Turn-count calculations can be added once your event schema supplies reliable turn ordering.

Aariya should distinguish between an interaction that had friction but recovered successfully and one in which the problem remained unresolved. *voice TTS.txt

55.7 — Measure continuity and emotional alignment
Create continuity.py:
from dataclasses import dataclassfrom .interaction import InteractionSnapshot@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class ContinuityEvidence:    topic_continuity: float | None    state_continuity: float | Noneclass ContinuityAnalyzer:    async def analyze(        self,        interaction: InteractionSnapshot,    ) -> ContinuityEvidence:        return ContinuityEvidence(            topic_continuity=self._read_metric(                interaction.outcome_metrics.get("topic_continuity")            ),            state_continuity=self._read_metric(                interaction.outcome_metrics.get("state_continuity")            ),        )    @staticmethod    def _read_metric(value: object) -> float | None:        if isinstance(value, bool):            return None        if isinstance(value, (int, float)):            return float(value)        return None



These values must come from actual continuity or state-tracking systems. They should remain unknown when the relevant measurement does not exist.

Create alignment.py:
from dataclasses import dataclassfrom .interaction import InteractionSnapshot@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class EmotionalAlignmentEvidence:    user_state: str | None    aariya_delivery: str | None    mismatch_metric: float | Noneclass EmotionalAlignmentAnalyzer:    async def analyze(        self,        interaction: InteractionSnapshot,    ) -> EmotionalAlignmentEvidence:        user_state = interaction.context.get(            "user_emotional_state"        )        aariya_delivery = interaction.context.get(            "aariya_emotional_state"        )        mismatch = interaction.voice_metrics.get(            "emotional_mismatch"        )        return EmotionalAlignmentEvidence(            user_state=user_state,            aariya_delivery=aariya_delivery,            mismatch_metric=(                float(mismatch)                if isinstance(mismatch, (float, int))                and not isinstance(mismatch, bool)                else None



This module records the difference between the available user-state estimate and Aariya's selected delivery. It must not assume that a particular perceived emotion tells Aariya what the user wants or how she should respond. Emotional interpretation remains uncertain unless validated by relevant evidence. *voice TTS.txt

55.8 — Separate satisfaction evidence from assumptions
Create satisfaction.py:
from dataclasses import dataclassfrom .interaction import InteractionSnapshot@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class SatisfactionEvidence:    explicit_feedback: str | None    task_outcome: str | None    accepted_response_count: int    rejected_response_count: intclass SatisfactionEvidenceCollector:    async def collect(        self,        interaction: InteractionSnapshot,    ) -> SatisfactionEvidence:        accepted = sum(            event.event_type == "response_accepted"            for event in interaction.events        )        rejected = sum(            event.event_type == "response_rejected"            for event in interaction.events        )        feedback = interaction.outcome_metrics.get(            "explicit_feedback"        )        task_outcome = interaction.outcome_metrics.get(            "task_outcome"        )



This is an evidence collector, not a satisfaction predictor. A long conversation, a user who continues interacting, or an accepted response does not independently prove that the user is satisfied.

The roadmap explicitly distinguishes observed, inferred, and predicted evidence. Preserve that distinction in the database and reports instead of producing fabricated values such as “user satisfaction: 94%.” *voice TTS.txt

55.9 — Represent outcomes and uncertainty
Create outcome.py:
from dataclasses import dataclassfrom enum import Enumclass OutcomeStatus(str, Enum):    COMPLETED = "completed"    PARTIALLY_COMPLETED = "partially_completed"    CORRECTED = "corrected"    ABANDONED = "abandoned"    INTERRUPTED = "interrupted"    ESCALATED = "escalated"    UNKNOWN = "unknown"@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class InteractionOutcome:    status: OutcomeStatus    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]    source: str



Create uncertainty.py:
from dataclasses import dataclass@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class ExperienceUncertainty:    evidence_count: int    confidence: float | None    limitations: tuple[str, ...]



For example, Aariya can observe that a user repeated a request several times. It may not know whether the cause was speech recognition, an ambiguous answer, an interruption, or the user's own decision to rephrase. The correct result is to preserve that uncertainty until more evidence is available.
55.10 — Model the experience across turns
Create trajectory.py:
from dataclasses import dataclass@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class ExperiencePoint:    turn_index: int    dimensions: dict[str, float | None]@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class ExperienceTrajectory:    points: tuple[ExperiencePoint, ...]    def __post_init__(self) -> None:        indices = [point.turn_index for point in self.points]        if len(indices) != len(set(indices)):            raise ValueError("Turn indices must be unique")        if indices != sorted(indices):            raise ValueError(                "Experience points must be ordered by turn"            )



The trajectory lets you analyze the sequence of an interaction:
Turn 1 → Initial understanding
             ↓
Turn 2 → Confusion detected
             ↓
Turn 3 → Clarification
             ↓
Turn 4 → Recovery
             ↓
Turn 5 → Task outcome


This matters because a conversation with early friction followed by successful recovery is different from a conversation with similar early friction followed by abandonment. A single final score would conceal that distinction. *voice TTS.txt

from __future__ import annotationsfrom dataclasses import dataclassfrom .effort import InteractionEffortAnalyzerfrom .fluency import ConversationFluencyAnalyzerfrom .responsiveness import ResponsivenessAnalyzerfrom .understanding import UnderstandingAnalyzerfrom .recovery import RecoveryAnalyzerfrom .continuity import ContinuityAnalyzerfrom .alignment import EmotionalAlignmentAnalyzerfrom .satisfaction import SatisfactionEvidenceCollectorfrom .interaction import InteractionSnapshot@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)class VoiceExperienceEvaluation:    effort: object    fluency: object    responsiveness: object    understanding: object    recoveries: tuple    continuity: object    emotional_alignment: object    satisfaction_evidence: objectclass VoiceExperienceEvaluator:    def __init__(self) -> None:        self._effort = InteractionEffortAnalyzer()        self._fluency = ConversationFluencyAnalyzer()        self._responsiveness = ResponsivenessAnalyzer()        self._understanding = UnderstandingAnalyzer()        self._recovery = RecoveryAnalyzer()        self._continuity = ContinuityAnalyzer()

55.14 — Connect Phase 55 to the other Aariya systems

Phase 51 — Observability

Provides the event timeline and operational measurements. Phase 55 interprets their meaning at the interaction level.




Phase 53 — Regression

Turns validated experience failures into regression contracts so that future changes do not reintroduce them unnoticed.




Phase 54 — Benchmarking

Tracks interaction effort, friction, recovery, continuity, and task outcomes across releases and relevant cohorts.




Phase 32 — Causal reasoning

Investigates why a pattern occurs instead of assuming that correlated events prove cause and effect.




Phase 45 and Phase 50 — Identity and governance

Keep experience optimization within voice identity constraints and the approved authorization process.



These connections turn genuine interaction failures into evidence for research, experimentation, regression protection, and future policy decisions. *voice TTS.txt

55.15 — Preserve user autonomy
A critical constraint in this phase is that Aariya should optimize for a better interaction, not maximum engagement.

Its improvement objectives should focus on clarity, appropriate responsiveness, lower unnecessary effort, successful task completion, natural recovery, identity consistency, and user autonomy.

Do not introduce logic that tries to make a user dependent on Aariya or prevents them from ending a conversation. Aariya should let the interaction end naturally when the user chooses to stop. *voice TTS.txt

55.16 — Acceptance criteria
Before treating Phase 55 as implemented, validate these conditions against your real runtime:
- [ ] Runtime events are captured with valid session, turn, and timestamp information.
- [ ] Interaction effort, interruption counts, response latency, and correction events are computed from genuine evidence.
- [ ] Recovery events are associated with the actual failures they resolve.
- [ ] Missing evidence is represented as unknown rather than a fabricated score.
- [ ] Emotional alignment does not automatically determine the user's intent.
- [ ] Complete interaction trajectories can be reconstructed across turns.
- [ ] Causal claims remain distinct from correlations.
- [ ] Reports and evidence are persisted with appropriate provenance and privacy controls.
- [ ] Validated experience failures can feed Phases 32, 53, and 54.
- [ ] Experience optimization cannot bypass identity constraints or governance.
- [ ] The system does not manipulate the user to prolong conversations.
Phase 55 is complete when Aariya can evaluate real interaction experiences, explain which observations support its conclusions, retain uncertainty, and route validated problems into the existing improvement lifecycle. Creating the package alone does not establish that these capabilities are operational.


Phase 56 — Autonomous Conversational Preference & Individualization Modeling
Phase 55 taught Aariya to evaluate the experience of interacting with her. Phase 56 teaches Aariya to adapt to an individual user's communication preferences without losing her own identity.

The distinction is fundamental:

Aariya's identity

Stable characteristics that adaptation must preserve

User preferences

Evidence-backed, contextual, and revisable

Current performance decision

Adapt through approved voice and conversation policies

For example, a user asking Aariya to speak more slowly right now should result in a contextual adjustment. It should not automatically create a permanent preference for slow speech. Repeated observations may justify a preference candidate, but that candidate needs evidence, context, confidence, and validation before becoming durable. 
*voice TTS.txt

56.1 — Create the package
Add this directory:

backend/app/voice/preferences/
├── __init__.py
├── observation.py
├── signal.py
├── preference.py

├── decay.py
├── validation.py
├── repository.py
├── controller.py
└── report.py
The end-to-end flow should be:

Real user interaction

Record preference evidence

Build a candidate

Check context and contradictions

Evaluate confidence and stability

Update profile if justified

Translate preference through voice policy

Enforce identity and privacy constraints

Observe the actual outcome

Actual outcomes feed future preference validation.

56.2 — Represent preferences explicitly
Start with preferences relevant to the interaction—not assumptions about who the user is.

Examples include concise or detailed responses, slower or faster delivery, fewer interruptions, direct correction, structured explanations, and pauses before complex explanations. These can differ depending on the task or conversation context. 
*voice TTS.txt

Create observation.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from datetime import datetime
from typing import Mapping


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class PreferenceObservation:
    observation_id: str
    session_id: str
    turn_id: str | None
    observed_at: datetime

    # Examples:
    # "user_requested_slower_speech"
    # "user_requested_more_detail"
    # "user_requested_no_interruptions"
    signal_type: str

    # Store a normalized communication preference, not a raw
    # transcript or unrelated personal information.
    value: str

    # Example: {"task_type": "technical_explanation"}
    context: Mapping[str, str] = field(default_factory=dict)

    # Reference to protected, genuine source evidence.
    evidence_ref: str = ""

    # Explicit requests apply to the current interaction immediately.
    # They do not imply that a durable preference has been established.
    explicit_request: bool = False

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        for name, value in (

Connect this to your actual runtime and interaction events. The observation must point to genuine evidence; it must not be invented from a guessed emotion or an assumed behavioral pattern.

56.3 — Model evidence, candidates, and durable preferences
Create evidence.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum
from typing import Mapping


class EvidenceRelation(str, Enum):
    SUPPORTS = "supports"
    CONTRADICTS = "contradicts"
    NEUTRAL = "neutral"
    UNKNOWN = "unknown"


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class PreferenceEvidence:
    observation_id: str
    relation: EvidenceRelation
    source: str
    strength: float | None = None
    metadata: Mapping[str, str] | None = None

Do not assign arbitrary universal weights to evidence. Evidence strength should come from an appropriately calibrated evaluation method. An explicit request is useful evidence of a preference for the current interaction, but repeated or contradictory observations still matter when deciding whether the preference is durable.

Create candidate.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum
from typing import Mapping


class CandidateStatus(str, Enum):
    CANDIDATE = "candidate"
    NEEDS_MORE_EVIDENCE = "needs_more_evidence"
    CONFLICTED = "conflicted"
    VALIDATED = "validated"
    REJECTED = "rejected"


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class PreferenceCandidate:
    candidate_id: str
    key: str
    value: str

    # Preference applies only in this context.
    context: Mapping[str, str]

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    confidence: float | None
    status: CandidateStatus = CandidateStatus.CANDIDATE

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if not self.candidate_id.strip():
            raise ValueError("candidate_id cannot be empty")

        if not self.key.strip() or not self.value.strip():
            raise ValueError("Preference key and value are required")

        if not self.evidence_ids:

Create preference.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime
from typing import Mapping


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class Preference:
    preference_id: str
    key: str
    value: str
    context: Mapping[str, str]

    confidence: float | None
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    first_observed: datetime
    last_observed: datetime
    expiration: datetime | None

    profile_version: int

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if not self.evidence_ids:
            raise ValueError("Durable preference requires evidence")

        if self.first_observed.tzinfo is None:
            raise ValueError("first_observed must be timezone-aware")

        if self.last_observed.tzinfo is None:
            raise ValueError("last_observed must be timezone-aware")

        if self.last_observed < self.first_observed:
            raise ValueError("Invalid preference observation interval")


A candidate and a durable preference are deliberately different data types. That separation helps prevent Aariya from converting a single observation into a permanent behavioral rule.

56.4 — Handle context and contradictions
Create context.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class PreferenceContext:
    task_type: str | None = None
    conversation_type: str | None = None
    urgency: str | None = None
    delivery_context: str | None = None

    def to_mapping(self) -> dict[str, str]:
        values = {
            "task_type": self.task_type,
            "conversation_type": self.conversation_type,
            "urgency": self.urgency,
            "delivery_context": self.delivery_context,
        }

        return {
            key: value
            for key, value in values.items()
            if value is not None
        }

Create conflict.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass

from .candidate import PreferenceCandidate


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class PreferenceConflict:
    candidate_a: str
    candidate_b: str
    key: str
    context: tuple[tuple[str, str], ...]
    evidence_a: tuple[str, ...]
    evidence_b: tuple[str, ...]


class PreferenceConflictDetector:

    def detect(
        self,
        candidates: tuple[PreferenceCandidate, ...],
    ) -> tuple[PreferenceConflict, ...]:

        conflicts = []

        for index, left in enumerate(candidates):
            for right in candidates[index + 1:]:
                if left.key != right.key:
                    continue

                if left.value == right.value:
                    continue

                # Different explicit contexts may represent legitimate
                # contextual preferences, not a true contradiction.

For example, “concise answers in general conversation” and “detailed explanations for technical topics” need not conflict because their contexts differ. When two candidates conflict in the same context, preserve both sets of evidence until the conflict-resolution system can evaluate them. Don't automatically overwrite the older preference.

Your roadmap explicitly calls for evidence, context, contradictory observations, and preference relationships to be represented rather than treating every new observation as reinforcement. 
*voice TTS.txt

56.5 — Infer candidates without inventing confidence
Create inference.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from collections import defaultdict
from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Mapping, Protocol, Sequence

from .candidate import PreferenceCandidate
from .observation import PreferenceObservation


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class PreferenceClaim:
    key: str
    value: str
    context: Mapping[str, str]


class PreferenceClaimExtractor(Protocol):
    async def extract(
        self,
        observation: PreferenceObservation,
    ) -> PreferenceClaim | None:
        ...


class ConfidenceEstimator(Protocol):
    async def estimate(
        self,
        claim: PreferenceClaim,
        evidence: Sequence[PreferenceObservation],
    ) -> float | None:
        ...


class PreferenceInferenceEngine:


PreferenceClaimExtractor and ConfidenceEstimator are interfaces that still need implementations. Connect them to your actual signal normalization and evidence evaluation systems. If no calibrated confidence estimate exists, return None rather than fabricate a probability.

The example candidate IDs must also be replaced with stable unique IDs from your ID-generation and persistence system before deployment.

56.6 — Validate preferences over time
Create temporal.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime
from enum import Enum


class PreferenceTemporalStatus(str, Enum):
    CURRENT = "current"
    RECENT = "recent"
    REPEATED = "repeated"
    STABLE = "stable"
    DECAYING = "decaying"
    CONTRADICTED = "contradicted"
    UNKNOWN = "unknown"


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class PreferenceTemporalState:
    first_observed: datetime
    last_observed: datetime
    observation_count: int
    stability: float | None
    expiration: datetime | None
    status: PreferenceTemporalStatus

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if self.observation_count < 1:
            raise ValueError("At least one observation is required")

        if self.last_observed < self.first_observed:
            raise ValueError("Invalid temporal interval")

Create decay.py:

from datetime import datetime

from .preference import Preference


class PreferenceDecayPolicy:

    def should_revalidate(
        self,
        preference: Preference,
        now: datetime,
    ) -> bool:
        if now.tzinfo is None:
            raise ValueError("now must be timezone-aware")

        if preference.expiration is None:
            return False

        return now >= preference.expiration

This handles explicit expiration, but it does not decide how long a preference should last. Your roadmap cautions against a universal arbitrary expiry period; revalidation behavior should be calibrated from observed preference stability.

Create validation.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Protocol, Sequence

from .evidence import EvidenceRelation
from .observation import PreferenceObservation
from .preference import Preference


class EvidenceMatcher(Protocol):
    async def classify(
        self,
        preference: Preference,
        observation: PreferenceObservation,
    ) -> EvidenceRelation:
        ...


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class PreferenceValidationResult:
    supporting: tuple[str, ...]
    contradicting: tuple[str, ...]
    unknown: tuple[str, ...]


class PreferenceValidator:

    def __init__(self, matcher: EvidenceMatcher) -> None:
        self._matcher = matcher

    async def validate(
        self,
        preference: Preference,
        observations: Sequence[PreferenceObservation],
    ) -> PreferenceValidationResult:

The matcher must use your actual preference model, evidence, and contextual rules—not superficial keyword matching. A candidate may remain uncertain, become contradicted, or need more observations. This validation result is evidence for the decision system, not automatic authorization to change Aariya's behavior. 
*voice TTS.txt

56.7 — Enforce privacy and identity boundaries
Create privacy.py:

from typing import Protocol

from .observation import PreferenceObservation


class Phase49PrivacyService(Protocol):
    async def is_allowed(
        self,
        signal: PreferenceObservation,
        purpose: str,
    ) -> bool:
        ...


class PreferencePrivacyPolicy:

    def __init__(
        self,
        phase49_policy: Phase49PrivacyService,
    ) -> None:
        self._phase49_policy = phase49_policy

    async def allowed(
        self,
        signal: PreferenceObservation,
        purpose: str,
    ) -> bool:
        if not purpose.strip():
            return False

        return await self._phase49_policy.is_allowed(
            signal,
            purpose,
        )

This delegates privacy decisions to your existing Phase 49 policy rather than introducing a separate policy that could disagree with the main system.

The preference model should focus on how the interaction should work. Do not infer sensitive characteristics—such as religion, political affiliation, sexual orientation, or medical conditions—from correlations in voice or conversational behavior for this personalization purpose. 
*voice TTS.txt

Create identity_gate.py:

from typing import Protocol


class IdentityConstraintService(Protocol):
    async def allows(
        self,
        candidate_behavior: object,
    ) -> bool:
        ...


class PreferenceIdentityGate:

    def __init__(
        self,
        identity_constraints: IdentityConstraintService,
    ) -> None:
        self._identity_constraints = identity_constraints

    async def validate(
        self,
        candidate_behavior: object,
    ) -> bool:
        return await self._identity_constraints.allows(
            candidate_behavior
        )

The identity gate is mandatory. A user's communication preference cannot override the limits that preserve Aariya's voice identity.

56.8 — Adapt through the existing performance policy
Create adaptation.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from typing import Protocol, Sequence

from .preference import Preference
from .identity_gate import PreferenceIdentityGate


class VoicePerformancePolicy(Protocol):
    async def apply_preference(
        self,
        performance_plan: object,
        preference: Preference,
        context: object,
    ) -> object:
        ...


class PreferenceAdapter:

    def __init__(
        self,
        policy: VoicePerformancePolicy,
        identity_gate: PreferenceIdentityGate,
    ) -> None:
        self._policy = policy
        self._identity_gate = identity_gate

    async def adapt(
        self,
        performance_plan: object,
        preferences: Sequence[Preference],
        context: object,
    ) -> object:

        candidate = performance_plan

This design does not directly manipulate raw TTS parameters using arbitrary formulas such as rate *= 0.8. Instead, it routes preferences through the calibrated performance policy, which can coordinate response structure, strategy, prosodic composition, grounding, and TTS.

The proposed plan is accepted only if the identity gate approves it. The voice policy and the identity-constraint service must be connected to your real implementations before this adapter is production-ready. 
*voice TTS.txt

56.10 — Implement the controller
The controller coordinates the stages, but it must not promote every candidate into a durable preference. Use an injected, calibrated decision policy to determine whether a candidate remains provisional, needs more evidence, requires review, or is eligible for promotion.

from __future__ import annotations

from typing import Protocol, Sequence

from .candidate import PreferenceCandidate
from .observation import PreferenceObservation
from .privacy import PreferencePrivacyPolicy
from .repository import PreferenceRepository
from .inference import PreferenceInferenceEngine
from .conflict import PreferenceConflictDetector
from .validation import PreferenceValidator


class PreferenceDecisionPolicy(Protocol):
    async def decide(
        self,
        candidate: PreferenceCandidate,
        supporting_evidence: Sequence[str],
        contradicting_evidence: Sequence[str],
        has_conflict: bool,
    ) -> str:
        ...


class PreferenceController:

    def __init__(
        self,
        inference: PreferenceInferenceEngine,
        validator: PreferenceValidator,
        conflict_detector: PreferenceConflictDetector,
        privacy: PreferencePrivacyPolicy,
        decision_policy: PreferenceDecisionPolicy,
        repository: PreferenceRepository,
    ) -> None:
        self._inference = inference

This is the orchestration scaffold—not the finished learning engine. Before deployment, complete the candidate-to-preference validation path, implement real contradiction and confidence evaluation, add transactional profile versioning, and ensure that only a validated and authorized profile-update operation can promote a durable preference.

Also use a stable ID generator rather than the illustrative IDs from earlier snippets. If a preference candidate is rejected or remains uncertain, preserve that status rather than silently turning it into an active preference. 
*voice TTS.txt

56.11 — Connect preferences to Phase 55's experience feedback
The complete loop should be:

Preference evidence

Context-aware adaptation

Identity gate

Real interaction

Phase 55 experience evaluation

Validate outcome and contradictions

New evidence updates the candidate or preference profile.

For example, suppose evidence suggests that a user prefers more concise answers. Aariya can apply that preference in a relevant context, then observe whether corrections, unnecessary clarification, and interaction effort change while identity remains stable.

If the adaptation makes the experience worse, re-evaluate the preference rather than blindly reinforcing it. If the evidence is ambiguous, keep it provisional and use the experiment system to investigate. Phase 54 can compare the before-and-after outcomes across quality, responsiveness, identity, friction, and task success. 
*voice TTS.txt

56.12 — Validate the phase before enabling durable personalization
Use real interactions and your established controlled validation scenarios to check these conditions:

Test

Expected behavior

User explicitly asks for slower speech now

Apply the request to the current context if the approved policy permits it.

One request is recorded

Do not automatically create a durable preference.

Repeated, context-consistent evidence is observed

Create a candidate for validation.

A later observation contradicts a preference

Lower confidence, re-evaluate, or flag the conflict using the calibrated decision policy.

Concise and detailed preferences occur in different contexts

Preserve both when context explains the difference.

A preference conflicts with Aariya's identity constraints

Reject the resulting voice behavior.

Privacy policy denies an observation

Do not use it for preference learning.

A preference expires under its configured policy

Revalidate before relying on it.

Evidence is insufficient

Preserve uncertainty instead of inventing confidence.

A profile changes

Retain its version and the reason for the change.

Phase 56's completion criterion: Aariya can turn real interaction evidence into contextual, revisable preferences; distinguish temporary requests from durable tendencies; check contradictions and privacy; adapt through the established performance policy; and enforce the identity gate.

Creating these files alone does not mean individualization is operational.

Phase 57 — Autonomous Conversational Memory-to-Voice Reconstruction
Phase 56 gives Aariya evidence-based knowledge of how a user prefers to communicate. Phase 57 adds another capability: using relevant remembered history to inform how Aariya responds in the present moment.

The important distinction is:

Memory: What happened previously?

Current context: What is happening now?

User preference: How does the user generally prefer to communicate?

Current state: What is appropriate for Aariya to express now?

Voice identity: What must remain consistent regardless of context?

A memory should influence the present delivery only when it is relevant, trustworthy, and appropriate. Historical emotion must not automatically become current emotion. 
*voice TTS.txt

For example, Aariya may remember that a user previously struggled with a project. If the user later says the project succeeded, Aariya should use the history to understand the significance of the achievement—not continue behaving as though the user is still struggling.

57.1 — Create the package
Add this directory to your backend:

backend/app/voice/memory_voice/

├── memory_ref.py
├── retrieval.py
├── relevance.py

├── identity_gate.py
├── uncertainty.py
├── evaluator.py
├── repository.py
├── controller.py
└── report.py
The intended architecture is:

Existing Aariya Memory System

Retrieve relevant memory references

Check relevance, confidence, and temporal validity

Apply privacy and safety policy

Create a semantic memory projection

Integrate with current cognitive state

Build a candidate voice-performance plan

Validate voice identity

Prosodic composition → grounding → TTS

The voice subsystem should consume your existing memory system, not create a second independent memory database. It should store references, projection metadata, provenance, conflicts, and evaluation results rather than duplicate complete memories. 
*voice TTS.txt

57.2 — Use memory references instead of copying the memory store
Create memory_ref.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class MemoryReference:
    memory_id: str
    created_at: datetime
    source: str
    confidence: float | None

    # Optional timestamp when the factual content was last verified.
    last_verified_at: datetime | None = None

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if not self.memory_id.strip():
            raise ValueError("memory_id cannot be empty")

        if self.created_at.tzinfo is None:
            raise ValueError("created_at must be timezone-aware")

        if self.last_verified_at is not None:
            if self.last_verified_at.tzinfo is None:
                raise ValueError(
                    "last_verified_at must be timezone-aware"
                )

        if self.confidence is not None:
            if not 0.0 <= self.confidence <= 1.0:
                raise ValueError(
                    "confidence must be between 0 and 1"
                )

The reference identifies a memory in the existing memory system. It does not contain a duplicated full transcript or an instruction to reproduce historical emotion.

57.3 — Retrieve memory through the authoritative memory service
Create retrieval.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from typing import Protocol, Sequence

from .memory_ref import MemoryReference


class ExistingMemorySystem(Protocol):
    async def retrieve_relevant(
        self,
        context: object,
        limit: int,
    ) -> Sequence[MemoryReference]:
        ...


class VoiceMemoryRetriever:

    def __init__(
        self,
        memory_system: ExistingMemorySystem,
        max_results: int,
    ) -> None:
        if max_results < 1:
            raise ValueError("max_results must be positive")

        self._memory_system = memory_system
        self._max_results = max_results

    async def retrieve(
        self,
        context: object,
    ) -> tuple[MemoryReference, ...]:
        memories = await self._memory_system.retrieve_relevant(
            context=context,
            limit=self._max_results,

ExistingMemorySystem is the integration interface. Connect it to your actual memory retrieval implementation—whether that uses your existing structured memory, knowledge graph, or retrieval pipeline. This code does not pretend that the retrieval backend is already wired up.

57.4 — Evaluate relevance and salience
Create relevance.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum


class RelevanceStatus(str, Enum):
    RELEVANT = "relevant"
    POSSIBLY_RELEVANT = "possibly_relevant"
    IRRELEVANT = "irrelevant"
    UNKNOWN = "unknown"


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class MemoryRelevance:
    memory_id: str
    status: RelevanceStatus
    confidence: float | None
    reason: str | None

    @property
    def may_influence_voice(self) -> bool:
        return self.status == RelevanceStatus.RELEVANT

Create salience.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class MemorySalience:
    memory_id: str
    contextual_relevance: float | None
    recency: float | None
    recurrence: float | None
    user_relevance: float | None

Do not assume the most recent memory is the most important one. An older, recurring project may be relevant to the current conversation, while a recent but unrelated memory may not be.

The implementation needs an evidence-backed relevance evaluator that returns these results. Until that evaluator is available, unknown relevance should not force a memory into Aariya's voice decision. 
*voice TTS.txt

57.5 — Track time and historical emotional context separately
Create temporal.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class TemporalMemoryContext:
    memory_time: datetime
    current_time: datetime
    temporal_distance_seconds: float

The time difference is useful context, but time alone must never determine the emotional delivery. The system also needs to know whether the remembered information remains accurate and whether the current conversation changes its meaning.

Create emotional_context.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class HistoricalEmotionalContext:
    memory_id: str
    historical_valence: float | None
    historical_arousal: float | None
    confidence: float | None

These fields describe historical context, not Aariya's present emotional state. They should come from the memory system's actual recorded information, with confidence retained when available.

For the current interaction, keep the state engine authoritative:

Historical emotional context
              +
Current user context
              +
Current Aariya cognitive state
              ↓
     Current state transition
              ↓
     Voice strategy decision
Create state_transition.py:

from typing import Protocol


class CognitiveStateEngine(Protocol):
    async def transition_from_context(
        self,
        current_state: object,
        memory_projection: object,
    ) -> object:
        ...


class MemoryStateIntegrator:

    def __init__(
        self,
        state_engine: CognitiveStateEngine,
    ) -> None:
        self._state_engine = state_engine

    async def integrate(
        self,
        current_state: object,
        projection: object,
    ) -> object:
        return await self._state_engine.transition_from_context(
            current_state=current_state,
            memory_projection=projection,
        )

This preserves your existing cognitive-state architecture instead of letting the voice-memory module independently set Aariya's emotions.

57.6 — Convert relevant memories into a semantic projection
Aariya should not take a retrieved memory and copy it straight into TTS. It should convert the memory into a small, structured representation of what is relevant to the current conversation.

For example:

Historical information: a project previously had an architecture problem.

Current topic: the user is discussing the same project.

New evidence: the user says the problem has been solved.

Useful projection: topic continuity and recognition of the changed outcome.

Create topic_context.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class TopicMemoryContext:
    current_topic: str | None
    related_memory_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    continuity_confidence: float | None

Create relationship_context.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class RelationshipMemoryContext:
    memory_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    continuity_evidence: tuple[str, ...]
    confidence: float | None

The relationship context represents evidence of ongoing conversational continuity. It must not invent intimacy merely because many conversations have occurred. 
*voice TTS.txt

Create memory_projection.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class MemoryProjection:
    topic_continuity: float | None
    historical_emotional_relevance: float | None
    conversational_continuity: float | None

    salient_memory_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    confidence: float | None

    # Preserve why the projection was produced.
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    limitations: tuple[str, ...]

Create semantic.py:

from typing import Protocol, Sequence

from .memory_ref import MemoryReference
from .memory_projection import MemoryProjection


class SemanticProjectionEngine(Protocol):
    async def project(
        self,
        memories: Sequence[MemoryReference],
        current_context: object,
    ) -> MemoryProjection:
        ...

SemanticProjectionEngine is a boundary for your actual semantic evaluation implementation. It should derive topic and continuity evidence from accessible memory metadata and current context. It must not fabricate a relevance confidence or claim that a memory matches when the evidence is ambiguous.

The projection intentionally contains no direct acoustic instructions such as a fixed pitch or speech-rate adjustment. It describes the context that the existing voice strategy can use.

57.7 — Apply privacy and safety checks before memory influences delivery
Create safety.py:

from typing import Protocol

from .memory_ref import MemoryReference


class MemoryPolicyService(Protocol):
    async def permits_memory_influence(
        self,
        memory: MemoryReference,
        current_context: object,
    ) -> bool:
        ...


class MemoryVoiceSafetyGate:

    def __init__(
        self,
        policy: MemoryPolicyService,
    ) -> None:
        self._policy = policy

    async def allows(
        self,
        memory: MemoryReference,
        context: object,
    ) -> bool:
        return await self._policy.permits_memory_influence(
            memory=memory,
            current_context=context,
        )

Connect MemoryPolicyService to the established privacy, security, and governance policy systems. The voice layer should not create a separate permission policy that bypasses Phase 49.

A retrieved memory should not automatically be spoken aloud or influence delivery just because it exists. It must pass relevance, privacy, safety, and context checks. 
*voice TTS.txt

57.8 — Reconstruct the present meaning of history

from __future__ import annotations

        self._relevance_engine = relevance_engine

    async def reconstruct(
        self,
        current_context: object,
        memories: Sequence[MemoryReference],
    ) -> tuple[MemoryReference, ...]:

        relevant = []

        for memory in memories:
            assessment = await self._relevance_engine.assess(
                memory,

This keeps uncertain matches out of the voice-influence path. A richer implementation may resolve uncertainty through additional retrieval or current-context evidence, but it should never simply treat an uncertain match as fact.

57.9 — Pass a memory-aware context to the voice strategy
Create performance_context.py:

Create voice_influence.py:

This component supplies memory context to the established Voice Director or performance policy. The existing strategy, performance planning, prosodic composition, grounding, and TTS stages continue to determine the resulting voice behavior.

The architecture is therefore:

Memory references
      ↓
Relevant semantic projection
      ↓
Current cognitive state
      ↓
Voice strategy
      ↓
Performance plan
      ↓
Identity gate
      ↓
Prosodic composition
      ↓
Grounding
      ↓

This is the central rule of Phase 57: memory informs the present decision; it does not directly control the voice's acoustic parameters.

57.10 — Enforce identity after creating the performance candidate

The identity gate must check the proposed performance, not merely the memory reference. Even valid, relevant historical context can lead to an inappropriate delivery choice.

If the proposed performance violates Aariya's established identity constraints, reject it and preserve the previously validated performance plan.

57.11 — Integrate the memory pipeline
Create controller.py:

This controller prepares the memory projection, but does not itself change the voice or activate a policy. Your runtime should consume the projection through the current-state and performance-policy interfaces, and then validate the resulting candidate with MemoryIdentityGate.

For stronger production auditing, the repository should also record the decision outcomes for each retrieved memory, including memories excluded by relevance or privacy checks. Keep sensitive memory content out of ordinary logs.

57.12 — Handle stale and conflicting memories
A memory can be accurate about the past and still be wrong for the present.

Examples include an old project architecture that has since changed, or an older communication preference contradicted by current evidence. The memory layer must reconcile historical information with current context and Phase 56's more direct preference representation. 
*voice TTS.txt

The conflict module should delegate to the authoritative memory and preference systems rather than maintain a second, conflicting database.

from typing import Protocol, Sequence

from .memory_ref import MemoryReference


class MemoryConflictPolicy(Protocol):
    async def is_superseded(
        self,
        memory: MemoryReference,
        current_context: object,
    ) -> bool:
        ...

    async def is_temporally_valid(
        self,
        memory: MemoryReference,
        current_context: object,
    ) -> bool:
        ...


class MemoryValidityChecker:

    def __init__(
        self,
        policy: MemoryConflictPolicy,
    ) -> None:
        self._policy = policy

    async def filter_valid(
        self,
        memories: Sequence[MemoryReference],
        context: object,
    ) -> tuple[MemoryReference, ...]:

        valid = []

Insert this validity check before constructing the projection. The policy implementation must use actual update history and evidence; the interface itself does not determine whether a particular memory is stale.

Keep the priority order explicit:

Security, privacy, safety, and governance restrictions.

Current evidence and the present conversational context.

Validated current user preferences.

Relevant historical context.

The uncertainty-aware voice-performance decision, constrained by Aariya's identity.

Historical memories never grant new permissions or override the user's current instructions. 
*voice TTS.txt

57.13 — Evaluate whether memory actually helped
Create evaluator.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class MemoryVoiceEvaluation:
    projection_used: bool
    interaction_outcome: str
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]
    limitations: tuple[str, ...]


class MemoryVoiceEvaluator:

    async def evaluate(
        self,
        projection: object,
        interaction_outcome: str,
        evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...],
        limitations: tuple[str, ...] = (),
    ) -> MemoryVoiceEvaluation:

        return MemoryVoiceEvaluation(
            projection_used=bool(
                getattr(projection, "salient_memory_ids", ())
            ),
            interaction_outcome=interaction_outcome,
            evidence_refs=evidence_refs,
            limitations=limitations,
        )

This is only the initial evaluation data structure. It does not prove that memory improved the conversation. Connect it to Phase 55's actual interaction outcomes and Phase 54's longitudinal benchmark, with experiments or causal analysis where you need to assess whether memory use contributed to an outcome.

A useful report should answer:

Which memory references influenced the projection?

Why were they considered relevant?

What confidence and limitations were recorded?

Which policy permitted their use?

What performance plan resulted, and did it pass identity validation?

What happened in the actual interaction afterward?

That traceability is especially important when an incorrect or stale memory makes an otherwise natural-sounding response factually inappropriate. 
*voice TTS.txt

57.14 — Store only voice-specific records
Add these logical entities to your existing database:

voice_memory_projections
voice_memory_influence_events
voice_memory_conflicts
voice_memory_validation
voice_memory_evaluations
Reference the existing memory IDs instead of storing duplicate full memories. Each projection and influence event should retain provenance sufficient to reconstruct the decision, subject to the established privacy and retention rules.

57.15 — Phase 57 acceptance criteria
Before enabling this pipeline in production, verify the following using the real Aariya runtime:





Memory retrieval uses the existing authoritative memory system.

Relevant memories are distinguishable from irrelevant, uncertain, and stale memories.

Historical emotional context is not treated as current emotional state.

Semantic projections retain source references, provenance, and uncertainty.

Memory influence passes the existing privacy and safety policy.

Current context and validated preferences can override stale historical assumptions.

Memory informs the performance strategy indirectly rather than manipulating raw TTS parameters.

The proposed performance passes the existing identity gate.

Actual memory influence and its resulting interaction are traceable.

The system can evaluate outcomes without automatically claiming causation.
Phase 57 is complete when Aariya can use relevant history to create more contextually continuous interactions, while preserving present-state accuracy, user preferences, privacy, and voice identity.

The code above provides integration scaffolding; the actual memory retrieval, relevance evaluation, policy services, semantic projection, identity guard, and repositories still need to be wired to your implementations and tested.


Phase 58 — Autonomous Conversational Anticipation & Intent Forecasting
Phase 57 gave Aariya memory-aware conversational continuity. Phase 58 lets Aariya anticipate what the user may be about to ask, prepare for likely next steps, and revise those preparations as new information arrives.

The defining principle is:

Prediction is not confirmation.

If someone starts saying, “Can you explain why the system…”, Aariya can prepare relevant technical context without interrupting or pretending to know the end of the question. She should wait for sufficient evidence before committing to a response or action. 
*voice TTS.txt

58.1 — Create the package
Add this directory to your backend:

backend/app/voice/anticipation/
├── __init__.py
├── signal.py
├── partial_turn.py
├── intent.py
├── candidate.py
├── hypothesis.py
├── unfinished.py
├── continuation.py
├── next_action.py
├── tool_need.py
├── clarification.py
├── interruption.py
├── timing.py
├── context.py
├── memory_context.py
├── preference_context.py
├── predictor.py
├── ranking.py
├── uncertainty.py
├── reversibility.py
├── prefetch.py
├── commitment.py
├── cancellation.py
├── correction.py
├── validation.py
├── repository.py
├── controller.py
└── report.py
The architecture should follow this sequence:

Live speech and partial ASR

Current context + rhythm + memory + preferences

Generate multiple intent hypotheses

Evaluate evidence, uncertainty, and risk

Check whether preparation is safe and reversible

Prepare likely resources without speaking

Re-evaluate when new speech arrives

Commit only when the request is sufficiently grounded

58.2 — Model partial speech
Create partial_turn.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class PartialTurn:
    session_id: str
    turn_id: str
    text: str
    sequence: int
    is_final: bool

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if not self.session_id.strip():
            raise ValueError("session_id cannot be empty")

        if not self.turn_id.strip():
            raise ValueError("turn_id cannot be empty")

        if self.sequence < 0:
            raise ValueError("sequence cannot be negative")

The speech-recognition layer should emit successive updates such as:

Sequence 1: "Can you..."
Sequence 2: "Can you explain..."
Sequence 3: "Can you explain why..."
Sequence 4: "Can you explain why the voice..."
Sequence 5: "Can you explain why the voice starts lagging?"
Each update represents the current state of an incomplete or completed turn. Connect this model to your actual streaming ASR implementation; the code above does not create an ASR engine.

The anticipation engine should use partial speech for preparation, but must not treat a partial transcript as a complete request. A pause can mean that the user is thinking, breathing, hesitating, or continuing their sentence. 
*voice TTS.txt

58.3 — Represent intent hypotheses
Create hypothesis.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime
from enum import Enum


class IntentStatus(str, Enum):
    HYPOTHESIS = "hypothesis"
    SUPPORTED = "supported"
    CONFIRMED = "confirmed"
    REJECTED = "rejected"
    EXPIRED = "expired"


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class IntentHypothesis:
    hypothesis_id: str
    intent: str

    # None means the predictor has not supplied a calibrated estimate.
    confidence: float | None

    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]
    generation: int
    status: IntentStatus

    reversible: bool
    expires_at: datetime | None = None

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if not self.hypothesis_id.strip():
            raise ValueError("hypothesis_id cannot be empty")

        if not self.intent.strip():
            raise ValueError("intent cannot be empty")

Create candidate.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class IntentCandidate:
    intent: str
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]
    context_ref: str | None = None

For the phrase “Can you explain why the system…”, the system might keep several hypotheses:

H1: technical explanation
H2: troubleshooting request
H3: request for an example
H4: unfinished thought; user will continue
These hypotheses must remain alternatives. The engine must not immediately select one as fact merely because it ranks first.

58.4 — Build the anticipation context
Create context.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Any

    conversation_context: Any
    user_rhythm: Any
    current_state: Any
