    session_id: str
    turn_id: str

    user_turn_complete: bool

This lets the predictor use more than the user's latest words. It can consider relevant context from Phase 57, communication preferences from Phase 56, rhythm information from Phase 16, and the current cognitive state.

However, historical context and preferences remain supporting evidence, not proof of the current intent. Current instructions and uncertainty take priority over unsupported assumptions. 
*voice TTS.txt

58.5 — Define a replaceable predictor
Create predictor.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from typing import Protocol, Sequence

from .candidate import IntentCandidate
from .context import AnticipationContext
from .hypothesis import IntentHypothesis


class IntentPredictionModel(Protocol):
    async def predict(
        self,
        context: AnticipationContext,
        candidates: Sequence[IntentCandidate],
        generation: int,
    ) -> Sequence[IntentHypothesis]:
        ...


class ConversationalPredictor:

    def __init__(
        self,
        model: IntentPredictionModel,
    ) -> None:
        self._model = model

    async def predict(
        self,
        context: AnticipationContext,
        candidates: Sequence[IntentCandidate],
        generation: int,
    ) -> tuple[IntentHypothesis, ...]:

        predictions = await self._model.predict(
            context,

IntentPredictionModel is an interface for your actual inference component. You can connect it to a model using genuine, labeled interaction data from the existing interaction and prediction systems.

Do not claim that this interface is a trained predictor. Its real implementation must evaluate multiple candidate intents, return evidence references, and provide calibrated confidence when a validated confidence model is available.

58.6 — Rank predictions using risk as well as confidence
Create ranking.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from typing import Protocol, Sequence

from .hypothesis import IntentHypothesis
from .context import AnticipationContext


class PredictionRankingPolicy(Protocol):
    async def rank(
        self,
        predictions: Sequence[IntentHypothesis],
        context: AnticipationContext,
    ) -> Sequence[IntentHypothesis]:
        ...


class AnticipationRanker:

    def __init__(
        self,
        policy: PredictionRankingPolicy,
    ) -> None:
        self._policy = policy

    async def rank(
        self,
        predictions: Sequence[IntentHypothesis],
        context: AnticipationContext,
    ) -> tuple[IntentHypothesis, ...]:
        ranked = await self._policy.rank(
            predictions,
            context,
        )

        return tuple(ranked)

Your ranking policy should consider probability, evidence quality, context fit, cost if wrong, reversibility, and potential latency benefit.

Do not use an arbitrary rule such as confidence > 0.7 to convert predictions into confirmed facts. The ranking policy should prioritize what is useful to prepare for, not decide that the user's unspoken request is already known. 
*voice TTS.txt

58.7 — Implement the reversibility gate
Anticipation is useful when it saves time without committing Aariya to an incorrect action.

Safe preparation can include retrieving potentially relevant memory, preparing response structure, preparing a provisional performance plan, or warming a connection. Sending a message, modifying user data, executing a transaction, or performing an irreversible operation must not happen speculatively.

Create reversibility.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum


class AnticipationActionType(str, Enum):
    PREFETCH_MEMORY = "prefetch_memory"
    PREPARE_REASONING = "prepare_reasoning"
    PREPARE_PERFORMANCE = "prepare_performance"
    WARM_TTS = "warm_tts"
    WARM_CONNECTION = "warm_connection"

    SEND_MESSAGE = "send_message"
    MODIFY_USER_DATA = "modify_user_data"
    EXECUTE_TRANSACTION = "execute_transaction"
    DELETE_DATA = "delete_data"


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class AnticipationAction:
    action_type: AnticipationActionType
    target_ref: str | None
    reversible: bool
    side_effect_free: bool


class ReversibilityGate:

    ALLOWED_PREFETCH_TYPES = frozenset({
        AnticipationActionType.PREFETCH_MEMORY,
        AnticipationActionType.PREPARE_REASONING,
        AnticipationActionType.PREPARE_PERFORMANCE,
        AnticipationActionType.WARM_TTS,
        AnticipationActionType.WARM_CONNECTION,
    })

This is a fail-closed allowlist for speculative preparation, not a substitute for the actual privacy or authorization checks.

For example, memory prefetch must still respect memory permissions and privacy rules. Warming a connection must not send an external message or perform the action the user might be about to request.

58.8 — Prepare resources without speaking
Create prefetch.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from typing import Protocol

from .hypothesis import IntentHypothesis
from .reversibility import AnticipationAction


class PrefetchBackend(Protocol):
    async def prepare(
        self,
        prediction: IntentHypothesis,
        generation: int,
    ) -> tuple[AnticipationAction, ...]:
        ...


class AnticipationPrefetcher:

    def __init__(
        self,
        backend: PrefetchBackend,
    ) -> None:
        self._backend = backend

    async def prepare(
        self,
        prediction: IntentHypothesis,
        generation: int,
    ) -> tuple[AnticipationAction, ...]:

        actions = await self._backend.prepare(
            prediction,
            generation,
        )


The backend should return proposed preparation actions; it should not directly execute arbitrary user-facing actions. Before running a proposed preparation, the controller must pass each action through the ReversibilityGate and the applicable privacy/security policy.

This distinction is essential:

Prepare is not execute. Prefetch is not permission.

58.9 — Cancel stale predictions
Create cancellation.py:

from __future__ import annotations

import asyncio
from collections import defaultdict
from collections.abc import Awaitable
from typing import TypeVar


T = TypeVar("T")


class AnticipationCancellation:

    def __init__(self) -> None:
        self._generation = 0
        self._tasks: dict[int, set[asyncio.Task]] = defaultdict(set)

    @property
    def current_generation(self) -> int:
        return self._generation

    def new_generation(self) -> int:
        self._generation += 1

        for generation, tasks in list(self._tasks.items()):
            if generation < self._generation:
                for task in tuple(tasks):
                    task.cancel()

        return self._generation

    def is_current(self, generation: int) -> bool:
        return generation == self._generation

    async def run(
        self,

When new speech invalidates an earlier prediction, start a new generation. The old preparation tasks are cancelled, and stale results cannot be returned as if they belong to the current turn.

Your Phase 58 roadmap explicitly connects this mechanism to the existing generation guards. Make sure cancellation also reaches your actual ASR, memory retrieval, TTS preparation, and performance-planning tasks where applicable. 
*voice TTS.txt

58.10 — Commit only when the request is grounded
Preparation must not cause Aariya to start speaking early. A separate commitment policy should decide whether the current request has sufficient evidence.

Create commitment.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from typing import Protocol

from .hypothesis import IntentHypothesis
from .context import AnticipationContext


class GroundingAuthority(Protocol):
    async def is_confirmed(
        self,
        prediction: IntentHypothesis,

    ) -> bool:
        ...


class AnticipationCommitter:

    def __init__(
        self,
        grounding: GroundingAuthority,
    ) -> None:
        self._grounding = grounding

    async def commit(
        self,
        prediction: IntentHypothesis,
        context: AnticipationContext,
        current_generation: int,
    ) -> bool:

        if prediction.generation != current_generation:
            return False

        if not context.user_turn_complete:

GroundingAuthority needs a real implementation. It should verify the user's completed request and preserve ambiguity when different interpretations are still plausible. Phase 59 supplies the dedicated conversational grounding and belief-state layer; until that integration exists, do not authorize a speculative prediction to trigger a committed action.

58.11 — Connect the pipeline
Create controller.py:

        ...


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class AnticipationResult:
    generation: int
    predictions: tuple[IntentHypothesis, ...]
    prepared_actions: tuple[AnticipationAction, ...]
    complete: bool


class AnticipationController:

    def __init__(

Integration note: this controller collects permitted preparation actions; it does not yet execute a concrete memory prefetch, TTS warm-up, or external operation. You must connect each permitted operation to an actual backend that enforces the stated side-effect-free contract and supports cancellation. Keep the speculative path separated from the confirmed execution path.

58.12 — Learn from real prediction errors
Every completed interaction should let Aariya compare its provisional predictions with the actual outcome:

Prediction
    ↓
Actual user intent/action
    ↓
Compare
    ↓
Classify prediction error
    ↓
Store evidence and context
    ↓
Evaluate and calibrate
    ↓
Improve future predictions
Create correction.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class PredictionOutcome:
    prediction_id: str
    predicted_intent: str
    actual_intent: str | None
    prediction_error_type: str
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]
    confirmed: bool

Use distinct error types for wrong intent, wrong timing, incorrect continuation prediction, incorrect tool-need prediction, incorrect interruption prediction, and incorrect clarification prediction.

Store real interaction outcomes as the basis of learning and evaluation. Controlled scenarios can validate the predictor separately, but must never be mislabeled as real user behavior. Confidence estimates must be calibrated using appropriate evaluation evidence; printing 0.90 does not make a prediction 90% reliable. 
*voice TTS.txt

58.13 — Integrate with Aariya's existing phases
Existing phase

Role in anticipation

13 — Anticipatory voice

Prepare likely voice output.

16 — User rhythm

Inform continuation and timing predictions.

17 — Turn-taking

Coordinate when to wait, listen, or respond.

40–41 — Interaction and prediction

Provide real outcome data and predictive evaluation.

42–43 — Meta-control and planning

Select appropriate strategy and next steps.

45 — Identity

Reject performance that violates identity constraints.

48 — Reliability

Support cancellation and recover from failed preparation.

49–50 — Security and governance

Enforce authorization and policy boundaries.

51 — Observability

Trace predictions, preparation, cancellation, and outcomes.

53–55 — Regression, benchmarking, experience

Detect mistakes and measure whether anticipation genuinely improves interaction.

56–57 — Preferences and memory

Supply relevant communication preferences and historical context.

These are integration points, not proof that all connections already exist in your codebase. 
*voice TTS.txt

58.14 — Test the critical behaviors
Before production use, validate the following with the actual runtime:

Scenario

Expected behavior

User is still speaking

Aariya prepares if useful but does not prematurely answer.

User changes topic mid-sentence

Old speculative work is cancelled and discarded.

Several intents remain plausible

Maintain alternatives and their evidence.

A likely tool action is predicted

Prepare only permitted, reversible resources; do not execute the unconfirmed action.

A predicted memory is irrelevant

Do not let it influence the response.

A prediction has no reliable confidence estimate

Preserve uncertainty.

New evidence contradicts a prediction

Revise or invalidate it.

A user pauses

Do not interpret silence alone as proof that the turn is complete.

Preparation fails

Preserve the normal conversational path and report the failure through observability.

Prediction quality declines

Investigate and reduce anticipation where it harms the interaction.

Phase 58 is complete when Aariya can make evidence-backed provisional predictions, prepare safely in advance, cancel stale work, and commit only when the user's intent is sufficiently grounded. The code here provides the main integration scaffold; the inference model, calibrated ranking policy, preparation backends, and grounding authority still need to be connected and tested.


Phase 59 — Autonomous Conversational Grounding & Belief-State Management
Phase 58 allows Aariya to predict what the user might mean next. Phase 59 prevents those predictions from silently becoming facts.

For example, when the user says, “Make it faster,” Aariya might consider three interpretations: increase speaking speed, reduce response-generation latency, or change media playback speed. Until the intended meaning is grounded, it must retain the alternatives and either wait for additional speech or ask a suitable clarification question. 
*voice TTS.txt

The core distinction is:

Observed

What the available evidence actually says

Interpreted

A possible meaning derived from that evidence

Assumed

A provisional proposition that still needs support

Confirmed

A proposition supported by sufficient evidence

Corrected or rejected

An earlier interpretation that must no longer be treated as active

These are distinct evidence states, not automatic sequential stages for every proposition.

59.1 — Create the grounding package
Add this directory:

backend/app/voice/grounding/
├── __init__.py
├── belief.py
├── proposition.py
├── evidence.py
├── observation.py
├── interpretation.py
├── assumption.py
├── confirmation.py
├── contradiction.py
├── correction.py
├── rejection.py
├── uncertainty.py
├── confidence.py
├── context.py
├── reference.py
├── entity.py
├── temporal.py
├── state.py
├── update.py
├── resolver.py
├── consistency.py
├── commitment.py
├── rollback.py
├── memory_bridge.py
├── anticipation_bridge.py
├── preference_bridge.py
├── experience_bridge.py
├── privacy.py
├── safety.py
├── repository.py
├── controller.py
└── report.py
The core architecture is:

User input / tool results / current context
                  ↓
             Observations
                  ↓
           Interpretations
                  ↓
         Provisional beliefs
                  ↓
         Evidence and confidence
                  ↓
      Grounding and consistency checks
                  ↓
      Confirmed / uncertain / rejected
                  ↓
    Reasoning + voice + authorized actions
The package should be the authoritative grounding layer for the current conversation. It should integrate the existing anticipation, memory, preference, and experience systems rather than create independent competing sources of truth. 
*voice TTS.txt

59.2 — Represent propositions explicitly
Create proposition.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Mapping


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class Proposition:
    proposition_id: str
    subject: str
    predicate: str
    value: str

    # Scope prevents unrelated contexts from being treated as conflicts.
    scope: str

    # Optional context such as a project, task, or current turn.
    context: Mapping[str, str]

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        for name, value in (
            ("proposition_id", self.proposition_id),
            ("subject", self.subject),
            ("predicate", self.predicate),
            ("scope", self.scope),
        ):
            if not value.strip():
                raise ValueError(f"{name} cannot be empty")

This gives Aariya a structured way to represent meanings instead of hiding assumptions inside strings.

For example:

proposition = Proposition(
    proposition_id="prop-current-topic",
    subject="current_topic",
    predicate="is",
    value="voice_latency",
    scope="conversation:current",
    context={"turn_id": "turn-reference"},
)

The identifier shown here is illustrative. In production, use your repository's stable ID generator and real conversation/turn identifiers.

59.3 — Keep observations and evidence separate
Create observation.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class ConversationalObservation:
    observation_id: str
    session_id: str
    turn_id: str | None
    observed_at: datetime

    # Reference to protected source content or a structured event.
    content_ref: str
    source: str

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if not self.observation_id.strip():
            raise ValueError("observation_id cannot be empty")

        if not self.session_id.strip():
            raise ValueError("session_id cannot be empty")

        if not self.content_ref.strip():
            raise ValueError("content_ref cannot be empty")

        if self.observed_at.tzinfo is None:
            raise ValueError("observed_at must be timezone-aware")

Create evidence.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class BeliefEvidence:
    evidence_id: str
    source: str
    reference: str
    reliability: float | None

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if not self.evidence_id.strip():
            raise ValueError("evidence_id cannot be empty")

        if not self.reference.strip():
            raise ValueError("reference cannot be empty")

        if self.reliability is not None:
            if not 0.0 <= self.reliability <= 1.0:
                raise ValueError(
                    "reliability must be between 0 and 1"
                )

Observations are the recorded inputs. Evidence is the information used to support or challenge a proposition. Evidence can originate from ASR output, explicit user statements, memories, tool results, corrections, or system observations—but each source should retain its provenance.

Do not automatically treat every source as equally reliable. A prediction, for example, is not equivalent to a confirmed user statement. 
*voice TTS.txt

59.4 — Model interpretations, assumptions, and uncertainty
Create interpretation.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass

from .proposition import Proposition


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class Interpretation:
    proposition: Proposition
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    confidence: float | None

Create assumption.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass

from .proposition import Proposition


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class Assumption:
    assumption_id: str
    proposition: Proposition
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    confidence: float | None
    expires_when: str | None

Create uncertainty.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class BeliefUncertainty:
    reason: str
    alternatives: tuple[str, ...]
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if not self.reason.strip():
            raise ValueError("An uncertainty reason is required")

        if not self.alternatives:
            raise ValueError(
                "Uncertainty should identify viable alternatives"
            )

A confidence value expresses an estimate; it is not proof that a proposition is true. When several interpretations remain plausible, Aariya should retain those alternatives and the evidence supporting them.

For example:

Observation:
"Make it faster."

Interpretations:
A. Increase speaking speed.
B. Reduce response-generation latency.
C. Change media playback speed.

Grounding state:
Uncertain.

Next decision:
Wait for more speech or ask for clarification.
This is the foundation for the belief state and resolver described in the roadmap. 
*voice TTS.txt

59.5 — Define belief status and lifecycle
Create belief.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime
from enum import Enum

from .proposition import Proposition


class BeliefStatus(str, Enum):
    HYPOTHESIS = "hypothesis"
    SUPPORTED = "supported"
    CONFIRMED = "confirmed"
    UNCERTAIN = "uncertain"
    CONTRADICTED = "contradicted"
    REJECTED = "rejected"
    SUPERSEDED = "superseded"
    EXPIRED = "expired"


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class BeliefRecord:
    proposition: Proposition
    status: BeliefStatus
    confidence: float | None

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    conflicting_evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    created_at: datetime
    updated_at: datetime
    source: str

    # Beliefs about the current conversational intent should generally
    # expire with the relevant turn or session.
    expires_at: datetime | None = None

The lifecycle supports provisional and validated beliefs while retaining contradicted, rejected, superseded, and expired states.

A belief can be strong but temporary. For example, the meaning of “it” may be confirmed for the current sentence, while that interpretation should not become a permanent user fact.

Create state.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass, field

from .belief import BeliefRecord


@dataclass
class BeliefState:
    confirmed: dict[str, BeliefRecord] = field(default_factory=dict)
    uncertain: dict[str, BeliefRecord] = field(default_factory=dict)
    assumptions: dict[str, BeliefRecord] = field(default_factory=dict)
    rejected: dict[str, BeliefRecord] = field(default_factory=dict)
    history: list[BeliefRecord] = field(default_factory=list)

This separates the active belief view from the historical record. In a production implementation, the repository should be authoritative; this in-memory model should represent the currently loaded conversation state rather than become a second durable database.

59.6 — Make corrections and rejections first-class events
Create correction.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass

@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class BeliefCorrection:
    old_proposition_id: str
    new_proposition: Proposition
    evidence_id: str

Create rejection.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class BeliefRejection:

Create confirmation.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass

from .proposition import Proposition


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class BeliefConfirmation:
    proposition: Proposition
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    source: str
    explicit_confirmation: bool

An explicit correction such as “No, I meant audio latency” must override Aariya's previous interpretation of the same request. The old interpretation becomes rejected or contradicted for that context, and the corrected interpretation becomes the active candidate. It should not remain active merely because its earlier confidence estimate was higher. 
*voice TTS.txt

59.7 — Resolve competing interpretations
Create resolver.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from typing import Protocol, Sequence

from .belief import BeliefRecord
from .interpretation import Interpretation


class InterpretationPolicy(Protocol):
    async def assess(
        self,
        interpretation: Interpretation,
        context: object,
    ) -> BeliefRecord:
        ...


class BeliefResolver:

    def __init__(
        self,
        policy: InterpretationPolicy,
    ) -> None:
        self._policy = policy

    async def resolve(
        self,
        candidates: Sequence[Interpretation],
        context: object,
    ) -> tuple[BeliefRecord, ...]:

        resolved = []

        for candidate in candidates:
            record = await self._policy.assess(
                candidate,

The injected InterpretationPolicy must evaluate evidence, context, recency, explicit corrections, and relevant policy. It should not simply return True for every candidate.

Create consistency.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Sequence

from .belief import BeliefRecord, BeliefStatus


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class BeliefConflict:
    proposition_a: str
    proposition_b: str
    reason: str


class BeliefConsistencyEngine:

    def find_conflicts(
        self,
        records: Sequence[BeliefRecord],
    ) -> tuple[BeliefConflict, ...]:

        active = [
            record
            for record in records
            if record.status in {
                BeliefStatus.SUPPORTED,
                BeliefStatus.CONFIRMED,
            }
        ]

        conflicts = []

        for index, left in enumerate(active):
            a = left.proposition


This is an initial consistency check, not a universal logical contradiction engine. Different values can legitimately apply at different times or in different contexts. The production resolver should use the relevant semantic and temporal rules to distinguish a true contradiction from a context-dependent statement.

For example, “the user generally prefers detailed answers” and “give me a short answer this time” are not necessarily contradictory. The current instruction can govern the current turn while the general preference remains valid. 
*voice TTS.txt

59.8 — Update the belief state correctly
Create update.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import replace

from .belief import BeliefRecord, BeliefStatus
from .state import BeliefState


class BeliefUpdater:

    async def apply(
        self,
        state: BeliefState,
        records: tuple[BeliefRecord, ...],
    ) -> BeliefState:

        # Copy the active indexes so that a failed update does not
        # partially mutate the caller's state.
        updated = BeliefState(
            confirmed=dict(state.confirmed),
            uncertain=dict(state.uncertain),
            assumptions=dict(state.assumptions),
            rejected=dict(state.rejected),
            history=list(state.history),
        )

        for record in records:
            proposition_id = record.proposition.proposition_id

            # Remove previous active indexes for this proposition.
            for index in (
                updated.confirmed,
                updated.uncertain,
                updated.assumptions,
                updated.rejected,
            ):

This is the initial in-memory update operation. For production, add a transactional repository operation that saves the new records and active state together. That prevents a correction from being recorded without also removing its old interpretation from the active state.

The repository should preserve all belief transitions in history while maintaining only the appropriate active status for each proposition.

59.9 — Connect Phase 58 predictions to belief candidates
Create anticipation_bridge.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Protocol, Sequence


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class GroundingCandidate:
    proposition_id: str
    subject: str
    predicate: str
    value: str
    source: str
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]
    confidence: float | None
    status: str


class PredictionAdapter(Protocol):
    async def to_candidate(
        self,
        prediction: object,
    ) -> GroundingCandidate:
        ...


class AnticipationGroundingBridge:

    def __init__(
        self,
        adapter: PredictionAdapter,
    ) -> None:
        self._adapter = adapter

    async def ingest(
        self,
        predictions: Sequence[object],
    ) -> tuple[GroundingCandidate, ...]:

The adapter must extract the underlying intent and evidence from your actual Phase 58 prediction objects. This bridge ensures that an anticipation result enters the grounding pipeline as a hypothesis—not as a confirmed belief.

This is one of Phase 59's most important integrations:

Phase 58 prediction
        ↓
Grounding hypothesis
        ↓
Evidence and context evaluation
        ↓
Supported / uncertain / confirmed
        ↓
Commitment decision
59.10 — Ground memory, preferences, and experience
The other phases contribute evidence, but none should bypass grounding.

Memory bridge — memory_bridge.py

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Sequence


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class MemoryGroundingEvidence:
    memory_id: str
    evidence_ref: str
    source: str = "memory"


class MemoryGroundingBridge:

    async def project(
        self,
        memories: Sequence[object],
    ) -> tuple[MemoryGroundingEvidence, ...]:

        results = []

        for memory in memories:
            memory_id = getattr(memory, "memory_id", None)

            if not isinstance(memory_id, str) or not memory_id:
                continue

            results.append(
                MemoryGroundingEvidence(
                    memory_id=memory_id,
                    evidence_ref=memory_id,
                )
            )

        return tuple(results)

This makes memory references available as historical evidence. It does not establish that the remembered information remains accurate; the grounding resolver must still check temporal validity and current evidence.

Preference bridge — preference_bridge.py

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class PreferenceGroundingContext:
    preference_id: str
    preference_key: str
    preference_value: str
    context_ref: str
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    validated: bool

The integration layer should load validated preferences from Phase 56. A current explicit instruction can override a durable preference for the relevant turn.

Experience bridge — experience_bridge.py

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class ExperienceGroundingEvidence:
    evidence_id: str
    interpretation_id: str
    outcome: str
    source: str = "experience"

Phase 55 can supply evidence that an earlier interpretation was accepted, corrected, or rejected. Such evidence can improve future grounding, but a past outcome must not become an absolute rule about what the user means now. 
*voice TTS.txt

59.11 — Allow actions only after grounding
Create commitment.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum
from typing import Protocol

from .belief import BeliefRecord, BeliefStatus


class GroundedAction(str, Enum):
    ANSWER = "answer"
    CLARIFY = "clarify"
    PREPARE = "prepare"
    MODIFY_DATA = "modify_data"
    EXECUTE_TOOL = "execute_tool"


class GroundingDecision(str, Enum):
    ALLOW = "allow"
    CLARIFY = "clarify"
    WAIT = "wait"
    BLOCK = "block"


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class CommitmentResult:
    decision: GroundingDecision
    reason: str
    belief_id: str


class ActionCommitmentPolicy(Protocol):
    async def evaluate(
        self,
        belief: BeliefRecord,
        action: GroundedAction,

The decision policy must consider the evidence, uncertainty, current user instructions, and action risk. A low-risk conversational answer and a state-changing tool action should not necessarily require the same level of confirmation.

The grounding gate is not a replacement for authorization. Tool actions must still pass the established security, safety, and governance checks, and the relevant Phase 50 approval requirements. 
*voice TTS.txt

59.12 — Handle explicit corrections and rollback
Extend correction.py with a handler that marks the earlier proposition as rejected and creates a new record for the corrected interpretation.

from dataclasses import replace
from datetime import datetime, timezone

from .belief import BeliefRecord, BeliefStatus
from .proposition import Proposition


class ExplicitCorrectionHandler:

    def apply(
        self,
        old_record: BeliefRecord,
        corrected_proposition: Proposition,
        correction_evidence_id: str,
    ) -> tuple[BeliefRecord, BeliefRecord]:

        now = datetime.now(timezone.utc)

        # The earlier interpretation is no longer active in this context.
        old_record_updated = replace(
            old_record,
            status=BeliefStatus.REJECTED,
            conflicting_evidence_ids=(
                *old_record.conflicting_evidence_ids,
                correction_evidence_id,
            ),
            updated_at=now,
        )

        # This confirms the corrected interpretation of the user's intent
        # in its stated scope; it does not establish unrelated facts.
        corrected_record = BeliefRecord(
            proposition=corrected_proposition,
            status=BeliefStatus.CONFIRMED,
            confidence=None,
            evidence_ids=(correction_evidence_id,),

This handler is intended for explicit user corrections to the interpretation of a current request. It should not be used to mark an unrelated external fact as true merely because the user corrected a different part of the conversation.

For production, the handler should also update the repository transactionally, append a correction event, and invalidate any speculative work tied to the rejected interpretation.

Create rollback.py:

from typing import Protocol


class SpeculativeWorkManager(Protocol):
    async def cancel_for_belief(
        self,
        proposition_id: str,
    ) -> None:
        ...


class BeliefRollbackController:

    def __init__(
        self,
        speculative_work: SpeculativeWorkManager,
    ) -> None:
        self._speculative_work = speculative_work

    async def rollback(
        self,
        proposition_id: str,
    ) -> None:
        await self._speculative_work.cancel_for_belief(
            proposition_id
        )

Connect SpeculativeWorkManager to the Phase 58 generation/cancellation system. When a belief is corrected, stale response preparation, TTS prefetch, tool preparation, and memory selection associated with it must be cancelled or invalidated where applicable.

That is the recovery path specified by Phase 59: new evidence → invalidate belief → roll back speculative state → re-ground → decide again. 
*voice TTS.txt

59.13 — Resolve contradictions before updating the state
Add this method to the BeliefConsistencyEngine defined earlier in consistency.py:

from dataclasses import replace
from datetime import datetime, timezone

from .belief import BeliefRecord, BeliefStatus


def reconcile(
    self,
    records: tuple[BeliefRecord, ...],
) -> tuple[
    tuple[BeliefRecord, ...],
    tuple[BeliefConflict, ...],
]:
    conflicts = self.find_conflicts(records)

    conflicting_ids = {
        proposition_id
        for conflict in conflicts
        for proposition_id in (
            conflict.proposition_a,
            conflict.proposition_b,
        )
    }

    now = datetime.now(timezone.utc)
    reconciled = []

    for record in records:
        proposition_id = record.proposition.proposition_id

        if proposition_id in conflicting_ids:
            record = replace(
                record,
                status=BeliefStatus.UNCERTAIN,
                conflicting_evidence_ids=tuple(
                    dict.fromkeys(

This prevents two conflicting propositions from remaining simultaneously confirmed after a single grounding update. The conflict must be investigated or resolved before a dependent action is committed.

The example uses a generic conflict marker to illustrate the mechanism. In production, record the actual conflicting proposition IDs and evidence IDs instead of the marker so that the reason for uncertainty is auditable.

59.14 — Build the grounding controller
Create controller.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Protocol, Sequence

from .belief import BeliefRecord
from .consistency import BeliefConflict, BeliefConsistencyEngine
from .state import BeliefState
from .update import BeliefUpdater


class InterpretationEngine(Protocol):
    async def interpret(
        self,
        observations: Sequence[object],
        context: object,
    ) -> Sequence[object]:
        ...


class BeliefResolutionEngine(Protocol):
    async def resolve(
        self,
        candidates: Sequence[object],
        context: object,
    ) -> tuple[BeliefRecord, ...]:
        ...


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class GroundingResult:
    state: BeliefState
    conflicts: tuple[BeliefConflict, ...]
    resolved_records: tuple[BeliefRecord, ...]



This controller provides the central grounding flow. The interpreter and resolution engines are interfaces until connected to your actual interpretation model and evidence-calibration logic.

The controller should be called when new observations arrive and when corrections or tool results change the current interpretation. Before using the resulting state to drive a response, pass the chosen belief through the commitment gate.

59.15 — Separate conversation state from long-term memory
Not every belief belongs in long-term memory. The system should keep current conversational interpretations separate from validated durable facts. 
*voice TTS.txt

For example:

Proposition

Appropriate lifetime

“The user means the voice-speaking rate in this sentence.”

Usually current turn or conversation.

“The user currently wants a detailed explanation.”

Current request or task context.

“The user's project currently uses provider B.”

Potential durable memory after validation.

“The user once preferred detailed explanations.”

Historical preference evidence, subject to current context and revalidation.

Use explicit expiry and supersession rules. If new evidence says that a project migrated from provider A to provider B, do not leave both providers active as the current project configuration unless the evidence genuinely supports that state.

The repository and memory integration should determine whether a validated proposition qualifies for durable memory. The grounding layer should not silently make every conversational belief permanent.

59.16 — Add privacy-aware persistence
Create repository.py as the persistence interface:

from typing import Protocol, Sequence

from .belief import BeliefRecord
from .state import BeliefState


class GroundingRepository(Protocol):

    async def load_state(
        self,
        session_id: str,
    ) -> BeliefState:
        ...

    async def apply_update(
        self,
        session_id: str,
        records: Sequence[BeliefRecord],
    ) -> BeliefState:
        ...

    async def record_conflicts(
        self,
        session_id: str,
        conflicts: Sequence[object],
    ) -> None:
        ...

    async def record_correction(
        self,
        session_id: str,
        old_proposition_id: str,
        new_proposition_id: str,
        evidence_id: str,
    ) -> None:
        ...

Implement this on the established persistence architecture, using transactional updates so active beliefs and their history cannot silently diverge.

The logical entities should include:

conversational_observations
belief_propositions
belief_evidence
belief_records
belief_assumptions
belief_confirmations
belief_contradictions
belief_corrections
belief_rejections
belief_uncertainty
belief_history
grounding_decisions
Apply Phase 49's authorization, minimization, retention, encryption, and audit controls. The grounding system must not become an unrestricted behavioral-profiling mechanism.

59.17 — Connect grounding to speech and action
The belief state should govern how committed Aariya becomes in her response.

Grounding result

Appropriate behavior

Intent remains ambiguous

Wait for more information or ask a clarification question.

Interpretation is supported but not confirmed

Take only actions permitted by the grounding and risk policies.

User confirms the intended meaning

Proceed through strategy and the applicable safety checks.

User rejects the interpretation

Invalidate it and re-evaluate the request.

New evidence contradicts the current belief

Reconcile the belief state and cancel dependent speculative work.

Grounding also informs prosodic composition. When the communicative intent is uncertain, the voice director may choose less committed delivery or wait for clarification. When the meaning is well grounded, it can choose a more decisive delivery—still within Aariya's identity constraints.

The same principle applies to TTS: do not produce a fully committed response from an ungrounded interpretation when the cost of being wrong is significant. 
*voice TTS.txt

59.18 — Validate the critical cases
Before production activation, test these scenarios against the actual Aariya runtime.

Test

Expected result

“Make it faster” has several plausible meanings

Keep the interpretations uncertain until enough evidence arrives.

The user clarifies “the speaking rate”

Ground the current request in that meaning.

The user says “No, I meant response latency”

Reject the previous interpretation for the current context and update the belief state.

Memory conflicts with the current statement

Evaluate memory as historical evidence; prioritize the grounded current interpretation.

A current instruction conflicts with a general preference

Apply the current instruction to the relevant turn.

Two active beliefs contradict each other

Flag the conflict and block dependent commitment until resolved.

An assumption expires

Stop treating it as an active assumption.

New evidence invalidates prepared work

Cancel the associated speculative work.

Confidence is not calibrated or evidence is missing

Preserve uncertainty instead of claiming certainty.

A tool action requires authorization

Require the existing authorization and governance checks independently of grounding.

59.19 — Phase 59 completion criteria
Phase 59 is ready to advance when Aariya can:


Represent observations, interpretations, assumptions, confirmations, corrections, and rejections separately.

Maintain an authoritative, versioned belief state for the current conversation.

Ground references to previous turns, entities, projects, and temporal expressions.

Reconcile contradictions without confusing different contexts.

Treat Phase 58 predictions as hypotheses rather than facts.

Use Phase 57 memories as historical evidence, not commands.

Respect Phase 56 preferences while allowing current instructions to take precedence.

Cancel stale speculative work after corrections.

Preserve provenance and uncertainty through persistence.

Enforce privacy, safety, identity, and governance constraints before action.
Phase 59 is complete when Aariya can distinguish what the user said from what she inferred, update her interpretation when corrected, and avoid acting on an unsupported assumption. The code here provides the main architectural scaffold; the actual interpretation model, evidence-calibration policy, resolver, transactional repository, and runtime integrations still need to be connected and tested.


Phase 60 — Autonomous Conversational Self-Monitoring & Metacognitive Control
Phase 59 gives Aariya an explicit model of what she currently believes the user means. Phase 60 adds a system that evaluates how reliable her own understanding and planned response are, then decides what to do about uncertainty.

This is an engineering capability, not a claim of consciousness. Aariya should be able to distinguish what she observed, what she inferred, how well supported that inference is, what could go wrong, and whether she should answer, clarify, wait, revise, hedge, or abstain. 
*voice TTS.txt

├── interruption.py
├── outcome.py
├── learning.py
├── provenance.py
├── policy.py
├── controller.py
├── repository.py
└── report.py
The central control loop is:

Interpret the current request

Inspect belief and response state

Assess uncertainty and likely errors

Evaluate risk and governing constraints

Choose a metacognitive action

Execute or revise the response

Monitor actual results

Update evidence and calibration

This builds on Phase 59's grounding state, Phase 58's provisional predictions, and the existing security, privacy, identity, and governance systems. It must not create a competing belief system or independently override those controls. 
*voice TTS.txt

60.2 — Represent self-assessment explicitly
Create observation.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class SelfAssessment:
    target_id: str
    target_type: str

    # None means a reliable confidence estimate is unavailable.
    confidence: float | None

    uncertainty_id: str | None
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    assessed_at: datetime

    calibrated: bool

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if not self.target_id.strip():
            raise ValueError("target_id cannot be empty")

        if not self.target_type.strip():
            raise ValueError("target_type cannot be empty")

        if self.assessed_at.tzinfo is None:
            raise ValueError("assessed_at must be timezone-aware")

        if self.confidence is not None:
            if not 0.0 <= self.confidence <= 1.0:
                raise ValueError(
                    "confidence must be between 0 and 1"
                )

The target_type can identify what Aariya is evaluating:

user_intent
memory_relevance
preference_relevance
response_correctness
tool_selection
voice_strategy
turn_taking
A critical design rule is that confidence in a belief is not the belief itself. For example, Aariya can believe that the user is referring to speaking speed while separately recording that the interpretation remains ambiguous.

60.3 — Model the proposed response before it reaches TTS
Create response_state.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Any


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class ResponseState:
    response_id: str

    intended_content: Any
    intended_action: Any | None

    grounded_belief_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    uncertainty_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    performance_plan: Any | None

    requires_external_action: bool
    generation_id: int

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if not self.response_id.strip():
            raise ValueError("response_id cannot be empty")

        if self.generation_id < 0:
            raise ValueError("generation_id cannot be negative")

This allows the metacognitive controller to inspect the planned response and its supporting beliefs before speech begins.

The proposed response should be checked against the user's current statement, confirmed beliefs, recent corrections, relevant tool results, and memory evidence. If those disagree, the response should be held and revised rather than sent to TTS in its current form. 
*voice TTS.txt

60.4 — Track different kinds of uncertainty
Create uncertainty.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum


class UncertaintyType(str, Enum):
    SEMANTIC = "semantic"
    CONTEXT = "context"
    MEMORY = "memory"
    USER_INTENT = "user_intent"
    TOOL = "tool"
    FACTUAL = "factual"
    RESPONSE = "response"
    VOICE_PERFORMANCE = "voice_performance"
    TIMING = "timing"


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class UncertaintyAssessment:
    uncertainty_id: str
    uncertainty_type: UncertaintyType

    reason: str
    alternatives: tuple[str, ...]
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    material_to_decision: bool

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if not self.uncertainty_id.strip():
            raise ValueError("uncertainty_id cannot be empty")

        if not self.reason.strip():
            raise ValueError("An uncertainty reason is required")

Do not collapse all uncertainty into one number. User intent can be well understood while factual correctness remains uncertain; similarly, the factual answer can be well supported while the timing of an interruption remains uncertain.

Create confidence.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class ConfidenceAssessment:
    value: float | None
    calibrated: bool
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        if self.value is not None:
            if not 0.0 <= self.value <= 1.0:
                raise ValueError(
                    "Confidence must be between 0 and 1"
                )

        if self.calibrated and self.value is None:
            raise ValueError(
                "Calibrated confidence requires an estimate"
            )

A raw model probability must not automatically be labeled calibrated. Calibration should be evaluated using genuine prediction outcomes and an appropriate measurement method, building on Phase 41. 
*voice TTS.txt

60.5 — Evaluate the risk of being wrong
Confidence alone is insufficient. An uncertain explanation and an uncertain request to change external data have different consequences.

Create risk.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum


class RiskSeverity(str, Enum):
    LOW = "low"
    MODERATE = "moderate"
    HIGH = "high"
    CRITICAL = "critical"
    UNKNOWN = "unknown"


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class DecisionRisk:
    consequence: str
    reversible: bool

    privacy_impact: str | None
    safety_impact: str | None

    severity: RiskSeverity
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

Create error_prediction.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class ErrorHypothesis:
    category: str
    probability: float | None
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    consequence: str

Potential error categories include wrong reference resolution, incorrect user intent, stale memory, poor tool selection, overconfident answers, inappropriate delivery, and premature interruption.

The risk assessor and error predictor should be connected to your existing diagnostic and governance systems. They must not fabricate probabilities or automatically infer that a high confidence estimate means an action is safe.

60.6 — Define explicit metacognitive decisions
Create policy.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum


class MetacognitiveAction(str, Enum):
    ANSWER = "answer"
    CLARIFY = "clarify"
    HEDGE = "hedge"
    WAIT = "wait"
    REVISE = "revise"
    ABSTAIN = "abstain"
    BLOCK = "block"


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class MetacognitiveDecision:
    action: MetacognitiveAction
    reason: str

    confidence: float | None
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    requires_new_evidence: bool

The decision policy should choose among these actions based on the current uncertainty, risk, reversibility, user context, grounding state, and governing constraints—not a single universal confidence threshold.

For example:

Situation

Appropriate decision

A routine answer is well grounded

ANSWER

Two meanings remain materially different

CLARIFY

A low-risk explanation has a supported but uncertain cause

HEDGE

The user is probably still speaking

WAIT

New evidence invalidates the draft

REVISE

The evidence cannot support a reliable answer

ABSTAIN

The proposed operation violates a required control

BLOCK

The metacognitive layer can select a response strategy, but it cannot override security, privacy, safety, governance, or identity constraints. 
*voice TTS.txt

60.7 — Build the pre-speech self-check
Create self_check.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Protocol, Sequence


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class SelfCheckFinding:
    category: str
    passed: bool
    reason: str
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class SelfCheckResult:
    findings: tuple[SelfCheckFinding, ...]

    @property
    def passed(self) -> bool:
        return all(item.passed for item in self.findings)

    @property
    def blockers(self) -> tuple[SelfCheckFinding, ...]:
        return tuple(
            item for item in self.findings
            if not item.passed
        )


class ResponseValidationService(Protocol):
    async def validate_grounding(
        self,
        belief_state: object,
        response_state: object,
        context: object,
    ) -> SelfCheckFinding:
        ...

This replaces a dangerous placeholder pattern such as returning "response_grounded": True unconditionally.

The three validators must use the actual Phase 59 grounding state and the relevant action/security policies. Until those validators are connected, the checks are not production-ready.

Before speech, Aariya should establish:

What does she currently believe the user means?

What is she about to say or do?

Does the proposed response agree with the grounded interpretation?

Is the response relying on an unsupported assumption?

Would clarification or a lower-risk response be preferable?

A failed check should prevent an ordinary ANSWER decision from proceeding unchanged.

60.8 — Detect ambiguity and contradictions
Create ambiguity.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class AmbiguityAssessment:
    material: bool
    alternatives: tuple[str, ...]
    reason: str
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

Create contradiction.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class ContradictionFinding:
    target_id: str
    conflicting_source: str
    reason: str
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

These modules should consume Phase 59's belief state, current user statement, recent corrections, actual tool results, and relevant memory.

If the planned response conflicts with reliable current evidence, the controller must stop the current response path and request a revision. Historical memory alone should not overrule a newer, grounded statement.

60.9 — Make clarification and abstention proper actions
Create clarification.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class ClarificationAssessment:
    ambiguity_is_material: bool
    cost_of_being_wrong_is_significant: bool
    clarification_is_safer_or_cheaper: bool

    @property
    def should_clarify(self) -> bool:
        return (
            self.ambiguity_is_material
            and self.cost_of_being_wrong_is_significant
            and self.clarification_is_safer_or_cheaper
        )

Create abstention.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class AbstentionAssessment:
    insufficient_evidence: bool
    reason: str
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

Abstention is a valid control action, not a system failure. If there is insufficient evidence to answer reliably, Aariya should avoid inventing certainty.

Create hedging.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class CommunicationCertainty:
    level: str
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    explanation: str

This determines how strongly a claim should be presented. The language-generation layer should choose the appropriate wording, rather than this module hardcoding phrases like “maybe” into every uncertain response.

Likewise, uncertainty should not automatically make Aariya sound nervous or hesitant. The voice director should use the metacognitive state to select contextually appropriate delivery, while preserving her identity. 
*voice TTS.txt

60.10 — Monitor responses while Aariya is speaking
Metacognition must remain active after TTS starts. A previously valid response may become invalid when the user interrupts, provides new information, corrects a reference, or rejects an interpretation.

Create during_speech.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum


class RuntimeResponseAction(str, Enum):
    CONTINUE = "continue"
    ADJUST = "adjust"
    STOP = "stop"
    REPLAN = "replan"


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class RuntimeChange:
    generation_id: int
    new_user_input: bool
    belief_invalidated: bool
    user_interrupted: bool
    audio_degraded: bool

The runtime monitor should evaluate these events using actual telemetry and Phase 59 belief updates. When an interruption invalidates a response, route the event through the existing playback-cancellation and generation-guard mechanisms.

For example:

Aariya: "The issue is probably the streaming layer—"

User: "No, I already checked that."

             ↓

Stop playback
      ↓
Cancel stale generation
      ↓
Invalidate the relevant hypothesis
      ↓
Update grounded beliefs
      ↓
Re-evaluate uncertainty and risk
      ↓
Replan
      ↓
Resume only with a valid response
This is more complete than cancelling audio alone: the reasoning behind the response must also be reconsidered. The roadmap explicitly connects this recovery path to Phases 6, 17, 48, 58, and 59. 
*voice TTS.txt

60.11 — Monitor the actual outcome after speech
Create outcome.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class SelfAssessmentOutcome:
    response_id: str
    prediction_correct: bool | None
    correction_received: bool
    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

The None value for prediction_correct is important. Not receiving a correction does not prove that the response was correct.

Create post_speech.py:

from typing import Protocol

from .outcome import SelfAssessmentOutcome


class OutcomeEvidenceCollector(Protocol):
    async def collect(
        self,
        response_id: str,
        interaction_context: object,
    ) -> SelfAssessmentOutcome:
        ...


class PostSpeechMonitor:

    def __init__(
        self,
        collector: OutcomeEvidenceCollector,
    ) -> None:
        self._collector = collector

    async def evaluate(
        self,
        response_id: str,
        interaction_context: object,
    ) -> SelfAssessmentOutcome:
        return await self._collector.collect(
            response_id,
            interaction_context,
        )

Connect the collector to actual interaction events, including user corrections, requests for clarification, interruptions, answer rejection, task completion, and successful continuation.

Feed the outcomes into Phase 55's experience model and Phase 41's prediction-learning pipeline. Keep observations separate from assumptions about what the user felt or intended.

60.12 — Record error hypotheses before the outcome is known
Aariya should evaluate how her response might fail before it is spoken, then compare that assessment with the actual result afterward.

Planned response
       ↓
Potential error hypotheses
       ↓
Risk assessment
       ↓
Metacognitive decision
       ↓
Response or clarification
       ↓
Actual outcome
       ↓
Calibration and learning
Possible error hypotheses include:

The selected reference points to the wrong component.

The user's actual intent differs from the interpretation.

Memory is outdated.

The selected tool is inappropriate.

The answer is overconfident.

The voice delivery is poorly matched to the grounded intent.

Aariya may interrupt prematurely.

Each hypothesis should carry evidence references and a consequence assessment. Predictions about errors should not automatically be treated as actual failures.

60.13 — Learn from real outcomes without rewriting source code
Create learning.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class CalibrationObservation:
    assessment_id: str
    target_type: str

    predicted_confidence: float | None
    actual_outcome: str | None

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    outcome_verified: bool

A learning service can aggregate these records to evaluate confidence calibration, recurring error categories, clarification decisions, abstention behavior, and anticipation thresholds.

Only use verified outcomes for appropriate calibration calculations. Do not treat every unchallenged answer as correct, and do not let the learning system directly rewrite Aariya's source code. Changes to models or policy parameters should follow the existing experiment, canary, regression, benchmarking, and governance processes. 
*voice TTS.txt

60.14 — Build the metacognitive controller
The controller coordinates the checks, risk assessment, error prediction, and decision policy. It should not implement an independent shortcut around the higher-level governance controls.

Create controller.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from typing import Protocol

from .policy import MetacognitiveDecision


class SelfCheckService(Protocol):
    async def check(
        self,
        belief_state: object,
        response_state: object,
        context: object,
    ) -> object:
        ...


class DecisionRiskEngine(Protocol):
    async def assess(
        self,
        response_state: object,
        context: object,
    ) -> object:
        ...


class ErrorPredictionEngine(Protocol):
    async def predict(
        self,
        belief_state: object,
        response_state: object,
        context: object,
    ) -> object:
        ...



The injected decision policy is responsible for the complete hierarchy:

Security and privacy.

Safety.

Governance.

Identity constraints.

Current user instructions.

Grounding.

Metacognitive control.

Preferences and performance optimization.

If a higher-level check fails, the decision policy must not authorize an ordinary response or action that violates that constraint. The actual authorization and governance services remain authoritative. 
*voice TTS.txt

60.15 — Preserve provenance for every self-assessment
Create provenance.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class SelfAssessmentProvenance:
    assessment_id: str

    belief_state_version: str
    response_id: str

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    policy_version: str
    model_version: str | None

    assessed_at: datetime

    decision_reason: str
    outcome_reference: str | None

This allows the diagnostic system to reconstruct what Aariya believed, the evidence supporting that belief, what she predicted, which policy produced the decision, and what actually happened.

Create report.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass

from .policy import MetacognitiveDecision


@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class MetacognitiveReport:
    current_interpretation: str
    confidence: float | None

    uncertainty_summary: tuple[str, ...]
    primary_risks: tuple[str, ...]

    response_grounding: str
    decision: MetacognitiveDecision

    prediction_summary: tuple[str, ...]
    outcome_summary: str | None

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

A report should distinguish a predicted outcome from an observed outcome. It should not label a response correct simply because the user continued the conversation or failed to object.

Add these logical entities to the established persistence architecture:

metacognitive_assessments

metacognitive_decisions
metacognitive_outcomes
