Link the records to the relevant belief state, response, evidence, interaction, model version, and policy version. Apply Phase 49's privacy, access-control, retention, and audit requirements.

60.17 — Connect Phase 60 to the existing phases
Phase

Integration

29 — Diagnosis

Identify recurring self-assessment and confidence errors.

33–34 — Experiments and canary

Test improvements under controlled evaluation before rollout.

41 — Prediction

Calibrate self-assessment using actual outcomes.

42 — Meta-control

Coordinate clarification, revision, waiting, and other response choices.

45 — Identity

Keep delivery consistent with Aariya's protected identity.

48 — Reliability

Handle interruptions, cancellation, and recovery failures.

49–50 — Security and governance

Enforce higher-level safety and authorization requirements.

51 — Observability

Record assessment and decision traces.

53–55 — Regression, benchmarking, and experience

Validate that self-monitoring improves reliability and interaction outcomes.

58–59 — Anticipation and grounding

Distinguish predicted intent, grounded belief, and confidence in that belief.

The objective is to create an operational self-model: known strengths, known weaknesses, calibration quality, recurring error categories, uncertainty patterns, and known system limitations. This is measurable self-monitoring, not proof of subjective awareness. 
*voice TTS.txt

60.18 — Test before enabling autonomous control
Validate these cases against your actual runtime:

Scenario

Expected result

User says “Make it faster”

Recognize material ambiguity and clarify before making the wrong change.

Answer is well grounded and low risk

Permit the answer if the other controls pass.

Cause is uncertain but a qualified explanation is useful

Allow an appropriately qualified response.

User is likely to continue speaking

Wait rather than interrupt prematurely.

New evidence invalidates the response draft

Revise and cancel stale generation or playback as necessary.

High-impact action is uncertain

Block execution until the applicable confirmation and governance requirements are met.

Current evidence contradicts memory

Reconcile the belief state instead of confidently using stale information.

User explicitly corrects an interpretation

Record the correction and update the active belief.

Model confidence is uncalibrated

Do not present it as a trustworthy probability.

User provides no feedback

Do not automatically classify the response as correct.

Self-assessment repeatedly overestimates confidence

Investigate and evaluate calibration improvements.

An assessment or outcome cannot be traced to evidence

Flag the evidence gap rather than claim the conclusion is verified.

Phase 60 completion criteria

Aariya inspects a structured response state before speaking.

It distinguishes uncertainty in intent, facts, memory, timing, and voice performance.

It evaluates the consequences of being wrong, not merely confidence.

It can answer, clarify, hedge, wait, revise, abstain, or block.

New information can invalidate a response while it is being generated or spoken.

Actual outcomes feed self-assessment and calibration.

Provenance is retained for each decision.

Learning updates validated models or policy parameters through the established governance process—not arbitrary source-code self-modification.

Security, privacy, identity, and governance remain higher-order constraints.
Phase 60 is complete when Aariya can assess the reliability of its own interpretations and planned responses, choose an appropriate response to uncertainty, and learn from verified outcomes. The code above establishes the integration structure; the assessment models, calibration method, risk engine, decision policy, repository, and live runtime connections still need implementation and testing.

Phase 61 — Autonomous Conversational Repair & Recovery Intelligence
Phase 60 gave Aariya the ability to monitor her own uncertainty and recognize when a response might be wrong. Phase 61 gives her a structured way to recover when a conversational breakdown occurs.

The key idea in your *voice TTS.txt roadmap is that natural conversation does not require a system that never makes mistakes. It requires one that can detect a failure, understand what went wrong, choose an appropriate recovery, verify the result, and return to the original conversation without making the mistake more disruptive than necessary. 
*voice TTS.txt

1. The architecture
Live conversation

Speech, text, audio, tools and current context

Breakdown detection and classification

Identify the failure type and supporting evidence

Cause, severity and risk analysis

Understand what failed, its impact and reversibility

Recovery policy

Select the least disruptive safe recovery

Stop and listen

Clarify

Correct or restart

Repair silently

Verify → Record outcome → Learn

Retain both the original failure and the recovery outcome

This follows the recovery loop described in Phase 61, including verification, recovery history, and feedback into Aariya's experience and learning systems. 
*voice TTS.txt

2. Create the package structure
Add this package to your backend:

backend/app/voice/repair/
├── __init__.py
├── event.py
├── detector.py
├── classifier.py
├── severity.py
├── cause.py
├── context.py
├── misunderstanding.py
├── reference_failure.py
├── memory_failure.py
├── preference_failure.py
├── timing_failure.py
├── interruption_failure.py
├── recognition_failure.py
├── generation_failure.py
├── tool_failure.py
├── voice_failure.py
├── strategy.py
├── acknowledgment.py
├── correction.py
├── clarification.py
├── restart.py
├── continuation.py
├── apology.py
├── silence.py
├── recovery.py
├── verification.py
├── outcome.py
├── learning.py
├── uncertainty.py
├── policy.py
├── identity_gate.py
├── repository.py
├── controller.py
└── report.py
This is the module layout specified in your roadmap. Each component should have one responsibility rather than combining every type of failure into the TTS controller. 
*voice TTS.txt

The intended dependency direction is:

Voice Runtime / Conversation Manager
                |
                v
       Repair Controller
                |
       +--------+--------+
       |        |        |
     Detect   Diagnose  Context
       |        |        |
       +--------+--------+
                |
           Repair Policy
                |
         Identity / Safety /
          Governance Gates
                |
                v
       Controlled Executor
                |
        +-------+-------+
        |       |       |
      Voice  Grounding  Tools
       TTS     Phase 59  Existing tool layer
                |
                v
         Verification
                |
         Outcome / Learning
Important: the repair package should coordinate existing components, not replace them. Grounding remains responsible for updating beliefs, TTS remains responsible for audio generation, and the existing security, privacy, governance, and identity controls retain authority.

3. Step one — Define repair events
Every detected breakdown needs a record that explains what happened and which evidence supports it.

Create event.py:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime
from typing import Any, Mapping


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class RepairEvent:
    event_id: str
    session_id: str
    turn_id: str | None
    event_type: str
    occurred_at: datetime
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]
    payload: Mapping[str, Any]

Possible event types include:

misunderstanding_detected
user_correction
reference_failure
memory_failure
preference_failure
interruption
recognition_failure
generation_failure
tool_failure
voice_output_failure
recovery_started
recovery_completed
recovery_failed
These are event names, not proof that the failure has already been correctly diagnosed.

For instance, user_correction tells Aariya that new information has arrived. The classifier and grounding system still need to determine what the correction changes.

A production implementation should use validated event schemas, reliable timestamps, turn correlation, and references to actual runtime evidence. Avoid placing unnecessary personal or conversation content in diagnostic payloads.

4. Step two — Detect conversational breakdowns
Create detector.py.

A breakdown occurs when Aariya can no longer reliably continue with the response she planned. The roadmap identifies misunderstanding, stale memory, incorrect personalization, speech-recognition errors, interruptions, tool failures, TTS failures, and audio synchronization problems as examples. 
*voice TTS.txt

An initial detector can consume events from the runtime:

from __future__ import annotations


class BreakdownDetector:

    RELEVANT_EVENTS = {
        "user_corrected_aariya",
        "user_requested_repetition",
        "clarification_requested",
        "tool_failed",
        "tts_failed",
        "audio_sync_failed",
        "user_interrupted",
        "belief_invalidated",
    }

    async def detect(self, interaction) -> list:
        detected = []

        for event in interaction.events:
            if event.event_type in self.RELEVANT_EVENTS:
                detected.append(event)

        return detected

This is an initial scaffold, not a complete detector.

The production version should also consume relevant states from the live runtime, including:

Phase 59 grounding and belief invalidation.

Phase 60 self-monitoring and response invalidation.

Speech-recognition confidence and correction signals.

Tool execution results.

TTS stream and audio-delivery status.

Interruption and turn-taking state.

Do not interpret every user interruption as evidence of an error. Someone may interrupt to add a new thought, change the topic, or correct an actual misunderstanding. The detector identifies a potential event; later stages determine its meaning.

5. Step three — Classify the failure
Create classifier.py.

A useful initial taxonomy is:

Category

Example

Main recovery direction

Misunderstanding

Aariya interpreted the request incorrectly

Correct the interpretation and response

Reference failure

“Change that one” refers to the wrong component

Re-ground the reference

Memory failure

Old information was used as current fact

Prefer current evidence and challenge the old memory

Preference failure

Aariya gives a short answer despite being asked for detail

Follow the current instruction

Timing failure

Aariya starts speaking before the user finishes

Stop, listen and resume at an appropriate time

Recognition failure

ASR misheard the user

Ask a targeted clarification

Generation failure

An answer contains an incorrect assertion

Revise the belief and correct the answer

Tool failure

The requested operation did not succeed

Report the actual outcome and recovery options

Voice failure

TTS or audio streaming fails

Resume or restart from a safe boundary

These categories and recovery directions follow your Phase 61 design. 
*voice TTS.txt

For a first iteration, known event types can map to candidate categories:

from __future__ import annotations


class BreakdownClassifier:

    INITIAL_MAPPING = {
        "user_corrected_aariya": "misunderstanding",
        "user_requested_repetition": "recognition_or_audio",
        "clarification_requested": "semantic_ambiguity",
        "tool_failed": "tool_failure",
        "tts_failed": "voice_failure",
        "user_interrupted": "possible_interruption",
        "belief_invalidated": "belief_or_grounding_failure",
    }

    async def classify(self, event) -> str:
        return self.INITIAL_MAPPING.get(
            event.event_type,
            "unknown",
        )

Keep this mapping as a baseline for the first implementation. The roadmap explicitly calls for later evidence-driven classification; the initial mapping should not become a permanent rule that ignores context.

The classifier should eventually return a richer result containing the candidate category, supporting evidence, confidence where available, and unresolved alternatives.

6. Step four — Determine severity and cause
Create severity.py and cause.py.

These two modules answer different questions.

Severity: How serious are the consequences?

Cause: What subsystem or reasoning process contributed to the failure?

A simple severity model could define:

from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum


class SeverityLevel(str, Enum):
    MINOR = "minor"
    MAJOR = "major"
    CRITICAL = "critical"


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class RepairSeverity:
    level: SeverityLevel
    consequence: str
    reversible: bool

Examples:

Minor: a pronunciation issue that does not change the meaning.

Major: an incorrect interpretation that changes the answer.

Critical: a breakdown associated with an unintended, consequential external action.

The cause model should retain evidence rather than merely storing a guess:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class RepairCause:
    category: str
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]
    confidence: float | None

Potential cause categories include ASR, grounding, memory, preference, planning, tool execution, TTS, streaming, timing and turn-taking.

Do not assume the first suspected cause is correct. An incorrect answer could arise from a recognition error, stale memory, ambiguous references, generation, or a combination of these.

When the evidence is insufficient, preserve that uncertainty instead of reporting an invented root cause. 
*voice TTS.txt

7. Step five — Build the recovery strategy and policy
This is the central decision layer.

Create strategy.py, policy.py, and recovery.py.

Aariya should have a repertoire of recovery strategies, rather than a single generic apology. Your roadmap includes acknowledgment, correction, clarification, restart, continuation, waiting, silent repair, apology and abstention. It also defines a minimal repair principle: use the smallest intervention that safely restores the correct conversational state. 
*voice TTS.txt

An initial set of strategy identifiers could be:

from enum import Enum


class RecoveryStrategy(str, Enum):
    SILENT_REPAIR = "silent_repair"
    STOP_AND_LISTEN = "stop_and_listen"
    CLARIFY = "clarify"
    ACKNOWLEDGE_AND_CORRECT = "acknowledge_and_correct"
    CONTINUE = "continue"
    RESUME_AUDIO = "resume_audio"
    RESTART_AT_SAFE_BOUNDARY = "restart_at_safe_boundary"
    REPORT_TOOL_FAILURE = "report_tool_failure"
    ABSTAIN = "abstain"
    PAUSE_AND_CONFIRM = "pause_and_confirm"
    REASSESS = "reassess"

The following is an initial deterministic policy, not a learned or production-validated decision model:

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class RecoveryPlan:
    strategy: RecoveryStrategy
    reason: str
    requires_confirmation: bool = False
    invalidates_current_response: bool = False


class RepairPolicy:

    async def choose(
        self,
        *,
        category: str,
        severity: SeverityLevel,
        user_interrupted: bool,
        incorrect_content_spoken: bool,
        materially_ambiguous: bool,
        external_action_pending: bool,
        action_reversible: bool,
    ) -> RecoveryPlan:

        # Do not allow uncertain, consequential actions
        # to proceed just because recovery is needed.
        if external_action_pending and (
            severity == SeverityLevel.CRITICAL
            or not action_reversible
        ):
            return RecoveryPlan(
                strategy=RecoveryStrategy.PAUSE_AND_CONFIRM,
                reason="consequential_action_requires_review",

The imports above assume the enum definitions from the preceding snippets are in scope; organize them in your package or a shared internal contracts module.

Critical implementation constraints
The policy must also account for user impact, current context, available evidence, recovery cost, confidence, reversibility, and the original conversational goal.

A few rules matter especially:

Silent repair: If Aariya catches a planning mistake before it is heard, revise internally. Do not announce an error that the user never experienced.

Correction: If incorrect content has already been spoken, correct the underlying interpretation or belief before producing a new answer. An apology without a real correction is not recovery.

Clarification: Ask the smallest useful question about the unresolved distinction.

Interruption: Stop speaking and listen rather than repeatedly apologizing.

External actions: Never represent a failed operation as successful. If an action is consequential or irreversible, apply the relevant governance and confirmation requirements.

Uncertainty: If there is not enough evidence to recover reliably, reassess, clarify or abstain.

These policies are consistent with the source architecture; the exact thresholds and decision quality still need to be developed and measured against real operational evidence. 
*voice TTS.txt

8. Step six — Execute recovery through controlled interfaces
Create controller.py.

The controller should orchestrate detection, classification, cause analysis, risk assessment, strategy selection, execution and verification. Phase 61 explicitly calls for a controlled executor between the recovery decision and actual conversational behavior. 
*voice TTS.txt

A starting controller scaffold:

from __future__ import annotations


class ConversationalRepairController:

    def __init__(
        self,
        detector,
        classifier,
        cause_analyzer,
        severity_engine,
        policy,
        identity_gate,
        executor,
        verifier,
        repository,
    ) -> None:
        self._detector = detector
        self._classifier = classifier
        self._cause = cause_analyzer
        self._severity = severity_engine
        self._policy = policy
        self._identity_gate = identity_gate
        self._executor = executor
        self._verifier = verifier
        self._repository = repository

    async def process(self, interaction) -> list[dict]:
        events = await self._detector.detect(interaction)
        results = []

        for event in events:
            classification = await self._classifier.classify(
                event,
                interaction,
            )

This is an integration scaffold, not a drop-in implementation. The example assumes dependency interfaces that you still need to define. In particular, the identity gate, executor, verification and event correlation need concrete implementations against your existing runtime.

The controller must also handle duplicate events, concurrent interruptions, cancellation, timeouts and execution failures. An event should not trigger two conflicting repairs just because the runtime reports it more than once.

9. Step seven — Connect the repair system to the existing Aariya phases
Phase 61 should be an integration layer over existing capabilities, not a new isolated intelligence system.

Existing phase

Responsibility during repair

Phase 41 — Prediction learning

Retain relevant correction and prediction-outcome evidence

Phase 53 — Regression

Turn important failures into regression scenarios

Phase 54 — Benchmarking

Measure repair quality over time

Phase 55 — Experience modeling

Measure interaction effort, latency, continuity and outcomes

Phase 57 — Memory-to-voice reconstruction

Identify challenged or outdated memories

Phase 58 — Anticipation

Cancel stale speculative responses and replan

Phase 59 — Grounding

Update the belief or interpretation that was wrong

Phase 60 — Metacognition

Detect uncertainty, invalid responses and likely errors

Your roadmap describes these connections as part of the repair loop. 
*voice TTS.txt

The runtime sequence for an interrupted response should be:

User interruption
       ↓
Cancel current speech output
       ↓
Invalidate stale response
       ↓
Update current conversational state
       ↓
Re-ground with new evidence
       ↓
Recalculate uncertainty
       ↓
Replan
       ↓
Resume conversation
For memory errors, challenge the relevant memory and prioritize current evidence. Do not automatically rewrite a durable memory merely because one contextual detail changed.

For preference errors, distinguish a temporary instruction from a stable preference. If someone usually prefers short answers but asks for a complete explanation now, honor the current request without automatically erasing the long-term preference.

For audio failures, track which portion was actually delivered. A restart should use a safe boundary or a verified resume point instead of blindly repeating the entire sentence. 
*voice TTS.txt

10. Step eight — Verify recovery instead of assuming success
Create verification.py and outcome.py.

Aariya must distinguish between these two events:

A failure occurred.

A recovery was attempted and appears to have succeeded.

A successful recovery does not erase the original failure from the event history.

Use an outcome model that supports an unknown result:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class RecoveryOutcome:
    succeeded: bool | None
    turns_to_recovery: int | None
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

The interpretation of succeeded should be:

True: sufficient evidence supports successful recovery.

False: evidence shows the repair failed or the problem persisted.

None: the available evidence cannot establish the outcome.

Not receiving another correction does not prove that the recovery worked. Your roadmap explicitly preserves this unknown state and recommends verifying whether the user corrected Aariya again, whether the conversation continued and whether the original task succeeded. 
*voice TTS.txt

In production, verification should use evidence tied to the specific repair and turn. A correction from an earlier turn should not incorrectly mark a new recovery as failed.

11. Step nine — Learn from actual recovery outcomes
Create learning.py, repository.py and report.py.

The learning loop should be:

Real failure
    ↓
Classification
    ↓
Cause hypothesis
    ↓
Recovery strategy
    ↓
Observed outcome
    ↓
Recovery evaluation
    ↓
Evidence-backed pattern
    ↓
Experiment
    ↓
Canary
    ↓
Regression tests
    ↓
Promotion or rollback
Use real operational evidence, including actual corrections, interruptions, failed tool calls, TTS errors and recovery outcomes. Do not manufacture successful outcomes, assume satisfaction, or use fake user responses as evidence that a strategy works. Synthetic fixtures can still be used to test that the software behaves correctly; they must not be mistaken for real-world learning evidence.

Do not allow the learning module to rewrite Aariya's source code directly. Learning should update bounded policy parameters or approved models through the existing evaluation and release process. 
*voice TTS.txt

Keep at least two independent quality dimensions:

Error frequency: How often does Aariya experience conversational breakdowns?

Recovery quality: How well and efficiently does she recover after those breakdowns?

A system can recover elegantly but still make too many mistakes. Both dimensions need measurement.

12. Step ten — Test Phase 61 against realistic failure cases
Start by verifying these scenarios before connecting learning to production traffic.

Test

Expected behavior

Internal response error detected before speech

Revise silently; do not announce an unexperienced error

User corrects the selected reference

Invalidate the wrong reference, re-ground, and continue

User interrupts Aariya

Stop playback and listen

ASR confidence is insufficient

Ask a concise, targeted clarification

Old memory conflicts with current evidence

Prefer current evidence and record the challenge

User's current request conflicts with a stored preference

Follow the current instruction

Tool action fails

Report the actual failure state; do not claim success

TTS stream is interrupted or corrupted

Resume or restart at a safe boundary

Recovery produces no clear outcome evidence

Store succeeded=None

Repair policy proposes a prohibited action

Existing governance and identity controls prevent execution

Add regression coverage for every important failure pattern. An improvement to the recovery policy should not silently reintroduce a previously fixed failure.

13. Definition of done
Phase 61 is ready to be considered implemented only when these checkpoints have been demonstrated:

Implementation checklist

0 / 12


Repair events include IDs, turn correlation, timestamps and evidence references.

Breakdowns are detected using real runtime events and relevant subsystem state.

Classification, cause and severity are separate, inspectable decisions.

Recovery selection considers user impact, ambiguity, risk and reversibility.

Security, privacy, governance and identity gates cannot be bypassed by repair logic.

Interruption handling promptly stops speech and cancels stale responses.

Corrections update grounding rather than merely changing the wording.

TTS recovery avoids unnecessary duplication of already-delivered speech.

Recovery verification preserves unknown outcomes rather than assuming success.

Failure history is retained even when the repair succeeds.

Real outcome records feed approved evaluation, regression and learning pipelines.

Tests cover failure handling, cancellation, repeated events and failed recovery.


Phase 62 — Autonomous Conversational Initiative & Agency Control
Phase 61 taught Aariya how to recover when a conversation goes wrong. Phase 62 teaches her when to take initiative, what kind of initiative is appropriate, and when doing nothing is the better decision.

The central principle in your *voice TTS.txt roadmap is:

Initiative does not mean constant activity.

Aariya should be able to notice a useful opportunity, evaluate it against the user's current goal, consider timing and risk, and then choose whether to speak, ask, suggest, prepare something silently, or wait. 
*voice TTS.txt

1. The architecture
Live conversation and runtime events

Actual user input, task changes, tool events and authorized background signals

Opportunity detection

Identify something Aariya could usefully do

Relevance and value

Does this support the current goal?

Timing and attention

Is this a good moment?

Agency arbitration and policy

Check intent, privacy, consent, risk and reversibility

Speak or ask

Suggest or warn

Prepare silently

Wait or discard

Observe outcome → Evaluate → Improve

An important distinction: identifying an opportunity does not mean Aariya has permission to act on it. A candidate must pass the relevant policy and authorization checks before execution. 
*voice TTS.txt

3. Step one — Detect real opportunities
Create opportunity.py and trigger.py.

An opportunity is something Aariya might usefully do. It is not yet an approved action.

Your roadmap identifies examples such as a task milestone being reached, a previously requested follow-up becoming due, an unresolved question blocking a task, or a real system failure worth reporting. It explicitly rejects fabricating a trigger merely because the user might need something. 
*voice TTS.txt

Use a structured opportunity model:

# opportunity.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Any


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class InitiativeOpportunity:
    opportunity_id: str
    opportunity_type: str
    context: Any
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]
    confidence: float | None

Example opportunities:

task_blocked
requested_followup_due
task_milestone_reached
unresolved_question
relevant_failure_detected
preparation_opportunity
The trigger detector should consume actual events from the runtime, scheduler, task manager or authorized monitoring service.

For instance, if the user has explicitly asked Aariya to report streaming failures, a real failure event can become an initiative opportunity. Merely assuming that the user might be interested in streaming performance should not create a false report of a detected failure.

4. Step two — Separate opportunities from candidate actions
Create candidate.py.

One opportunity can produce several possible responses.

Suppose the user is debugging voice latency. Aariya could:

Prepare relevant latency metrics silently.

Ask which provider is affected if that parameter blocks the investigation.

Offer a relevant suggestion when the user reaches a natural conversational boundary.

Wait until the user finishes explaining the problem.

The candidate model should preserve this distinction:

# candidate.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class InitiativeCandidate:
    candidate_id: str
    opportunity_id: str
    action_type: str
    expected_value: float | None
    confidence: float | None
    reversible: bool
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

expected_value and confidence may be unknown. Do not invent values just to make prioritization possible.

A candidate is not automatically permitted to execute, even if its expected usefulness appears high. Policy, authorization and timing are assessed later.

5. Step three — Evaluate relevance and actual value
Create relevance.py and value.py.

Relevance asks: Does this opportunity matter to the user's current situation?

Value asks: Would acting on it provide meaningful assistance?

Your roadmap lists useful benefits including reducing user effort, preventing errors, improving task completion, resolving ambiguity, preserving continuity and improving safety. It explicitly rejects prolonging a conversation merely to increase engagement. 
*voice TTS.txt

Consider this example:

Relevant initiative

The user is investigating repeated TTS stream failures. Aariya has actual, relevant telemetry and can prepare the matching incident records.

Irrelevant initiative

The user is explaining that failure, and Aariya interrupts to discuss an unrelated avatar animation improvement.

Implementations should evaluate relevance against the active task, current user instruction, grounded context and the evidence behind the opportunity. Avoid making “proactivity” a goal in itself.

6. Step four — Add timing and attention control
Create timing.py, attention.py and interruption_cost.py.

A relevant observation can still be badly timed. Phase 62 must integrate with your existing turn-taking logic.

The contextual inputs include whether the user is speaking, whether a pause is likely temporary, whether Aariya is expected to respond, current cognitive load, urgency and the cost of interrupting.

For example:

User is explaining an important issue
           ↓
Aariya discovers a useful optimization
           ↓
The optimization is not urgent
           ↓
Prepare or queue it silently
           ↓
Wait for a suitable conversational boundary
Compare that with a user waiting for an answer when relevant information has already been retrieved: responding promptly may be the appropriate action.

The source describes attention as a limited resource and identifies interruption cost as an explicit decision input. 
*voice TTS.txt

A useful starting model is:

# user_state.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class InitiativeUserState:
    speaking: bool
    likely_continuing: bool
    attention_available: float | None
    urgency: float | None
    interaction_mode: str | None

These fields are inputs to a decision, not a definitive reading of the user's internal state. For example, a pause in speech is not sufficient evidence that the user wants Aariya to interrupt.

7. Step five — Use memory and preferences without overriding the present
Create goal.py, memory.py and preference.py.

Phase 62 consumes relevant context from Aariya's earlier systems:

Phase 57 provides relevant historical information, unresolved tasks and requested follow-ups.

Phase 56 provides preferences about reminders, interruptions and communication.

Phase 59 supplies the current grounded beliefs and conversational context.

Phase 55 supplies evidence about previous initiative outcomes.

However, memory is not an independent permission to initiate an action, and a stored preference must not override a contrary current instruction. 
*voice TTS.txt

Example:

Stored preference:
User usually appreciates reminders about active tasks.

Current instruction:
"Don't remind me about this task."

Correct behavior:
Stop the relevant proactive reminders.
For Aariya, the important architecture decision is to keep a distinction between:

Long-term preference
        ↓
Contextual preference signal
        ↓
Current instruction and situation
        ↓
Initiative decision
Do not turn one contextual request into an automatic permanent change to the user's preference profile.

8. Step six — Define initiative decisions
Create initiative.py.

The roadmap defines several possible decisions:

 speak, ask, suggest, prepare, remind, warn, confirm, wait, and discard.

Silence is an intentional outcome, not a failure to make a decision. 
*voice TTS.txt

# initiative.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class InitiativeDecision:
    action: str
    confidence: float | None
    reason: str
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

An initial action policy should consider:

Current user intent
Current turn ownership
Current goal
Relevance and expected value
Evidence and confidence
Timing and interruption cost
Privacy and consent
Safety and governance
Reversibility
User preferences
Aariya should not have a permanent voice_mode = "proactive" setting that automatically makes her talk more. Initiative should be evaluated continuously as context changes. At different moments, the same system can appropriately be quiet, responsive, proactive or cautious. 
*voice TTS.txt

9. Step seven — Control the different initiative types
Create speech.py, question.py, suggestion.py, reminder.py, preparation.py and silence.py.

Each type of action needs different constraints.

Initiative

Conditions to consider

Proactive speech

Strong relevance, useful information, appropriate timing and acceptable interruption cost

Proactive question

A genuine unresolved issue or missing parameter that matters to progress

Suggestion

Evidence-supported, relevant, optional and non-coercive

Reminder

A real task or event, appropriate timing and required authorization

Silent preparation

Relevant, preferably reversible and within available permissions

Warning

A substantiated issue whose consequences justify drawing attention to it

Wait

Speaking now would be unnecessary or disruptive

Discard

The opportunity is stale, irrelevant, already resolved or otherwise unsuitable

The roadmap gives special importance to preparation without speaking. Aariya could retrieve relevant telemetry, prepare a response or organize task context without occupying the user's attention. 
*voice TTS.txt

Risk and authorization are separate gates
Create risk.py, privacy.py, consent.py and reversibility.py.

A useful distinction from the source is:

Prefetching relevant information is generally reversible.

Preparing a draft is generally reversible.

Sending a message externally may be difficult to reverse.

Proactive behavior must not bypass authorization, privacy, secrets management, data-access controls or audit requirements. Recurring monitoring and reminders require the relevant explicit authorization, where applicable. Higher-consequence actions should follow the existing governance and security policies rather than using an independent agency threshold. 
*voice TTS.txt

10. Step eight — Prevent stale initiative from executing
Create commitment.py and cancellation.py.

An initiative can be appropriate when first considered but irrelevant by the time execution begins.

Example:

Aariya prepares to suggest a latency fix
               ↓
User changes the topic
               ↓
Current initiative is invalidated
               ↓
Aariya cancels the candidate
               ↓
New user intent takes priority
Your roadmap proposes a generation counter to invalidate stale work. 
*voice TTS.txt

# cancellation.py

from __future__ import annotations

import asyncio


class InitiativeCancellation:

    def __init__(self) -> None:
        self._generation = 0
        self._lock = asyncio.Lock()

    async def current(self) -> int:
        async with self._lock:
            return self._generation

    async def invalidate(self) -> int:
        async with self._lock:
            self._generation += 1
            return self._generation

    async def is_valid(self, generation: int) -> bool:
        async with self._lock:
            return generation == self._generation

A newly received user input or cancellation event can invalidate pending initiative. Before execution, the controller must check that the candidate remains valid.

This counter alone does not stop an already-running operation. The runtime must also cancel or safely terminate the relevant task or audio stream, and the execution layer must recheck authorization before consequential side effects.

11. Step nine — Implement the initiative controller
Create controller.py.

The main workflow from your roadmap is:

Detect real opportunities.

Evaluate each opportunity.

Generate candidate actions.

Prioritize candidates.

Apply the initiative policy.

Commit to a still-valid candidate.

Execute through the authorized action layer.

Record the outcome.

The following is a starter integration scaffold:

# controller.py

from __future__ import annotations


class InitiativeController:

    def __init__(
        self,
        detector,
        evaluator,
        policy,
        prioritizer,
        commitment,
        cancellation,
        executor,
        repository,
    ) -> None:
        self._detector = detector
        self._evaluator = evaluator
        self._policy = policy
        self._prioritizer = prioritizer
        self._commitment = commitment
        self._cancellation = cancellation
        self._executor = executor
        self._repository = repository

    async def process(self, context):
        generation = await self._cancellation.current()

        opportunities = await self._detector.detect(context)
        candidates = []

        for opportunity in opportunities:
            if not await self._cancellation.is_valid(generation):
                return {"action": "wait", "reason": "context_changed"}

This assumes the injected components provide the corresponding interfaces; those implementations still need to be created and connected to the actual Aariya runtime.

In production, the executor should validate the cancellation generation immediately before acting. Long-running work also needs cancellation handling, and the repository must record execution failures, not only successful actions.

The core rule: the initiative controller proposes and coordinates. It must not bypass the systems that authorize what Aariya may do. 
*voice TTS.txt

12. Step ten — Evaluate what actually happened
Create outcome.py, evaluation.py, learning.py, repository.py and report.py.

The roadmap identifies these possible outcomes:

Accepted.

Ignored.

Interrupted.

Rejected.

Helpful or unhelpful.

Required correction.

Unknown.

An unacknowledged suggestion is not automatically an unsuccessful one, just as an accepted suggestion does not necessarily prove that it helped.

Record outcome evidence:

# outcome.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class InitiativeOutcome:
    action_id: str
    outcome_type: str
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

Use real interaction evidence to assess whether initiative reduced effort, supported task completion, created an interruption, required correction or was unnecessary. Unknown outcomes must remain unknown when the system cannot establish the result. 
*voice TTS.txt

Example learning loop
Aariya offers a relevant technical suggestion
                  ↓
Actual conversation outcome recorded
                  ↓
Experience model evaluates the interaction
                  ↓
Evidence indicates whether the suggestion helped
                  ↓
Initiative policy is evaluated
                  ↓
Approved change tested against regression cases
                  ↓
Promote or roll back
Learning should adjust validated policies or approved model parameters. Do not let this module directly rewrite production source code or infer durable user preferences from one isolated reaction.

14. Definition of done
Phase 62 implementation checklist

0 / 12


Opportunity detection is driven by real events or explicitly authorized monitoring.

Opportunities and candidate actions remain separate from permission to act.

Relevance and expected value are assessed against the current user goal.

Turn-taking and interruption cost are checked before proactive speech.

Memory and preferences cannot override the current user instruction.

Privacy, consent, identity, security and governance checks remain authoritative.

Phase 63 — Autonomous Conversational Attention & Salience Management
Phase 62 gave Aariya controlled initiative: she can decide whether to speak, ask, suggest, prepare something silently, or wait.

Phase 63 determines what deserves her attention in the first place.

Your *voice TTS.txt roadmap identifies the core challenge: Aariya receives signals from live speech, memory, predictions, beliefs, tools, task state, interruptions, voice performance and background processes. Processing all of them equally would create unnecessary work, distract from the current conversation and increase latency.

The objective is dynamic attention allocation: prioritize what matters now, monitor what might matter, defer what can wait, and suppress irrelevant noise without deleting useful information. 
*voice TTS.txt

1. The architecture
Incoming signals

User speech · Goals · Memory · Predictions · Tools · Runtime events

Attention candidates

Group signals into things that may need attention

Relevance

Does this matter to the current user goal?

Salience and urgency

How important is this signal right now?

Attention arbitration

Consider user focus, uncertainty, consequences and switching cost

ACTIVE

Process now

MONITORED

Track lightly

DEFERRED

Consider later

SUPPRESSED

Do not process actively now

Process → Observe outcomes → Update attention

The four destinations are important. A background signal need not compete with the live conversation to remain available, and suppressing a signal does not mean forgetting it. Mandatory security or governance events must still receive the handling required by their controlling policies. 
*voice TTS.txt

2. Create the attention package
Add this directory to the existing backend:

backend/app/voice/attention/

Responsibility boundaries

The attention package should coordinate the existing runtime, grounding and governance layers. It must not replace them.

3. Step one — Represent incoming signals
Create signal.py.

Every signal that can influence attention should have an explicit representation and evidence references.

# backend/app/voice/attention/signal.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime
from typing import Any


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class AttentionSignal:
    signal_id: str
    source: str
    signal_type: str
    payload: Any
    occurred_at: datetime
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

Examples of signals from your roadmap include:

user_started_speaking
user_finished_speaking
new_task_instruction
memory_match
tool_result
interruption
error
deadline_event
prediction_update
belief_correction
voice_failure
These should originate from actual runtime events, relevant memory retrieval, task state, or authorized monitoring. They should not be fabricated just to fill the attention queue. 
*voice TTS.txt

A signal is not yet an attention decision. It only records something that might matter.

4. Step two — Build attention candidates
Create candidate.py.

A candidate represents the thing that Aariya may need to focus on after considering one or more incoming signals.

# candidate.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Any


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class AttentionCandidate:
    candidate_id: str
    signal_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    target: Any
    context: Any

For example, several related signals could contribute to one candidate:

Signal A: user reports delayed audio
Signal B: recent stream-latency telemetry
Signal C: matching historical incident
                   ↓
Candidate: investigate current audio latency
The signals have different origins, so retain their separate evidence references. Combining them into one candidate should not erase their provenance or imply that they all support the same conclusion.

The candidate builder should use the current grounded context rather than combining anything that merely shares a keyword.

5. Step three — Separate salience, relevance, urgency and novelty
Create salience.py, relevance.py, urgency.py and novelty.py.

These components answer different questions.

Salience: How worthy of attention is this signal right now?

Relevance: Does it matter to the current conversation or task?

Urgency: Does it need to be handled soon?

Novelty: Does it provide genuinely new information?

Your roadmap identifies factors such as novelty, urgency, goal alignment, contextual relevance, user focus, consequences, change magnitude and recurrence. It also emphasizes that a signal may be salient but irrelevant. 
*voice TTS.txt

For instance, a diagnostic report may contain new and potentially useful information while the user is explaining an unresolved issue. That report might be monitored rather than interrupting the conversation.

A useful evidence model is:

# salience.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class SalienceEvidence:
    novelty: float | None
    urgency: float | None
    goal_alignment: float | None
    contextual_relevance: float | None
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

The optional values preserve the fact that the system may not know all dimensions.

Do not start by inventing a universal weighted attention score. Your source describes attention dimensions as evidence-backed rather than hardcoded points. Begin by recording each dimension, its supporting evidence and its uncertainty. Develop a ranking policy only when you can evaluate it against actual observations.

6. Step four — Maintain conversational focus
Create user_focus.py and conversational_focus.py.

Aariya needs a representation of what the user currently appears to be discussing and what remains unresolved.

# conversational_focus.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ConversationalFocus:
    topic: str | None
    goal: str | None
    active_entities: tuple[str, ...]
    unresolved_questions: tuple[str, ...]
    confidence: float | None

Consider this conversation:

Turn 1: The user discusses voice latency.
Turn 2: The user continues the same issue.
Turn 3: The user identifies a provider-specific failure.
The active focus should normally continue as the problem becomes more specific. It should not be reset after every turn.

If the user clearly changes subjects, Aariya should promote the new topic and demote the old focus while preserving the older context for later use. 
*voice TTS.txt

The key distinction is between:

What is active in the current conversation.

What is relevant but not currently active.

What is historical and only needs retrieval if relevant again.

Phase 64 in your roadmap will introduce the more complete conversational thread lifecycle. Phase 63 should provide attention and focus signals without duplicating that future subsystem.

7. Step five — Create the attention working set
Create working_set.py.

The working set maintains the items that are currently active, monitored or deferred.

# working_set.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from typing import Any


@dataclass
class AttentionWorkingSet:
    active: list[Any] = field(default_factory=list)
    monitored: list[Any] = field(default_factory=list)
    deferred: list[Any] = field(default_factory=list)

These categories have different meanings:

State

Meaning

Example

Active

Directly affects current processing

The user's current question

Monitored

Could become important; check it lightly

TTS connection health

Deferred

Potentially useful but not needed now

An unrelated optimization

Suppressed

Do not actively process at present

Duplicate low-value telemetry

SUPPRESSED is a decision about current attention, not a deletion state. The underlying record remains subject to normal privacy, retention and data-management policies. 
*voice TTS.txt

A practical lifecycle looks like:

DEFERRED
   ↓ new evidence increases relevance
MONITORED
   ↓ importance becomes actionable
ACTIVE
   ↓ relevance decreases
MONITORED or DEFERRED
These transitions should be evidence-driven, not triggered by an arbitrary timer alone.

8. Step six — Resolve competing attention demands
Create priority.py and arbitration.py.

This is the core of Phase 63.

Aariya can simultaneously have a current user request, related historical memory, a new tool result, a prediction and a background system event. The arbitrator decides which of those needs active attention, which should be monitored, and which can wait.

The roadmap identifies current user intent, task relevance, urgency, consequences, novelty, uncertainty, goal alignment and attention cost as priority considerations. It also says that priority should not be determined merely by which event arrived most recently. 
*voice TTS.txt

An initial policy contract could look like this:

# arbitration.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum


class AttentionDisposition(str, Enum):
    ACTIVE = "active"
    MONITORED = "monitored"
    DEFERRED = "deferred"
    SUPPRESSED = "suppressed"


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ArbitrationDecision:
    candidate_id: str
    disposition: AttentionDisposition
    reason: str
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

Then define an arbitrator whose rules are supplied by explicit policy components:

# arbitration.py

class AttentionArbitrator:

    def __init__(self, policy) -> None:
        self._policy = policy

    async def resolve(self, evaluated_candidates, context):
        decisions = []

        for item in evaluated_candidates:
            candidate = item["candidate"]

            decision = await self._policy.decide(
                candidate=candidate,
                salience=item["salience"],
                relevance=item["relevance"],
                context=context,
            )

            decisions.append(decision)

        return await self._policy.resolve_conflicts(
            decisions=decisions,
            context=context,
        )

This is a structural scaffold; the policy and conflict-resolution interfaces still need implementation.

Non-negotiable arbitration rules
The initial design should preserve these principles:

The user's current intent normally takes precedence over ordinary background events and speculative information.

A real, high-consequence reliability failure can be promoted when its impact warrants it.

Mandatory security or governance events must not be suppressed against their controlling policies.

An uncertain topic interpretation should not cause the entire working set to shift confidently to a new topic.

Switching attention has a cost; switch when the new demand justifies it.

The source describes the same balance between current conversational focus and important background events, with security and governance controls able to elevate mandatory handling. 
*voice TTS.txt

9. Step seven — Control attention switching, persistence and decay
Create switching.py, persistence.py and decay.py.

Without these components, Aariya could repeatedly bounce between subjects and lose the context needed to answer coherently.

Consider:

Active focus: voice latency
             ↓
Background diagnostic arrives
             ↓
Diagnostic is not urgent
             ↓
Keep monitoring it
             ↓
Continue the latency investigation
If the stream genuinely becomes unhealthy, the evidence may justify promoting the reliability event to active attention.

Three responsibilities matter here:

Switching: Determine whether a competing demand justifies changing the current focus.

Persistence: Preserve useful focus across short interruptions or temporary diversions.

Decay: Reduce attention when an item loses relevance, rather than keeping everything active indefinitely.

These decisions should be contextual. A fixed, universal timeout is not enough to determine whether a topic matters. 
*voice TTS.txt

10. Step eight — Monitor the background without creating constant activity
Create background.py, monitoring.py and interruption.py.

Some processes should continue to be monitored even while the conversational system is focused elsewhere. Your source gives stream health, TTS connections, important running tasks and authorized background operations as examples.

The architecture is:

PRIMARY ATTENTION
Current question, task or active failure
                 +
BACKGROUND MONITORING
Relevant signals checked with limited work
                 ↓
        Evidence crosses policy threshold
                 ↓
        Request attention promotion
                 ↓
        Arbitration decides what changes
Monitoring must stay within the user's authorizations and the system's privacy controls. Phase 63 must not become an excuse for indiscriminate surveillance. 
*voice TTS.txt

The attention layer should also take advantage of the existing anticipation and reliability subsystems. For example, if actual telemetry demonstrates that a particular warning often precedes a stream failure, the system may later learn to prioritize that warning. That pattern must be established from real observations rather than assumed in advance.

11. Step nine — Build the attention controller
Create controller.py.

The controller ties together candidate construction, salience evaluation, relevance assessment, suppression, prioritization and arbitration.

The intended workflow follows your source's Phase 63 design. 
*voice TTS.txt

# controller.py

from __future__ import annotations


class AttentionController:

    def __init__(
        self,
        candidate_builder,
        salience,
        relevance,
        suppressor,
        prioritizer,
        arbitrator,
        repository,
    ) -> None:
        self._candidate_builder = candidate_builder
        self._salience = salience
        self._relevance = relevance
        self._suppressor = suppressor
        self._prioritizer = prioritizer
        self._arbitrator = arbitrator
        self._repository = repository

    async def process(self, signals, context):
        candidates = await self._candidate_builder.build(
            signals,
            context,
        )

        evaluated = []

        for candidate in candidates:
            # Mandatory-event and policy handling must occur
            # before a candidate can be suppressed.

This shows the intended orchestration only. The injected interfaces, handling rules and repository methods need to be implemented against Aariya's existing runtime. Mandatory event detection and security policy integration also need to be designed carefully so that the ordinary suppression path cannot bypass them.

A useful controller result should identify the active focus, monitored signals, deferred candidates, suppression decisions and reasons for important attention changes.

12. Step ten — Use attention to improve voice naturalness
Phase 63 affects how Aariya organizes and speaks her answers, not simply which subsystem receives computing resources.

The roadmap connects attention to this sequence:

Attention
    ↓
Semantic importance
    ↓
Information structure
    ↓
Prosodic focus
    ↓
Emphasis, pitch and timing
For example, when the user asks why audio is lagging, Aariya should prioritize the probable cause, relevant evidence and next useful step over unrelated architectural background. The voice should then emphasize the information that matters most rather than giving every sentence equal prominence. 
*voice TTS.txt

This connects attention with your existing response planning and prosody systems. Do not build another voice-personality mode for focused speech; pass the semantic priority information to the existing voice composition layer.

13. Step eleven — Learn from real attention outcomes
Create outcome.py, learning.py, repository.py and report.py.

The attention system can learn which signals tend to matter in a particular context, which are usually noise, which switches are costly, and which background events later become relevant.

For example:

Real TTS telemetry
       ↓
Warning followed by stream degradation
       ↓
Record evidence and outcome
       ↓
Evaluate whether earlier promotion would help
       ↓
Test an attention policy change
       ↓
Measure false promotions and missed failures
       ↓
Promote or reject the change
Your source explicitly says the learning system should use actual historical evidence. 
*voice TTS.txt

Do not assume every warning predicts failure or that every attention switch is beneficial. Evaluate improvements against both successful detections and false alarms. Keep the original evidence and the provenance of learned patterns so that Aariya does not treat an unverified correlation as a confirmed rule.

14. Definition of done
Use the following checklist as an acceptance gate for Phase 63.

Phase 63 checklist

0 / 12


Signals have stable identifiers, timestamps and evidence references.

Candidate construction preserves the provenance of its contributing signals.

Salience, relevance, urgency and novelty remain distinct evaluation dimensions.

Current user intent and goal alignment influence attention arbitration.

The working set supports active, monitored and deferred states.

Suppression does not delete records or bypass required security and governance handling.

Attention switching accounts for focus continuity and switching cost.

Background monitoring stays within authorized scope.

Important runtime failures can be promoted according to existing policy.

Attention decisions can be inspected through a diagnostic report.

Prosodic emphasis uses semantic importance from the attention system.

Learning uses genuine outcomes and is evaluated before policy changes are promoted.


Phase 64 — Autonomous Conversational Thread & Context Lifecycle Management
Phase 63 gave Aariya the ability to decide what deserves attention right now. Phase 64 gives her the ability to manage multiple conversations and unfinished topics over time without losing important context.

The problem is that real conversations rarely follow a single uninterrupted path. You might discuss voice latency, switch to a TTS provider question, talk about deployment, and later return to the original latency issue.

Aariya needs to preserve unfinished work, recognize when a familiar topic returns, restore only the relevant context, and distinguish current facts from outdated assumptions. That is the purpose of Phase 64 in your *voice TTS.txt roadmap. 
*voice TTS.txt

1. The architecture
Incoming conversation

New message, continued topic, subject change or return to an old topic

Thread detection and identification

Determine which conversational workstream the input belongs to

Continue

Update current thread

Switch

Restore an existing thread

Create

Start a new thread

Consistency and freshness checks

Validate restored context against current beliefs, memory and instructions

Active context window

Current goal, validated facts, decisions, unresolved questions and relevant evidence

Continue working

Reason and respond using restored context

Manage lifecycle

Suspend, compress, complete or archive

Your roadmap defines the key lifecycle states as active, suspended, completed, blocked, expired, and archived. These describe the state of the conversational workstream—not the retention state of its underlying data. 
*voice TTS.txt

The package should own conversational thread identity, lifecycle, context restoration, and context compression. It should not replace your existing memory, grounding, attention, preference or anticipation systems.

3. Step one — Create the thread model
Create thread.py.

A thread represents a coherent conversational workstream: for example, diagnosing voice latency, configuring a TTS provider, or preparing a deployment.

# backend/app/voice/threads/thread.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from datetime import datetime
from enum import Enum


class ThreadStatus(str, Enum):
    ACTIVE = "active"
    SUSPENDED = "suspended"
    COMPLETED = "completed"
    BLOCKED = "blocked"
    EXPIRED = "expired"
    ARCHIVED = "archived"


@dataclass
class ConversationThread:
    thread_id: str
    topic: str | None
    goal: str | None
    state: ThreadStatus
    created_at: datetime
    updated_at: datetime
    active: bool = False
    unresolved_items: list[str] = field(
        default_factory=list
    )

The thread_id is the stable identifier. The topic is a human-readable description that can evolve as understanding improves.

Do not identify a thread solely by matching its exact wording. “Why does streaming become slow?” and “The audio latency increases over time” might refer to the same workstream.

Likewise, two messages that both mention “TTS” might belong to different workstreams, such as voice quality and provider configuration. Identification must use grounded context and conversational history, not keywords alone. 
*voice TTS.txt

Define an explicit thread state
Create state.py:

# state.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ThreadState:
    thread_id: str
    status: str
    last_turn_id: str | None
    context_version: int
    confidence: float | None

The context version allows other components to distinguish the latest thread state from an older snapshot.

For a production implementation, persist updates transactionally and guard against concurrent updates. An old asynchronous response should not overwrite a newer thread state.

4. Step two — Detect the correct thread
Create detector.py and classifier.py.

The system needs to distinguish four outcomes:

continue_existing
switch_existing
start_new
uncertain
The first three permit a confident lifecycle decision. The fourth means Aariya does not yet have sufficient evidence to determine where the input belongs.

# classifier.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ThreadClassification:
    action: str
    thread_id: str | None
    confidence: float | None
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

The detector should consult the grounded current input, active thread, known threads and relevant conversation history.

For example:

User input

Possible classification

“The latency is still high.”

Continue an existing latency investigation, if context supports it

“Before that, which TTS provider are we using?”

Switch to the existing provider thread, if one matches

“Now help me plan the deployment.”

Switch to a matching deployment thread or create a new one

“What about that other issue?”

Uncertain if several unresolved threads fit

These are illustrative examples, not fixed keyword rules.

Important: if the classification is uncertain, do not silently switch to whichever thread looks most likely. Preserve the current state, retain the candidate alternatives and seek clarification when the distinction matters. This follows your roadmap's requirement to represent uncertainty during thread identification. 
*voice TTS.txt
*voice TTS.txt

5. Step three — Maintain separate active and historical context
Create context_window.py.

Aariya should not carry the entire conversation history into every response. The context window should separate information by its current usefulness.

# context_window.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from typing import Any


@dataclass
class ContextWindow:
    active: list[Any] = field(default_factory=list)
    recent: list[Any] = field(default_factory=list)
    compressed: list[Any] = field(default_factory=list)
    referenced: list[Any] = field(default_factory=list)

The categories mean:

Active: information directly needed for the current response.

Recent: nearby conversational context that may explain references or transitions.

Compressed: validated summaries of longer discussions.

Referenced: historical information retrieved when the current task needs it.

This lets the thread manager restore a useful working context without treating every previous sentence as equally important. The source describes this separation as a core scalability feature. 
*voice TTS.txt

For Aariya, this also establishes a clean distinction between long-term memory and active reasoning. A memory may be relevant to a topic but still not deserve a place in the current working set.

6. Step four — Switch threads without losing unfinished work
Create switch.py, suspension.py, and resumption.py.

Suppose the conversation follows this sequence:

Thread A: Voice latency investigation
       ↓
User asks about the TTS provider
       ↓
Thread A becomes suspended
Thread B becomes active
       ↓
User says: "Back to the latency issue."
       ↓
Thread A becomes active again
The thread manager should preserve the original workstream and its unresolved questions during the switch.

The switching behavior is:

# switch.py — interface scaffold

from __future__ import annotations


class ThreadSwitcher:

    def __init__(self, repository) -> None:
        self._repository = repository

    async def switch(self, target_thread_id, state):
        current = state.active_thread

        if current is not None:
            await self._repository.update_status(
                thread_id=current.thread_id,
                status="suspended",
            )

        target = await self._repository.get_thread(
            target_thread_id
        )

        if target is None:
            raise LookupError(
                f"Unknown thread: {target_thread_id}"
            )

        await self._repository.update_status(
            thread_id=target.thread_id,
            status="active",
        )

        return target

This is a starting interface, not a complete transactional implementation. In production, switching, updating active-thread metadata, and recording state changes should be coordinated atomically so that two threads do not accidentally become active for the same session.

Suspending a thread means it is no longer the active focus; it does not mean the thread is deleted. When the user returns, Aariya should restore the relevant context and incorporate newer evidence. 
*voice TTS.txt

7. Step five — Restore context selectively
Create restoration.py.

A common failure in conversational systems is to retrieve too much historical information, including outdated assumptions and unrelated details.

The Phase 64 restoration pipeline should select:

The thread's current goal.

The latest validated facts.

Important decisions and constraints.

Unresolved questions and unfinished tasks.

Relevant user preferences.

Necessary memory references.

A current-belief consistency check.

That is the restoration process defined in your roadmap. 
*voice TTS.txt

A starter model for restored context:

# restoration.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass, field


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class RestoredContext:
    thread_id: str
    goal: str | None
    validated_facts: tuple[str, ...]
    decisions: tuple[str, ...]
    open_questions: tuple[str, ...]
    constraints: tuple[str, ...]
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]
    warnings: tuple[str, ...] = field(
        default_factory=tuple
    )

The fields above are a simple starting contract. In a more complete implementation, facts and decisions should be structured records with status, provenance, timestamps, and source references rather than untyped strings.

Restoring a thread is not restoring its old beliefs blindly
Imagine that an earlier conversation recorded:

Original assumption:
Provider A is responsible for the latency.
Later, the user corrects it:

Correction:
The affected component uses Provider B.
The restored context should distinguish the old, corrected assumption from the current supported information. It must not present both as equally valid facts.

This is why restoration must run a consistency check against current grounding, memory and user instructions before making a thread active. 
*voice TTS.txt

8. Step six — Compress long conversations without losing meaning
Create compression.py and summarization.py.

Aariya needs to compress long threads as they grow, but a summary must preserve the information needed to continue the work correctly.

Your roadmap specifies this progression:

Raw conversation
       ↓
Recent context
       ↓
Important events
       ↓
Compressed context
       ↓
Thread summary
       ↓
Long-term memory candidates
Each stage should retain provenance. A compressed summary is derived information, not a replacement for the original evidence. 
*voice TTS.txt

The summary should preserve:

Information

What must survive compression

Goal

What the conversation is trying to accomplish

Validated facts

What has been supported by evidence

Corrections

Which previous assumptions have been superseded

Decisions

What the user selected or rejected

Constraints

Required limitations and conditions

Open questions

What remains unresolved

Progress

What has actually been completed

Evidence

References to records or source material

For example, a lengthy discussion might compress to:

THREAD: Voice latency investigation

GOAL:
Identify why latency increases during extended sessions.

CONFIRMED:
Streaming is enabled.
Evidence: [source references]

CORRECTED:
Provider A was an earlier mistaken assumption.
Current provider: Provider B.
Evidence: [source references]

DECISION:
Approach B was selected.
Approach A was rejected.

UNRESOLVED:
The cause of the latency increase remains unknown.

NEXT USEFUL INVESTIGATION:
Inspect time-correlated streaming and buffering telemetry.
The final line should be included as a proposed next step only when it is supported by the actual task state and evidence.

Do not compress an unresolved question into nothing. Do not record a task as complete because the conversation became long. Do not merge an earlier belief and a later correction without marking their relationship.

9. Step seven — Separate thread completion, expiration and forgetting
Create resolution.py, expiration.py, and forgetting.py.

These modules have different jobs.

Completion determines whether the thread's goal has been achieved.

Expiration evaluates whether a thread has become stale enough to consider retiring its active context.

Forgetting manages the removal or retirement of information under the applicable retention policies.

Your roadmap explicitly warns that simply stopping discussion does not prove a goal was completed. Likewise, making a thread inactive does not automatically mean its underlying records should be deleted. 
*voice TTS.txt

A reasonable lifecycle could be:

ACTIVE
  ↓ topic change
SUSPENDED
  ↓ no longer useful in the working set
COMPRESSED
  ↓ lifecycle policy permits
ARCHIVED
  ↓ explicit expiration conditions met
EXPIRED
This is not a mandatory sequence for every thread: a thread can instead become completed or blocked depending on its state.

Keep two separate controls:

Context lifecycle: whether the information remains active, restorable, archived or expired as a conversational thread.

Data retention: whether stored records may continue to be retained under privacy and retention rules.

Never use a thread-lifecycle change as an implicit substitute for deletion or privacy enforcement.

10. Step eight — Integrate thread management with Aariya's existing systems
Existing phase

Integration responsibility

Phase 55 — Experience

Measure whether restoring context reduces repeated explanations and switching friction

Phase 56 — Preferences

Apply relevant communication preferences within the current thread

Phase 57 — Memory

Retrieve relevant history; create memory candidates only for information that warrants long-term retention

Phase 58 — Anticipation

Predict possible returns to suspended threads, without treating a prediction as certainty

Phase 59 — Grounding

Validate restored facts and resolve conflicts with current evidence

Phase 60 — Metacognition

Assess whether the correct thread was restored and whether assumptions remain reliable

Phase 61 — Repair

Recover if Aariya selects the wrong thread

Phase 62 — Initiative

Decide whether an unfinished topic merits proactive mention; an unfinished thread alone does not justify interrupting

Phase 63 — Attention

Select which existing thread should be active now

These responsibilities and integration paths are specified in Phase 64 of your source. 
*voice TTS.txt

The runtime relationship becomes:

User input
    ↓
Grounding
    ↓
Thread identification
    ↓
Attention selection
    ↓
Thread context restoration
    ↓
Consistency / freshness checks
    ↓
Memory + preferences + anticipation
    ↓
Metacognition
    ↓
Planning and response
    ↓
Voice and runtime monitoring
    ↓
Experience evaluation
The thread manager should expose clear interfaces to these systems. It should not duplicate their underlying models or take ownership of their authorization rules.

11. Step nine — Build the main controller
Create controller.py.

The controller coordinates detection, classification, switching or thread creation, consistency checks, context restoration and persistence.

# controller.py

from __future__ import annotations


class ThreadController:

    def __init__(
        self,
        detector,
        classifier,
        switcher,
        restorer,
        consistency,
        repository,
    ) -> None:
        self._detector = detector
        self._classifier = classifier
        self._switcher = switcher
        self._restorer = restorer
        self._consistency = consistency
        self._repository = repository

    async def process(self, current_input, state):
        detection = await self._detector.detect(
            current_input=current_input,
            active_thread=state.active_thread,
            known_threads=state.threads,
            context=state.context,
        )

        classification = await self._classifier.classify(
            detection,
            state,
        )


This is an integration scaffold, not a drop-in working implementation. The switcher.create, classifier, repository, consistency checker and restorer need concrete interfaces that match your codebase.

Before production, also handle concurrent turns, persistence failures, thread-update conflicts and cancellation of stale asynchronous work. If a restoration fails, Aariya should avoid pretending that the expected context was successfully recovered.

12. Step ten — Test the thread lifecycle
Use these as initial acceptance tests.

Test scenario

Expected result

User continues the current topic

Continue the same thread

User changes to a known, unfinished topic

Suspend the former thread and activate the matching one

User returns to an old issue

Restore its relevant context

User refers ambiguously to multiple issues

Preserve the current state and represent uncertainty

A restored summary contains an outdated assumption

Mark the conflict and prefer current validated evidence

A thread has several unresolved questions

Preserve them during compression

The user previously rejected an approach

Restore the rejection as a decision, not an open option

A thread has not been mentioned for a while

Do not assume the goal is complete

Two updates race to change active-thread state

Prevent inconsistent active-thread metadata

A thread is archived

Apply retention rules independently from the archive action

A wrong thread is selected

Support switching back and route the incident to repair

A thread is compressed and then restored

Retain provenance for the facts and decisions used

Definition of done
Phase 64 checklist

0 / 12


Threads have stable IDs, explicit lifecycle states and versioned context.

Thread identification distinguishes continuing, switching, creating and uncertain cases.

Uncertainty does not silently force an incorrect thread switch.

Active, recent, compressed and referenced contexts are handled separately.

Suspended threads can be restored without replaying the full conversation.

Restoration checks current grounding and labels outdated assumptions.

Compression preserves corrections, decisions, constraints and unresolved questions.

Thread completion is not inferred merely from a lack of discussion.

Context lifecycle and data retention are independently enforced.

Memory candidates are validated rather than generated from every thread.

Thread switching is reversible and safe against concurrent updates.

Tests cover restoration, compression, uncertainty, conflicts and persistence failure.

Phase 65 — Autonomous Conversational Goal & Task Management
Phase 64 gave Aariya structured conversational threads and the ability to restore relevant context. Phase 65 gives each thread an explicit goal and manages the tasks required to achieve it.

The central question is:

What are we trying to accomplish, what has actually been completed, what remains, what is blocking progress, and what should happen next?

Your *voice TTS.txt roadmap moves from conversation to goal, tasks, progress, next actions and verified outcomes. It also establishes an important boundary: Aariya may help execute the user's goal, but should not invent goals on the user's behalf. 
*voice TTS.txt

1. The architecture
User instruction and current context

Current intent, grounded beliefs and active thread

Goal identification and grounding

Identify the intended outcome; preserve uncertainty

Goal decomposition

Goals → Tasks → Subtasks → Dependencies → Prerequisites

Progress

What is verified as complete?

Blockers

What prevents further work?

Next-action candidates and prioritization

Consider dependencies, risk, cost, evidence and authorization

Execute

When authorized and ready

Ask

When clarification is needed

Wait

When blocked or not ready

Verify outcome → Update task and goal state

This structure follows Phase 65's distinction between identifying the next task and managing the complete goal state, including dependencies, blockers, execution, verification and recovery. 
*voice TTS.txt

The responsibilities should remain separate:

Component

Responsibility

Goals and intent

Tasks and dependencies

Integration bridges
