The goal manager should coordinate existing capabilities. It should not reimplement the TTS engine, tool execution system, memory provider or security controls.

3. Step one — Define explicit goal states
Create goal.py, goal_state.py and intent.py.

A goal describes a desired outcome grounded in the conversation. The roadmap identifies states including active, blocked, paused, completed, abandoned and unknown. It also distinguishes an initially hypothesized goal from one that has been confirmed. 
*voice TTS.txt

# backend/app/voice/goals/goal.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum


class GoalStatus(str, Enum):
    ACTIVE = "active"
    BLOCKED = "blocked"
    PAUSED = "paused"
    COMPLETED = "completed"
    ABANDONED = "abandoned"
    UNKNOWN = "unknown"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"
    SUPERSEDED = "superseded"


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Goal:
    goal_id: str
    description: str
    thread_id: str
    status: GoalStatus
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

A goal needs a stable identifier, a description, its owning conversation thread, a lifecycle state and evidence references.

Now define the initial intent:

# intent.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class GoalIntent:
    description: str
    confidence: float | None
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]
    confirmed: bool

The confirmed field is an initial implementation choice for distinguishing a proposed goal from a grounded goal. In a full implementation, represent confirmation and uncertainty more richly if your grounding interface already supports those concepts.

The most important rule: distinguish observation from intent
Consider these requests:

User: “I'm looking at the voice latency.”

That does not necessarily establish a goal to change the system.

User: “I want to find out why voice latency is increasing.”

This supports a diagnostic goal.

User: “Now fix the latency issue.”

This expands the scope toward remediation, subject to available capabilities and authorization.

User: “Don't change anything. Just explain the problem.”

This narrows the goal back to analysis and explanation.

The roadmap explicitly requires goal inference to be evidence-based and current user intent to override a conflicting prior plan. 
*voice TTS.txt

4. Step two — Represent tasks, subtasks and dependencies
Create task.py, subtask.py, dependency.py and prerequisite.py.

A goal can contain several tasks, and a task can depend on another task being completed first.

For example:

GOAL: Diagnose voice latency
  |
  +-- Task 1: Capture current latency measurements
  |
  +-- Task 2: Identify the bottleneck
  |       Depends on: Task 1
  |
  +-- Task 3: Test a candidate fix
  |       Depends on: Task 2
  |
  +-- Task 4: Validate the improvement
          Depends on: Task 3
Your roadmap uses this kind of hierarchy to turn a vague desired result into structured work. It also specifies that task dependencies must be explicit rather than repeatedly attempting work whose prerequisites are incomplete. 
*voice TTS.txt

Start with task.py:

# task.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from enum import Enum


class TaskStatus(str, Enum):
    PENDING = "pending"
    ACTIVE = "active"
    BLOCKED = "blocked"
    COMPLETED = "completed"
    FAILED = "failed"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Task:
    task_id: str
    goal_id: str
    description: str
    status: TaskStatus
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

Then define dependencies in dependency.py:

# dependency.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class TaskDependency:
    task_id: str
    depends_on: str
    relation: str

Possible relations in the source include requires, blocks, enables and follows.

A production dependency manager should reject invalid dependency references and detect cycles that would prevent work from becoming ready. A task that depends on its own completion, directly or indirectly, must not be repeatedly scheduled as though it were executable.

5. Step three — Model milestones, progress and blockers
Create milestone.py, progress.py and blocker.py.

Aariya needs to know not only what tasks exist but what has actually happened.

For example:

VOICE LATENCY GOAL

✓ Capture baseline measurements
✓ Validate first-audio delay
✓ Confirm TTS streaming behavior
→ Investigate buffering
○ Run controlled experiment
○ Validate improvement
○ Promote the approved change
The symbols should correspond to real task records and evidence, not text generated to make the goal look productive. The roadmap specifically rejects fabricated percentages and synthetic success states. 
*voice TTS.txt

A starting progress model is:

# progress.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ProgressState:
    completed_tasks: int
    total_known_tasks: int
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

Do not derive a meaningful percentage if the task model is incomplete or if tasks have unequal importance and effort. Reporting “3 of 5 known tasks completed” may be more truthful than displaying an unjustified “60% complete.”

Create an explicit blocker model:

# blocker.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Blocker:
    blocker_id: str
    task_id: str
    category: str
    description: str
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

Examples from the roadmap include:

Missing information.

Waiting for the user.

Unavailable tool.

Incomplete dependency.

Authorization required.

Failed validation.

Uncertain requirement.

External system failure.

A blocker should affect scheduling. If authorization is required, the scheduler must not repeatedly retry the task as though it were ready.

6. Step four — Represent next-action candidates
Create next_action.py and candidate.py.

A goal may have several possible next actions, and the system should distinguish a candidate from an approved decision.

Your source defines possible action types such as continue, ask, clarify, research, inspect, measure, experiment, execute, verify, wait, pause and escalate. 
*voice TTS.txt

# next_action.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class NextAction:
    action_id: str
    task_id: str
    action_type: str
    description: str
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

A candidate can carry additional evaluation information:

# candidate.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ActionCandidate:
    action_id: str
    task_id: str
    action_type: str
    expected_value: float | None
    risk: object
    confidence: float | None
    reversible: bool

These values should be grounded in actual evaluation results where available. Do not fabricate expected value or confidence merely to force a ranking.

Prioritize the right action, not the easiest action
Your roadmap calls for considering goal relevance, dependency readiness, urgency, expected value, cost, risk, confidence, current user intent and reversibility. 
*voice TTS.txt

For example:

Candidate

Readiness

Appropriate decision

Inspect an available latency trace

Prerequisites satisfied and permitted

Consider executing

Run a benchmark requiring an unavailable configuration

Blocked by dependency

Wait or resolve the prerequisite

Modify a consequential configuration

Requires authorization under policy

Obtain required authorization first

Explain an observed error

User requested explanation only

Answer without expanding into system modifications

The prioritization system should not blindly select whichever action is simplest or has the highest guessed score.

7. Step five — Add scheduling and readiness checks
Create scheduling.py.

A candidate action is ready only when its dependencies, required information, authorization and active goal state permit it. Your roadmap states that the outcome should be execution when ready, and blocking, waiting or asking otherwise. 
*voice TTS.txt

The scheduling contract can begin simply:

# scheduling.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ReadinessResult:
    ready: bool
    reason: str
    missing_requirements: tuple[str, ...] = ()


class TaskScheduler:

    def __init__(self, dependency_checker, policy) -> None:
        self._dependency_checker = dependency_checker
        self._policy = policy

    async def assess(self, action, goal, context):
        dependencies_ready = (
            await self._dependency_checker.check(
                action.task_id,
                context,
            )
        )

        if not dependencies_ready.ready:
            return ReadinessResult(
                ready=False,
                reason="dependency_not_ready",
                missing_requirements=(
                    dependencies_ready.missing_requirements
                ),
            )

This is a starter interface. The actual dependency checker and policy must be connected to the existing task graph and governance services.

Phase 50 remains authoritative. Do not hardcode every action's confirmation requirement in the goal manager. The existing governance architecture should determine which actions are permitted, which need confirmation and which are prohibited.

8. Step six — Delegate execution to existing capabilities
Create execution.py.

The goal manager should not implement its own copy of every action. Your source specifically recommends delegating execution to the existing subsystem controller registered for the requested action type. 
*voice TTS.txt

# execution.py

from __future__ import annotations


class GoalTaskExecutor:

    async def execute(self, action, context):
        controller = context.action_registry.resolve(
            action.action_type
        )

        return await controller.execute(
            action,
            context,
        )

For Aariya, this is a significant architectural boundary:

Goal Manager
    ↓
Select justified action
    ↓
Readiness and authorization checks
    ↓
Existing capability/controller
    ↓
Actual execution result
    ↓
Goal verification
The goals package manages why and when an action is selected. The existing capability owns how that action is performed.

An action registry must not become an authorization bypass. The selected controller and its execution pathway must enforce the relevant identity, security, privacy and governance restrictions too.

9. Step seven — Verify outcomes before marking tasks complete
Create verification.py and completion.py.

This is one of the most important parts of Phase 65.

Executing an action does not prove that the intended outcome occurred.

Suppose Aariya changes a TTS configuration. The configuration update succeeds, but subsequent measurement shows that latency worsened. The task is not complete just because the write operation succeeded.

The roadmap specifies this lifecycle:

Execute
   ↓
Measure
   ↓
Verify
   ↓
Complete only when evidence supports success
*voice TTS.txt
Use a verification contract:

# verification.py

from __future__ import annotations


class TaskVerifier:

    async def verify(self, task, result, context):
        return await context.verification_engine.verify(
            task=task,
            result=result,
        )

The verification engine should check the intended task outcome against actual evidence.

When success is not established, preserve an appropriate state such as active, blocked, failed or unknown rather than automatically marking the task complete.

If a failed change requires rollback or diagnosis, connect to Phase 61 for recovery and update Phase 65's task state so subsequent planning reflects the real outcome. 
*voice TTS.txt

10. Step eight — Handle pause, resumption and abandonment
Create pause.py, resumption.py and abandonment.py.

A goal can stop making progress without being complete or abandoned.

The roadmap gives examples such as waiting for the user, waiting for an external system, a missing dependency, excessive uncertainty, required authorization or a user changing context. 
*voice TTS.txt

The lifecycle should distinguish:

Paused: Work is temporarily suspended.

Blocked: A specific prerequisite or condition prevents progress.

Completed: The desired outcome has been verified.

Cancelled: Execution has been stopped under the applicable control.

Abandoned: Evidence supports ending the goal rather than merely postponing it.

Superseded: A newer goal or instruction has replaced the previous plan.

A topic change alone does not prove abandonment. Phase 64 preserves the thread; Phase 65 preserves the goal and its task state within that thread.

When the user returns to an unfinished goal, restore validated task records and blockers rather than reconstructing progress from memory alone. 
*voice TTS.txt

11. Step nine — Integrate with the existing Aariya phases
Existing phase

Contribution to goal management

Phase 50 — Governance

Determines action authorization, confirmation requirements and policy restrictions

Phase 55 — Experience

Measures task friction, unnecessary steps, recovery and successful outcomes

Phase 56 — Preferences

Adapts progress communication without changing the goal itself

Phase 57 — Memory

Supplies validated prior decisions and historical task information

Phase 58 — Anticipation

Helps prepare for likely next tasks without automatically executing consequential actions

Phase 59 — Grounding

Validates the interpretation of the goal and current facts

Phase 60 — Metacognition

Evaluates confidence, assumptions and whether clarification is needed

Phase 61 — Repair

Handles conversational recovery while the task state is updated

Phase 62 — Initiative

Determines whether autonomously continuing is appropriate

Phase 63 — Attention

Allocates attention among multiple active goals

Phase 64 — Thread management

Supplies the owning thread and supports goal resumption

These are the integration responsibilities described in Phase 65 of your source. 
*voice TTS.txt

The hierarchy now becomes:

THREAD
   ↓
GOAL
   ↓
TASK
   ↓
SUBTASK
   ↓
ACTION
   ↓
RESULT
   ↓
VERIFIED OUTCOME
A thread can contain multiple goals over its lifetime. Goals can be paused and resumed; tasks can be blocked or retried; actions can fail. Those states should remain explicit instead of being hidden in conversational text.

12. Step ten — Build the Goal Controller
Create controller.py.

The controller should coordinate goal detection, planning, prioritization, readiness checks, execution, verification and persistence.

# controller.py

from __future__ import annotations


class GoalController:

    def __init__(
        self,
        detector,
        planner,
        prioritizer,
        scheduler,
        executor,
        verifier,
        repository,
    ) -> None:
        self._detector = detector
        self._planner = planner
        self._prioritizer = prioritizer
        self._scheduler = scheduler
        self._executor = executor
        self._verifier = verifier
        self._repository = repository

    async def process(self, context):
        goals = await self._detector.detect(context)
        records = []

        for goal in goals:
            # Recheck against current intent and grounding.
            goal_check = await context.grounding.validate_goal(
                goal,
                context,
            )


This is an architectural scaffold, not a production-ready implementation. The repository, planner, verifier, grounding bridge, scheduler and goal-state operations must be concretely defined against the current codebase. In particular, the controller needs explicit handling for failed execution, user cancellation, concurrent instructions and stale plans before it can be connected to live autonomous operation.

The important behavior is the separation between interpreting the user's goal, proposing an action, checking readiness, delegating execution and verifying the result. That is the structure described in your roadmap. 
*voice TTS.txt

0 / 12


Goals and initial goal intent have separate representations.

Goals, tasks, dependencies, prerequisites and blockers are explicit records.

Phase 66 — Autonomous Goal-Oriented Planning & Hierarchical Action Reasoning
Phase 65 gave Aariya goals, tasks, dependencies, blockers and next-action candidates. Phase 66 gives her the ability to reason about complete plans, compare alternative approaches, anticipate failures and change course when new evidence arrives.

Your *voice TTS.txt roadmap draws a clear distinction:

Task management: What should happen next?

Planning: Which sequence of actions is most likely to achieve the goal, given the current state, constraints, risks, costs and possible failures?

The planner should not simply generate a sequence of steps and execute them blindly. It should develop alternatives, evaluate them, validate the chosen plan, execute through controlled interfaces, observe actual results and replan when necessary. 
*voice TTS.txt

1. The architecture
Grounded goal + current state

Goal, beliefs, completed tasks, blockers, resources and constraints

Available action space

Only capabilities that actually exist and are available

Plan generation

Construct several possible paths to the desired outcome

Plan A

Current approach

Plan B

Alternative approach

Plan C

Contingency path

Plan evaluation and meta-control

Compare success prospects, evidence, risks, cost, user effort and reversibility

Validation and governance

Check preconditions, constraints, resources and authorization

Controlled execution with checkpoints

Result matches

Continue and verify

Result differs

Reassess or replan

This reflects the full planning loop defined in Phase 66: generate alternatives, evaluate consequences, validate, execute, compare predicted and actual outcomes, and replan when necessary. 
*voice TTS.txt

*voice TTS.txt

The modules should be separated by responsibility:

Module group

Purpose

plan.py, state.py, objective.py

Represent the plan, current world/task state and measurable success conditions

constraint.py, assumption.py, precondition.py

Define what must remain true and what must be verified before acting

action.py, action_space.py, effect.py, dependency.py

Represent actual available actions, their consequences and dependencies

validator.py, commitment.py, confirmation.py, governance.py

Validate plans and control authorization and commitment

replanner.py, interruption.py, rollback.py

React to changed conditions, cancellation and failed execution

learning.py, repository.py, report.py

Preserve evidence and evaluate planning performance

The core design rule is that the planner coordinates existing capabilities. It must not create a parallel implementation of every tool, subsystem or execution mechanism.

3. Step one — Define the planning state and success conditions
Create state.py and objective.py.

Before Aariya generates a plan, she needs to know the goal, current state, completed work, blockers, relevant beliefs and resource availability. 
*voice TTS.txt

# state.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class PlanningState:
    goal: object
    active_tasks: tuple
    completed_tasks: tuple
    blockers: tuple
    beliefs: object
    resources: object
    context: object

The objective must also describe what success actually means:

# objective.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class PlanningObjective:
    description: str
    success_conditions: tuple
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

For a voice-latency goal, “make it faster” is not a sufficiently precise success condition. A better objective might be:

Measured first-audio latency improves against a valid baseline.

Voice quality does not regress beyond the permitted limit.

Identity and safety requirements remain satisfied.

The result can be reproduced and verified.

The actual thresholds and constraints must come from the system's requirements and measurements. Do not invent values to make a plan look precise.

4. Step two — Model constraints and assumptions explicitly
Create constraint.py and assumption.py.

A plan is not valid merely because its actions could achieve the goal.

Constraints can be hard or soft. Hard constraints include security requirements, user instructions, authorization and governance restrictions. Soft constraints can include preferences for lower latency, reduced cost or less user effort. 
*voice TTS.txt

# constraint.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class PlanningConstraint:
    constraint_id: str
    description: str
    hard: bool
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

Assumptions need their own records:

# assumption.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class PlanningAssumption:
    assumption_id: str
    proposition: object
    confidence: float | None
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

For example, a plan may assume that the active TTS provider supports a particular audio control. Before executing a step that depends on that feature, Aariya needs to validate the assumption against the provider's actual capabilities.

If an assumption materially affects safety or correctness and remains unverified, the plan should gather evidence, ask the user, choose another path or wait.

5. Step three — Define available actions and expected effects
Create action.py, action_space.py, precondition.py and effect.py.

This is where Phase 66 goes beyond Phase 65's next-action model.

Phase 65 might know that the next task is to inspect latency. Phase 66 also represents what the inspection requires, what it might reveal, what it changes and which later actions depend on its result. 
*voice TTS.txt

# action.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class PlannedAction:
    action_id: str
    action_type: str
    description: str
    preconditions: tuple
    expected_effects: tuple
    reversible: bool

The planner must work within the action space registered by existing capabilities:

# action_space.py

from __future__ import annotations


class ActionSpace:

    def __init__(self, action_registry) -> None:
        self._registry = action_registry

    async def available(self, context):
        return await self._registry.available_actions(
            context
        )

Do not invent a capability just because a plan would be easier if it existed. The available action space should reflect actual registered actions, current availability and the permissions that apply.

Expected effects should be treated as predictions. They are not evidence of what actually happened.

6. Step four — Build hierarchical plans with dependencies and branches
Create node.py, branch.py, contingency.py and alternative.py.

A useful plan can contain a tree or graph of actions. Some steps must happen sequentially; some may run independently; others depend on a condition.

Consider the voice-latency example:

Goal: Reduce first-audio latency
                  |
                  v
        Measure baseline latency
                  |
                  v
        Identify dominant bottleneck
             /      |       \
            /       |        \
       Provider  Buffering  Orchestration
          |          |           |
          v          v           v
       Diagnose   Diagnose     Diagnose
          \          |           /
           \         |          /
            v        v         v
            Select evidence-supported fix
                          |
                          v
                   Controlled test
                          |
                          v
                  Compare with baseline
                          |
                   +------+------+
                   |             |
                Improves      Regresses
                   |             |
                   v             v
                 Canary       Rollback or
                   |          reassess
                   v
              Verify result
This is an illustrative planning structure, not a claim that any particular bottleneck has been observed in your Aariya runtime. Your source describes these conditional provider, buffering and orchestration branches as examples of evidence-driven planning. 
*voice TTS.txt

A contingency explicitly connects a trigger to an alternative plan:

# contingency.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Contingency:
    trigger: object
    alternative_plan_id: str
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

For example, if an actual provider test demonstrates that a required feature is unavailable, the plan can branch toward a validated alternative rather than repeatedly attempting the same unsupported action.

7. Step five — Generate multiple candidate plans
Create generator.py and alternative.py.

The first plausible plan should not automatically win. The roadmap explicitly calls for generating and comparing alternatives. 
*voice TTS.txt

For a grounded goal, Aariya might generate:

Candidate

Strategy

Main consideration

Plan A

Diagnose and optimize the current provider

May minimize migration effort

Plan B

Evaluate a different provider

May address a validated provider limitation, but adds migration work

Plan C

Investigate buffering and orchestration first

May identify an internal bottleneck without changing providers

Which plan is best depends on actual evidence. The example does not establish that one of these approaches is currently preferable.

Use an initial generator interface:

# generator.py

from __future__ import annotations


class PlanGenerator:

    async def generate(self, state, action_space):
        actions = await action_space.available(
            state.context
        )

        return await self._construct_candidates(
            state,
            actions,
        )

    async def _construct_candidates(self, state, actions):

        # Candidate plans must be built from available actions,
        # task state, dependencies, constraints and evidence.
        raise NotImplementedError

Important: the source's sample implementation returns a placeholder containing the goal and all available actions. That is only a structural foundation, not a working plan generator. A production generator must construct coherent action sequences, dependencies and branches rather than treating every available action as one plan.

8. Step six — Evaluate plans across separate dimensions
Create evaluator.py, scorer.py and selector.py.

Your roadmap says plan evaluation should consider success likelihood, risk, cost, latency, user effort, reversibility, evidence quality and constraint compliance. It also cautions against collapsing every dimension into one arbitrary permanent score. 
*voice TTS.txt

An evaluation result should make trade-offs inspectable. For example:

Dimension

Plan A

Plan B

Success likelihood

Unknown until evidence is gathered

Unknown until evidence is gathered

Operational risk

To be assessed

To be assessed

Cost

Actual or estimated with provenance

Actual or estimated with provenance

Reversibility

Depends on the proposed changes

Depends on the proposed changes

User effort

Estimate or unknown

Estimate or unknown

Constraint compliance

Must be validated

Must be validated

Avoid filling the table with fabricated ratings. Unknown values should remain unknown until the system has evidence.

The selector should pass these dimension-specific results to the existing Phase 42 meta-control system, which is the trade-off authority identified in your source. The planner must not independently override higher-level decision policies. 
*voice TTS.txt

9. Step seven — Validate the complete plan before committing
Create validator.py, commitment.py, confirmation.py and governance.py.

Before execution, validate the plan against all applicable requirements:

The goal is still grounded in the current user instruction.

Dependencies and preconditions are satisfied.

Material assumptions have been checked.

Required resources are available.

Every planned action is actually available.

Risk is within the permitted boundary.

Identity, security and privacy constraints are satisfied.

The required governance authorization is present.

These checks are the plan-validation stage specified in your roadmap. 
*voice TTS.txt

A plan should move through explicit states:

CANDIDATE
    ↓
VALIDATED
    ↓
COMMITTED
    ↓
EXECUTING
    ↓
VERIFIED COMPLETION
An invalid or unauthorized plan stops before commitment. A candidate plan must never accidentally become an executed action.

The planner consumes the existing governance decision. It does not define its own authority. If a step requires explicit user confirmation or human approval, pause and request it before execution.

10. Step eight — Execute with checkpoints and observations
Create executor.py, checkpoint.py and observation.py.

Long plans should not run as one uninterruptible sequence. The source defines the pattern as execute an action, observe the result, checkpoint, and decide whether to continue. 
*voice TTS.txt

# observation.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class PlanObservation:
    node_id: str
    actual_result: object
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

After each important action, Aariya should examine:

Is the original goal still valid?

Is the current plan still valid?

Did the result match the prediction?

Has the user changed the instruction?

Have resources, constraints or authorization changed?

Should the next branch execute, or should planning stop and restart?

For instance, if measuring latency shows that buffering is not the bottleneck, Aariya should not blindly continue the buffering-optimization steps. It should use the new evidence to reconsider the provider or orchestration path.

A necessary interface consistency check
Your source declares a Plan with a root_node_id, but its sample executor iterates over plan.nodes. Those interfaces need to be reconciled before implementation. The actual executor also needs branch-selection behavior; simply iterating every node would not correctly execute a conditional plan.

A sound implementation can preserve the root-node representation while providing a graph lookup and traversal interface, or define the plan to contain a complete node collection. The important point is to settle that contract explicitly rather than silently treating the sample snippets as a finished working system. 
*voice TTS.txt
*voice TTS.txt

11. Step nine — Replan when reality differs from the plan
Create progress.py, replanner.py, interruption.py and rollback.py.

The roadmap lists several reasons to replan:

New user instruction.

New evidence or unexpected results.

Action failure.

Goal or constraint changes.

Resource loss.

Provider changes.

*voice TTS.txt
A simplified loop is:

# replanner.py

from __future__ import annotations


class Replanner:

    async def replan(
        self,
        current_plan,
        observation,
        state,
    ):
        return await state.planner.replan(
            current_plan=current_plan,
            observation=observation,
            state=state,
        )

The actual replanner must use the current state, observations and remaining goal requirements to generate a new plan version.

For example:

Plan v1:
Optimize buffering
        ↓
Observation:
Buffering is not the dominant bottleneck
        ↓
Invalidate the unsupported continuation
        ↓
Plan v2:
Investigate provider latency
        ↓
Preserve the reason for the change
Do not retry the same failed plan indefinitely. A failed action should become new evidence for the next planning cycle.

If the user says “Stop, let's do something else,” the system should cancel the pending plan safely, preserve appropriate state and process the new instruction. Rollback should be attempted when a reversible change requires it and the rollback itself is safe and authorized.

12. Step ten — Build the planning controller
Create controller.py.

The controller coordinates candidate generation, evaluation, selection, validation, execution, observation and replanning. The following is an architectural scaffold:

# controller.py

from __future__ import annotations


class PlanningController:

    def __init__(
        self,
        generator,
        evaluator,
        selector,
        validator,
        executor,
        replanner,
        repository,
    ) -> None:
        self._generator = generator
        self._evaluator = evaluator
        self._selector = selector
        self._validator = validator
        self._executor = executor
        self._replanner = replanner
        self._repository = repository

    async def run(self, state):
        candidates = await self._generator.generate(
            state,
            state.action_space,
        )

        evaluated = []

        for candidate in candidates:
            evaluated.append(
                await self._evaluator.evaluate(

This remains a scaffold, not a complete executor. It assumes async streaming from the executor and certain repository/state interfaces. A production implementation must explicitly define conditional branch traversal, cancellation handling, checkpoint policy, observation persistence, idempotency and verified completion. The sample's replanning path must resume execution under the new plan rather than merely returning the revised plan.

The controller must also recheck that the goal remains current before executing each consequential action. Every replan introduces a new candidate and must pass validation and the applicable authorization controls.

13. Integrate Phase 66 with the existing Aariya architecture
Existing phase

Role in planning

Phase 29–30 — Diagnosis and research

Supplies investigated causes, external evidence and candidate approaches

Phase 42 — Meta-control

Selects among evaluated plans and manages trade-offs

Phase 46–47 — Capability evolution and architecture

Identifies capability gaps and supports candidate architecture paths

Phase 48–50 — Reliability, security and governance

Supplies runtime state and enforces safety, authorization and rollback boundaries

Phase 53–54 — Regression and benchmarking

Evaluates whether planning changes preserve correctness and improve measured outcomes

Phase 55 — Experience

Measures user effort, unnecessary steps and task friction

Phase 58 — Anticipation

Helps prepare alternative branches without executing them prematurely

Phase 59 — Grounding

Ensures the plan remains aligned with current evidence and the user's current goal

Phase 60 — Metacognition

Evaluates uncertainty and whether more research or clarification is needed

Phase 61 — Repair

Supports recovery after a failed action and informs replanning

Phase 63 — Attention

Selects the active branch and allocates resources to the relevant work

Phase 64–65 — Threads and goals

Supply structured thread context, goals and task state

Your source describes these as the bridges that turn a plan manager into an integrated planning capability rather than a separate reasoning silo. 
*voice TTS.txt

14. Definition of done
Use this as the Phase 66 acceptance gate.

Phase 66 checklist

0 / 14


Plans have stable identifiers, explicit versions and traceable change reasons.

Success criteria, hard constraints, soft preferences and assumptions are represented separately.

The action space comes from existing registered capabilities.

Actions have explicit preconditions, expected effects and dependencies.

The planner generates coherent alternatives and conditional contingencies.

Risk, cost, user effort, reversibility and evidence quality remain inspectable dimensions.

Meta-control selects plans without bypassing higher-level constraints.

Validation and required authorization happen before commitment and execution.

Execution uses checkpoints and records actual observations.

Unexpected outcomes trigger state re-evaluation and justified replanning.

User interruption or goal changes invalidate stale plans and stop unsafe continuation.

Rollback is limited to safe, supported and authorized recovery paths.

Plan versions retain provenance and previous versions are not silently overwritten.

Completion is based on the verified goal outcome, not merely successful action execution.



Phase 67 — World Model & Current Environment State
Phase 66 gave Aariya the ability to plan possible courses of action. Phase 67 gives her an evidence-backed representation of what is actually happening in the environment, what has changed, and what is currently possible.

Your *voice TTS.txt roadmap distinguishes three things:

World state: What the environment actually reports.

Belief state (Phase 59): What Aariya currently believes about the conversation and its context.

Planning (Phase 66): What Aariya should do, given the goal and available evidence.

These must remain connected but separate. A plan can rely on an outdated assumption, and a memory can describe a state that was correct previously but is no longer true. Phase 67 provides the observations, freshness checks and reconciliation needed to identify those conflicts before they undermine execution. 
*voice TTS.txt

1. The architecture
Real environment

Actual provider responses, runtime telemetry, task results and authorized system observations

Authorized observation collection

Check permissions, collect evidence and record timestamps and provenance

Freshness

Is the observation current enough?

Source authority

How trustworthy is the source for this property?

World-state reconciliation

Resolve compatible evidence; preserve unresolved contradictions

State snapshot and change detection

Compare with the previous state and identify meaningful changes

Plan remains valid

Continue under current controls

State changed

Assess impact and replan if necessary

Persist history → Observe again → Verify outcomes

This follows the world-model architecture in Phase 67: collect real observations, construct and reconcile world state, detect changes, assess their impact on plans, and update the state from subsequent observations. 
*voice TTS.txt

2. Create the world-model package
Unlike several preceding phases, Phase 67 introduces a broader package at backend/app/world/. Its scope includes entities, observations, evidence, timestamps, state changes, relationships, resource availability, environment topology and integration with the planning and governance layers. 
*voice TTS.txt

Create this structure:

backend/app/world/
├── __init__.py
├── entity.py
├── property.py
├── state.py
├── observation.py
├── source.py
├── evidence.py
├── confidence.py
├── uncertainty.py
├── timestamp.py
├── freshness.py
├── change.py
├── diff.py
├── snapshot.py
├── history.py
├── dependency.py
├── availability.py
├── capability.py
├── resource.py
├── environment.py
├── topology.py
├── relationship.py
├── event.py
├── anomaly.py
├── reconciliation.py
├── consistency.py
├── prediction.py
├── impact.py
├── planner_bridge.py
├── goal_bridge.py

├── repair_bridge.py
├── security.py
├── privacy.py

├── controller.py
└── report.py
Keep the responsibilities separate:

Component group

Responsibility

entity.py, property.py, state.py

Represent observed entities and their properties

observation.py, source.py, evidence.py

Record where information came from and what was observed

confidence.py, uncertainty.py, freshness.py, timestamp.py

Preserve uncertainty, temporal validity and source limitations

change.py, diff.py, snapshot.py, history.py

Detect and retain state transitions

dependency.py, availability.py, capability.py, resource.py, topology.py, relationship.py

Describe operational dependencies and what is available

anomaly.py, reconciliation.py, consistency.py, prediction.py, impact.py

Identify unusual conditions, reconcile evidence and assess consequences

planner_bridge.py, goal_bridge.py, other bridges

Deliver relevant state changes to the existing cognitive subsystems

security.py, privacy.py, authorization.py

Enforce observation boundaries and data-access rules

repository.py, controller.py, report.py

Orchestrate collection, persistence and diagnostics

The world model should consolidate information from actual systems. It must not fabricate connectors or claim access to a provider, service, database or device that your deployed Aariya does not actually have. 
*voice TTS.txt

3. Step one — Represent real-world entities
Create entity.py and property.py.

An entity is something Aariya can observe, such as a TTS provider, audio stream, backend service, database, task or network connection.

# backend/app/world/entity.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class WorldEntity:
    entity_id: str
    entity_type: str
    canonical_name: str

Properties capture observations about those entities:

# backend/app/world/property.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime
from typing import Any


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class WorldProperty:
    entity_id: str
    name: str
    value: Any
    observed_at: datetime
    evidence_ref: str

For example, an actual runtime integration could report a provider's observed streaming status or the measured latency of an audio stream.

These are different kinds of information: an entity identifies what is being observed, while its properties describe what a source reports about it. Your source establishes this entity/property model as a foundation for the world state. 
*voice TTS.txt

Important: the property record does not prove that the value is currently correct. The world model must evaluate provenance, freshness and uncertainty before relying on it.

4. Step two — Make observations and provenance first-class
Create observation.py, source.py and evidence.py.

Every observation needs to be traceable to its source.

# observation.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime
from typing import Any


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class WorldObservation:
    observation_id: str
    entity_id: str
    property_name: str
    value: Any
    observed_at: datetime
    source_id: str
    evidence_ref: str

Represent the source separately:

# source.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class WorldSource:
    source_id: str
    source_type: str
    authority: str | None

And the evidence record:

# evidence.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class WorldEvidence:
    evidence_id: str
    source_id: str
    observation_id: str
    collected_at: datetime
    provenance: str

The authority field is descriptive metadata, not an authorization decision. Actual permission to access a source belongs to the existing security and authorization services.

The source lists runtime instrumentation, database records, real provider responses, service health, authorized device telemetry, task state and genuine tool outputs as possible sources, depending on the deployment. 
*voice TTS.txt

For Aariya's implementation, start with the real integrations that exist in the current repository. Add new sources only after a genuine connector or instrumentation path is available.

5. Step three — Track freshness, confidence and uncertainty
Create confidence.py, uncertainty.py, timestamp.py and freshness.py.

A world observation may have been correct when it was collected but be unsuitable for a current decision.

For example, a provider was reported as healthy several minutes ago. If a plan now needs to know whether the provider is connected, that older observation might no longer be sufficient.

Your source defines the freshness categories fresh, aging, stale and unknown, and stresses that freshness requirements depend on the property being measured. 
*voice TTS.txt

A basic model:

# freshness.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime
from enum import Enum


class FreshnessClass(str, Enum):
    FRESH = "fresh"
    AGING = "aging"
    STALE = "stale"
    UNKNOWN = "unknown"


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class StateFreshness:
    observed_at: datetime
    age_seconds: float | None
    freshness_class: FreshnessClass

The classification policy must use the timestamp of the observation and a suitable rule for that property's required freshness. Do not assume one universal timeout is appropriate for provider connectivity, current CPU load and a long-lived project decision.

Keep confidence and freshness separate:

Confidence describes the strength and limitations of the evidence supporting a conclusion.

Freshness describes how temporally suitable the observation is.

Uncertainty describes what remains unresolved.

A fresh observation from an unsuitable source is not automatically trustworthy. A reliable historical observation does not prove that the same state still exists.

When a decision depends on fresh state and the available evidence is stale, Aariya should refresh the observation or preserve uncertainty instead of asserting current availability.

6. Step four — Build world-state snapshots
Create state.py and snapshot.py.

A world-state snapshot represents the environment as observed at a particular point in time.

# state.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class WorldState:
    state_id: str
    observed_at: datetime
    entities: tuple
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

Then define the snapshot:

# snapshot.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime
from typing import Any


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class WorldSnapshot:
    snapshot_id: str
    captured_at: datetime
    state: Any

Snapshots let Aariya compare the current observed state with an earlier state and answer questions such as what changed, when a service became degraded, or which provider status was reported during a failure.

A snapshot is not a complete representation of everything that exists in the world. It is a record of the state that Aariya was authorized and able to observe, with the evidence and limitations recorded. 
*voice TTS.txt

7. Step five — Detect changes and retain history
Create change.py, diff.py and history.py.

A state change should be a structured event, not just a difference in generated text.

# change.py

from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class WorldChange:
    entity_id: str
    property_name: str
    previous_value: object
    current_value: object
    observed_at: datetime
    evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

The differ compares two real snapshots:

# diff.py

from __future__ import annotations


class WorldDiffer:

    def compare(self, previous, current):
        # Implement comparison against actual snapshot records.
        # Preserve evidence and report unknown or incompatible
        # values rather than assuming they are equal.
        raise NotImplementedError

The roadmap lists meaningful transitions such as entities being created or removed, properties changing, services recovering, resources degrading or capabilities becoming unavailable. It also requires real snapshot comparison rather than placeholder output. 
*voice TTS.txt

History should preserve the observed transition. For example:

TTS provider
    ↓
Healthy
    ↓
Degraded
    ↓
Connection lost
    ↓
Recovery in progress
    ↓
Healthy again
The sequence is illustrative; each transition must be backed by the actual event or observation.

This history becomes valuable to the existing reliability and observability systems because it enables time-correlated investigation without mistaking inferred causes for established facts.

8. Step six — Model system topology, capabilities and resources
Create dependency.py, availability.py, capability.py, resource.py, environment.py, topology.py and relationship.py.

Aariya needs to understand how the systems around her depend on one another.

For example:

Voice orchestrator
        ↓
TTS adapter
        ↓
Provider connection
        ↓
Network
If the network connection fails, several dependent operations may be affected. The topology and dependency records help the planner trace which capabilities depend on the unavailable resource.

The source also explicitly distinguishes:

A capability exists.

A capability is currently available.

A capability is authorized.

These are three different dimensions. 
*voice TTS.txt

For Aariya, the world model could consume observations about available CPU, memory, network connections, provider quotas, storage and TTS capacity—but only within the deployment's authorized observation scope.

The world model should report actual available resources. It should not invent a resource measurement merely because the planner needs a number.

9. Step seven — Reconcile contradictory observations
Create reconciliation.py and consistency.py.

This is one of the most important parts of Phase 67.

Suppose the provider API reports that streaming is available while fresh internal telemetry reports that the connection is disconnected. Aariya should not randomly choose whichever result is most convenient.

Your source calls for reconciliation based on source authority, recency, consistency and evidence; unresolved contradictions should remain visible. 
*voice TTS.txt

# reconciliation.py

from __future__ import annotations


class WorldReconciler:

    def __init__(
        self,
        authority_resolver,
        freshness_policy,
        consistency_checker,
    ) -> None:
        self._authority = authority_resolver
        self._freshness = freshness_policy
        self._consistency = consistency_checker

    async def reconcile(self, observations):
        eligible = []

        for observation in observations:
            freshness = await self._freshness.evaluate(
                observation
            )

            eligible.append({
                "observation": observation,
                "freshness": freshness,
            })

        result = await self._authority.resolve(
            eligible
        )

        return await self._consistency.assess(
            observations=eligible,

This is a scaffold that assumes you will implement the three injected interfaces. The source does not provide a complete reconciliation algorithm; the actual policy must be developed against the available source types and operational requirements.

For a conflict, the resulting state should be capable of representing:

Property: provider availability

Observation A:
API reports available.

Observation B:
Connection telemetry reports disconnected.

Result:
Uncertain or degraded, pending reconciliation.
The exact conclusion depends on the sources, freshness and operational semantics. A source can be authoritative for one property but unsuitable for another.

Do not silently erase conflicting evidence just to produce a single definitive value.

10. Step eight — Separate actual state from predicted state
Create prediction.py.

Phase 67 permits predictions about likely future changes, such as whether a connection may recover. However, your roadmap explicitly states that predictions must remain predictions and must not be mistaken for observed reality. 
*voice TTS.txt

The distinction should remain explicit:

Record type

Example

Observation

A real health check returned a disconnected status

Reconciled state

The connection is currently considered unavailable, given the available evidence

Prediction

The connection may recover

Unknown

There is insufficient current evidence to determine availability

A plan may use a prediction to prepare a contingency. It must not present a predicted recovery as proof that the service is currently healthy.

This boundary also prevents synthetic predictions from contaminating the real-state history.

11. Step nine — Assess the impact of world-state changes
Create impact.py and the planning, goal and attention bridges.

A world-state change matters when it affects a current goal, task, plan, capability or risk. Your source uses provider unavailability as an example: a plan relying on that provider may become invalid, and the planner must reassess the available alternatives. 
*voice TTS.txt

The impact flow should be:

Actual state change
        ↓
Determine affected entities and dependencies
        ↓
Identify active goals and plans that rely on them
        ↓
Assess consequence and urgency
        ↓
Promote relevant attention
        ↓
Continue, block, recover or replan
This connects Phase 67 with Phase 66 planning and Phase 63 attention.

Example: TTS provider becomes unavailable
World state:
Provider A is unavailable
             ↓
Active plan:
Continue streaming through Provider A
             ↓
Impact:
Plan can no longer proceed as written
             ↓
Check actual available alternatives
             ↓
If a validated alternative exists:
Revalidate and replan
             ↓
Otherwise:
Graceful degradation and truthful status reporting
A world-state change can also affect a goal's feasibility. But the world model must not automatically replace the user's goal or change a provider without the authority required by governance and user instructions.

12. Step ten — Enforce security, privacy and authorization at collection time
Create security.py, privacy.py and authorization.py.

Your source makes a strict distinction between what Aariya can technically observe and what Aariya is allowed to access. The world model must obey the Phase 49 security and privacy boundaries. 
*voice TTS.txt

A starting authorization interface:

# authorization.py

from __future__ import annotations


class WorldAuthorization:

    async def can_observe(self, source, principal) -> bool:
        return await principal.permissions.allows(
            source
        )

This is only an interface-level sketch. The production version must use the actual authorization service, identity context, source-level permissions and audit requirements.

In particular:

Do not collect data merely because it is technically available.

Collect only the information needed for the authorized purpose.

Apply access controls and retention policies.

Recheck authorization when the scope or permission changes.

Do not let a world-model or planning operation override a security restriction.

If authorization is revoked, the planner must stop before relying on the formerly authorized action. Phase 67 reports the relevant world-state change; the existing security and governance layers determine the consequences for permitted execution.

13. Step eleven — Build the world-state controller
Create controller.py.

The controller should coordinate collection, reconciliation, snapshots, change detection, impact assessment and persistence. Your roadmap presents this exact orchestration pattern. 
*voice TTS.txt

# controller.py

from __future__ import annotations


class WorldStateController:

    def __init__(
        self,
        collector,
        reconciler,
        differ,
        impact_engine,
        repository,
        snapshot_factory,
    ) -> None:
        self._collector = collector
        self._reconciler = reconciler
        self._differ = differ
        self._impact = impact_engine
        self._repository = repository
        self._snapshot_factory = snapshot_factory

    async def update(
        self,
        context,
        previous_snapshot=None,
    ):
        observations = await self._collector.collect(
            context
        )

        state = await self._reconciler.reconcile(
            observations
        )


This is an implementation scaffold, not a complete working controller. The collector, source adapters, reconciler, differ, snapshot factory, impact engine and repository must be connected to actual runtime interfaces.

A production implementation should also handle unavailable sources, partial collection, duplicate events, timestamps that arrive out of order, persistence failures and concurrent updates. An incomplete collection must not be misrepresented as a complete and current world snapshot.

Phase 29–30 — Diagnosis and research

Investigate unexplained changes using actual evidence

Phase 46–48 — Capability evolution, architecture and reliability

Reassess available capabilities, dependencies and failures

Phase 49–50 — Security and governance

Control access to world observations and prohibit unauthorized action

Phase 51 — Digital twin and observability

Use grounded world state as the foundation for simulation and operational analysis

Turn validated real failure conditions into regression coverage and measurable evaluations

Phase 58 — Anticipation

Use world-state information to prepare possible future branches

Phase 59 — Grounding

The source explicitly distinguishes the real world, world state, digital twin and simulation in this hierarchy:

REAL OBSERVATIONS
        ↓
WORLD STATE
        ↓
DIGITAL TWIN
        ↓
SIMULATION / WHAT-IF
The digital twin may explore possible outcomes, but it cannot override actual observed reality. 
*voice TTS.txt

15. Definition of done
Use the following checklist as the Phase 67 acceptance gate.

Phase 67 checklist

0 / 14


World entities and properties have stable identifiers.

Observations record source, timestamps and provenance.

Source authorization is checked before collection.

Freshness, confidence and uncertainty are recorded separately.

Snapshots represent actual collected observations and their limitations.

Diffing compares real snapshots rather than returning placeholders.

World-state history preserves meaningful transitions.

Source reconciliation preserves unresolved contradictions.

Predictions cannot be mistaken for observed current state.

Capability existence, availability and authorization remain separate.

World changes are assessed against affected goals and plans.

Security, privacy and retention controls remain authoritative.

Partial collection and stale telemetry cannot produce false certainty.

Changes and impact decisions are traceable and inspectable.
These are acceptance criteria derived from Phase 67 of your roadmap. They do not imply that the package, provider adapters or integrations have already been implemented or tested in the repository.

1. The finalization roadmap
1. Implementation audit

Inspect the real repository and classify each subsystem by implementation status.

2. Architecture consolidation

Resolve duplicate controllers, shared state, interfaces and overlapping responsibilities.

3. One authoritative runtime

Connect perception, cognition, voice, execution and monitoring through controlled interfaces.

4. Real provider integration

Measure actual TTS behavior, streaming, interruption, cancellation and synchronization.

5. End-to-end validation

Verify correctness, safety, security, governance, recovery and real-world outcomes.

Architecture freeze → Canary → Production → Iterative improvement

These stages follow the finalization direction in your source. 
*voice TTS.txt

2. First task — Audit the actual repository
Before changing code, establish what is implemented, what is partial, what exists only as a design, and what is missing.

The roadmap is an architectural specification; it does not establish that every module exists or works in your current repository.

From your macOS terminal, navigate to your actual Aariya project root and inspect its structure:

cd /path/to/your/aariya-project

# Inspect project structure.
find backend/app -maxdepth 4 -type f | sort

# Locate runtime entry points and controllers.
rg -n "process_turn|class .*Controller|async def process" backend/app

# Locate common voice-runtime components.
rg -n "TTS|ASR|VAD|WebSocket|WebRTC|stream|interrupt|cancel" backend/app

# Check current repository changes.
git status --short

Replace the example project path with the real path. The commands assume your project has the backend/app structure described in the roadmap; adjust them if the repository differs. If rg is not installed, use grep -R -n for text searches.

Do not immediately rename or delete modules that look duplicated. First identify their callers, state ownership, side effects, tests and dependencies.

Create an implementation-status matrix
Use this table as the first audit record.

Status

Meaning

Implemented and verified

Real code exists and relevant tests or runtime evidence support it

Implemented, unverified

Code exists, but the required behavior has not been demonstrated

Partial

Only some required behavior is implemented

Designed only

Architecture or scaffold exists without operational implementation

Missing

No applicable implementation has been found

Blocked

Implementation depends on an unavailable resource, integration or decision

Audit at least these capability groups:

Area

What must be inspected

Perception

Actual ASR, VAD, speech input and event handling

Conversation

Grounding, threads, continuity, attention and turn-taking

Cognition

Memory, preferences, anticipation, metacognition and learning

Goals and planning

Goal state, dependencies, plan validation, checkpoints and replanning

Voice

Voice Director, prosody, TTS adapter, streaming, cancellation and synchronization

Autonomy controls

Initiative, recovery, permissions, confirmation and governance

Environment

Real world-state observations, freshness, reconciliation and availability

Reliability

Failure detection, telemetry, regression, benchmarking, rollback and recovery

Infrastructure

Persistence, security, observability and runtime lifecycle

The audit must record evidence for every status. A Python class existing is not proof that a capability is connected to the live runtime, and a passing isolated unit test is not proof of end-to-end behavior.

3. Second task — Consolidate the architecture
Your source proposes reducing the completed system to a smaller set of cooperating planes rather than allowing dozens of subsystems to become separate, competing engines. 
*voice TTS.txt

The intended high-level structure is:

Perception

ASR, VAD, user signals and environment input

Cognition

Grounding, memory, goals, planning, attention and learning

Voice

Strategy, prosody, TTS, streaming and synchronization

Real-time core and orchestration

One controlled interaction lifecycle with explicit subsystem interfaces

Security and governance

Reliability and observability

Real observations → Validation → Controlled learning

Resolve the overlapping subsystems
The source specifically highlights three areas where responsibilities should be consolidated:

Anticipation and prediction: Phases 13, 41 and 58 should cooperate through one coherent prediction/anticipation architecture rather than competing engines.

Memory and context: Phases 14, 57 and 64 should share a coherent memory/context design with clear ownership of historical facts, active context and thread lifecycle.

Diagnosis and recovery: Phases 29, 48 and 61 should share failure evidence and diagnostic infrastructure rather than maintaining conflicting incident histories.

*voice TTS.txt
For each overlapping group, choose one canonical owner for state and decisions. Other systems should consume its interfaces. Do not merge everything into one massive controller: consolidation means fewer authoritative implementations with clean contracts, not a monolith.

4. Third task — Establish one authoritative runtime path
The source calls for one top-level orchestration path. Individual subsystems should stay modular, but every user interaction needs a controlled path through them. 
*voice TTS.txt

The intended flow is:

USER INPUT
    ↓
PERCEPTION
    ↓
CONTEXT / THREAD
    ↓
GROUNDING
    ↓
ATTENTION
    ↓
COGNITION
    ↓
DECISION / PLAN
    ↓
RESPONSE GENERATION
    ↓
VOICE DIRECTOR
    ↓
PROSODY / PERFORMANCE
    ↓
TTS
    ↓
STREAMING
    ↓
USER
    ↓
OUTCOME / EXPERIENCE
    ↓
LEARNING
Security, safety, identity and governance controls must be able to stop inappropriate actions at the relevant boundaries. An interruption, cancellation or new user instruction must also be able to invalidate stale work.

A starter orchestration interface might look like this:

from __future__ import annotations


async def process_turn(
    input_event,
    *,
    perception,
    context_engine,
    grounding,
    attention,
    cognition,
    decision_engine,
    response_engine,
    voice_engine,
    realtime_engine,
):
    perceived = await perception.process(input_event)

    context = await context_engine.build(perceived)

    grounded = await grounding.process(
        perceived,
        context,
    )

    focus = await attention.process(
        grounded,
        context,
    )

    cognitive_state = await cognition.process(
        grounded,
        focus,
        context,
    )


This is an interface sketch, not executable integration with your existing classes yet. Your actual implementation must account for streaming input, cancellation, exceptions, tool execution, authorization and observation of the final outcome.

The key architectural requirement is one controlled execution path, not that every subsystem must run on every turn. The orchestrator should invoke the relevant capabilities based on the current interaction and decision.

5. Fourth task — Complete real TTS provider integration
For your voice-focused Aariya system, this is one of the most important practical milestones.

Your source says the abstract architecture is not complete until an actual TTS provider is integrated and its real behavior measured. 
*voice TTS.txt

Capability

What must be verified

Streaming

Audio arrives incrementally and can be consumed correctly

Speaking rate

Supported controls have measurable effects

Pitch and volume

Actual provider behavior matches the adapter's documented contract

Prosody and emphasis

Supported controls work; unsupported controls are reported honestly

Audio format

Encoding, sample rate and channel count are correctly handled

Timing and alignment

Available timestamps or alignment metadata are validated

Visemes and synchronization

Actual synchronization behavior is measured when supported

Cancellation

Cancelled output stops correctly and stale chunks are not played

Latency

First-audio and end-to-end latency are measured from real requests

Connection behavior

Disconnects, failures, timeouts and recovery are observed and tested

Do not mark a feature supported merely because the adapter exposes a field for it. A provider may not support a requested pitch control or alignment feature; the adapter should state that limitation rather than fabricate the behavior.

The voice pathway itself remains:

Meaning + context + relationship + current state
                     ↓
             Communicative intent
                     ↓
               Voice strategy
                     ↓
             Prosodic composition
                     ↓
             Pitch / rhythm / pauses
                     ↓
          Emphasis / articulation / rate
                     ↓
                  Real TTS
                     ↓
                  Streaming
                     ↓
            Face / lip synchronization
The source explicitly rejects reducing emotional behavior to simplistic rules such as “happy means higher pitch.” Voice realization should follow meaning, context, relationship, current state, certainty, attention and conversational history. 
*voice TTS.txt

6. Fifth task — Validate the complete system
Before treating the architecture as finished, demonstrate the full interaction lifecycle rather than only individual modules.

Your source defines architectural completion through a checklist spanning real speech input, context, grounding, memory, preferences, attention, anticipation, metacognition, goals, planning, repair, initiative, world state, voice control, streaming, synchronization, security, governance, observability, evaluation, regression, learning and rollback. 
*voice TTS.txt

Use this checklist to track the work:

Finalization checklist

0 / 18


Real ASR and VAD are integrated into the runtime.

Conversation context, grounding, threads and memory are operational.

Preferences, attention and anticipation are connected to current context.

Metacognition, goals, task management and planning are operational.

Repair/recovery works, including interruption handling and cancellation.

Initiative is controlled by user intent, permissions and governance.

World-state integration uses real, authorized observations.

Voice Director and prosodic composition are operational.

The actual TTS provider is integrated and measured.

Audio streaming, barge-in and cancellation work end to end.

Lip, face and audio synchronization are validated where supported.

Voice identity boundaries are enforced.

Persistence and lifecycle behavior are validated.

Security, privacy and governance checks are operational.

Observability and production-data logging are working.

Evaluation, benchmarking and regression systems are connected.

Learning uses genuine outcomes and follows approved controls.

Rollback and recovery are tested against real failure conditions.
These are acceptance criteria, not a claim that the capabilities are already operational. The checklist in your source is the starting definition of architectural completion; the actual audit must establish the status of each item from the repository and runtime evidence. 
*voice TTS.txt

7. What happens after architecture freeze?
Once the audit, consolidation and integration are complete, the source recommends this lifecycle:

1

Architecture freeze

Stabilize subsystem ownership, interfaces and state flow.

2

Integration

Connect actual implementations through the authoritative runtime.

3

Real-data validation

Verify behavior against actual observations and provider responses.

4

Canary

Test a controlled release with monitoring and rollback readiness.

5

Production

Promote only after the validation gates pass.

6

Iterative improvement

Use measured failures and outcomes to justify changes.

The source explicitly recommends continuing with implementation hardening, integration, actual-provider calibration, end-to-end testing and production validation—not adding more numbered phases. 
*voice TTS.txt
